#6953. Movado’s New Tempo-Matic Watch Is Inspired by an Iconic Piece from the ’70s

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Proof once again that it’s hip to be square.

Movado was founded in 1881 in La Chaux-de-Fonds, Switzerland, which isn’t a particularly odd backstory for a heritage Swiss watch brand. But it has been American owned since 1983 and has a distinct quality tied deeply to its long history in the U.S. market—which puts it slightly outside the predictable purview of many watch collectors.

Right now, a growing band of horological nerds is finding rich pickings in the Movado back catalog and pushing up vintage prices considerably—especially for its mid-century chronographs and its legendary Museum Watch (which celebrates 80 years in 2027; more of that to come) in the process.

It’s not only the collectors who are digging into Movado’s past, however. Last month, the brand released the Heritage Tempo-Matic ($995–$1,095), a re-creation of a watch that merges sporty and retro design in a neat and slinky package. The Tempo-Matic, Movado’s first automatic movement, was launched in 1945 and went on to power a broad range of Movado’s wristwatches. But the rounded yet boxy “TV” shape of the new Heritage Tempo-Matic is unmistakably 1970s, when watch design took a giant leap forward into sexy new territory.

The 2026 version is available in three executions, each powered by the reliable Sellita SW200-1B movement and fitted with integrated steel bracelets that positively scream seventies. Two of them come in brushed steel with vertically brushed burgundy or blue dials. A third—which, we must admit, is our favorite—is gold-tone PVD with a warm gray dial. You’d be hard-pressed to find such an elegant, authentic, and affordable homage to the 1970s—a decade that’s currently a very hot motif in watch-collecting circles—anywhere else.

For further Movado inspiration, you might enjoy a glimpse into the brand’s rich watchmaking history at the Horological Society of New York, a fascinating nonprofit organization—effectively a guild created to support immigrant German watchmakers—that since 1866 has promoted the art and science of watchmaking in America. In the guild’s expansive watchmaking library, until July 2027, a dedicated display charts the Movado brand’s achievements in watchmaking, advertising, and design from as early as the ahead-of-its-time, art-deco-style Polyplan of 1912. Visits to the Movado exhibit are free, but we advise you to call ahead to check availability. And if you really get the bug while you’re there, you might like to sign up for membership, regular lectures, and even a series of watchmaking workshops where you get hands-on watchmaking experience under expert tutorship.

#6952. Meet the history-makers of the 120th Congress

Democrat Annie Andrews, here addressing supporters at her election night party in Charleston, S.C., on Tuesday, is the first woman elected to the Senate from South Carolina. (Bill Clark/CQ Roll Call) (Bill Clark CQ Roll Call)

The incoming freshman class of the 120th Congress includes several people who scored historic wins in their states and the nation at large.

Among them were women breaking glass ceilings in the Senate and trailblazers for Native American representation. Beyond Washington, a handful of nominees pulled off milestone victories in gubernatorial elections, including four Black candidates. Congress is also poised to witness history next year with New York Democrat Hakeem Jeffries expected to become the first Black speaker.

Here are some of the candidates who made history on election night, both in Congress and in the governor's mansions:

Alfonso, the son-in-law of Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy, is the first Gen Z Republican elected to Congress with his victory in Wisconsin’s open 7th District, a seat his father-in-law once held.

Andrews, a pediatrician, becomes the first woman elected to the Senate from South Carolina after defeating appointed Republican incumbent Darline Graham, the first female senator from the Palmetto State.

With her victory in New York’s 13th District, Avila Chevalier becomes the first Muslim person elected to Congress from the Empire State. She succeeds Democratic Rep. Adriano Espaillat, whom she defeated in the primary.

Benitez-Thompson, a former majority leader of the Nevada State Assembly, is the first Democrat elected to represent the state’s 2nd District since its creation after the 1980 census.

With his victory over Republican Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith, Colom, a district attorney, will be Mississippi’s first Black senator since Reconstruction. He will also be the Magnolia State’s first Black senator elected directly by voters.

Conely, a Navy veteran, unseats Republican Rep. Mike Lawler to become the first openly gay woman elected to Congress from New York.

Dandiya, the daughter of Indian immigrants, becomes the first South Asian American elected to Congress from Florida with her victory in the redrawn 22nd District. She is also one of the first adherents of Jainism to be elected to Congress, along with Arizona's Amish Shah (see below).

With his victory over Republican Mike Rogers, El-Sayed, the son of Egyptian immigrants, becomes the first Muslim person elected to the Senate.

A member of the White Earth Band of Ojibwe, Flanagan becomes the first Native American woman elected to the Senate with her victory over Republican Michele Tafoya. The outgoing Minnesota lieutenant governor is also the first Native American to represent the state in Congress.

Ford, a state senator, unseated Republican Rep. Victoria Spartz to become the first openly gay person elected to Congress from Indiana.

Hamawy, a former Army surgeon who emigrated from Egypt to the U.S. as an infant, is the first Muslim person elected to Congress from New Jersey, with his victory in the state’s 12th District.

Killin, an Army veteran and onetime chief of staff to former second gentleman Doug Emhoff, is the first Democrat elected to represent Colorado’s 5th District since its creation after the 1970 census.

Kiros, who upset Rep. Diana DeGette in the Democratic primary for Colorado’s 1st District, easily won the general election to become the first Black woman elected to Congress from the Centennial State. Kiros, who was born in Addis Ababa, Ethiopia, and emigrated to the U.S. as an infant, is also the first Gen Z woman elected to Congress.

Koh, the son of a Korean American father and a Lebanese American mother, is the first Asian American elected to Congress from Massachusetts. The former Biden administration alumnus will succeed Democrat Seth Moulton in the state’s 6th District.

Letlow, the first Republican woman elected to the House from Louisiana, earns the same distinction in the Senate with her victory over Democrat Jamie Davis in the open-seat race to succeed GOP Sen. Bill Cassidy.

Limtiaco will be the first Chamorro woman to represent Guam in Congress after she unseated Republican Del. James C. Moylan. She previously was the first woman to serve as U.S. attorney for the Districts of Guam and the Northern Mariana Islands.

McCann, a state senator, defeated Republican Rep. Bill Huizenga to become the first Native American elected to Congress from Michigan. McCann is a member of the Citizen Potawatomi Nation.

Moss, the president pro tempore of the Michigan Senate, keeps Rep. Haley Stevens’ 11th District in Democratic hands, becoming the first openly gay person elected to Congress from Michigan.

The grandson of a Mexican immigrant, Ojeda, who defeated National Republican Congressional Committee Chairman Richard Hudson, is the first Latino elected to Congress from North Carolina.

By defeating former Sen. John E. Sununu, Pappas, a fourth-term congressman, becomes the first openly gay man elected to the Senate.

The daughter of immigrants from Chile and Cuba, Powell is the first Latino person elected to Congress from Nebraska after flipping the state’s 2nd District. She is also the first Democratic woman elected to Congress from Nebraska.

Unofficial returns show Sarauw winning the open-seat race to succeed outgoing Del. Stacey Plaskett, making her the first openly gay person elected to Congress from the U.S. Virgin Islands.

The son of Indian immigrants, Shah is the first South Asian American elected to Congress from Arizona. The former state legislator is also one of the first adherents of Jainism to be elected to Congress, along with Florida’s Pia Dandiya.

A citizen of the Ysleta del Sur Pueblo Nation, Valdez is the first Native American elected to Congress from New York. The New York State assemblywoman will succeed retiring Democratic Rep. Nydia M. Velázquez.

Wiener, a state senator who succeeds former Speaker Nancy Pelosi in California’s 11th District, is the first openly gay person elected to represent San Francisco in Congress.

With her victory over Republican Vivek Ramaswamy, Acton becomes the first woman elected governor of Ohio. Republican Nancy Hollister briefly served as the state’s top executive in the late 1990s but was never elected governor.

The son of Mexican immigrants, Becerra, a former congressman and Health and Human Services secretary, is the first Latino to be elected governor of California. He is the state’s second Latino governor after Romualdo Pacheco, who was never elected to the role but briefly succeeded an incumbent in 1875.

Blackburn, Tennessee’s first female senator, will soon become the Volunteer State’s first female governor after her victory over Democrat Jerri Green.

Crowley, the Milwaukee County executive, will be the first Black person elected governor of Wisconsin after he defeated Republican Rep. Tom Tiffany.

Republican Rep. Byron Donalds will become Florida's first Black governor after he defeated Democrat David Jolly, a former GOP congressman.

Ford, the current Nevada attorney general, will become his state’s first Black governor after defeating Republican incumbent Joe Lombardo.

Haaland, a member of the Pueblo of Laguna, will become the first Native American woman to be elected governor in the U.S. after defeating Republican Gregg Hull. The former Interior secretary was also one of the first Native American women elected to Congress, along with Kansas Rep. Sharice Davids, in 2018.

Hinojosa, a state representative, will become the first Hispanic person elected Texas governor since statehood after she unseated three-term Republican incumbent Greg Abbott.

Klobuchar, the first woman to be elected senator from Minnesota, makes history again with her election as her state’s first female governor.

With her victory over Republican businessman Rick Jackson, Lance Bottoms becomes the first woman to be elected governor of Georgia. The former Atlanta mayor is also the state’s first Black governor and the first Black woman to be elected governor anywhere in the country, along with Del. Stacey Plaskett in the U.S. Virgin Islands.

Unofficial returns show Plaskett, the nonvoting delegate from the U.S. Virgin Islands, becoming the first woman to be elected governor of the island territory. She is also one of the first Black women to be elected governor in the country, along with Lance Bottoms in Georgia.

#6942. Kylie Cantrall Wants to Be Your Modern-Day Carrie Bradshaw

After all, the rising star has a single named after the Sex and the City character.

Last night at the Moxy Downtown Los Angeles, rising pop star Kylie Cantrall teased her next project. “It’s gaggy. It’s giving 21,” she joked onstage during a small, intimate performance. “I actually haven’t written any ballads so far. I’m healed.”

Cantrall, who is perhaps best known for portraying Red, daughter of the Queen of Hearts, in the Descendants film series, has had a banner year. The L.A. native just wrapped a nationwide tour, appeared on Fox’s The Masked Singer, is set to perform at iHeart’s Jingle Ball, and dropped her EP Valley Girl Problems. A standout on the release is “Carrie Bradshaw,” which examines the similarities between Cantrall and the iconic Sex and the City character, played by Sarah Jessica Parker. “I think my fashion sense has been influenced by Carrie,” Cantrall says. “Also, she’s just kind of a mess, but she keeps it together on the outside. That’s honestly how I feel about myself. The choices are questionable, sometimes. Choices in dating, questionable.”

At the show, Cantrall played four tracks, including “Carrie Bradshaw.” She took pictures with her fans, signed vinyls, and, for one fan, even signed their chest. The experience is a part of a larger effort to use music as a connector at the Moxy, which has also hosted Ayra Starr at its London property. The brand unites artists with their fans in private settings and gives travelers the opportunity to relax with a musical backdrop. “I’m so excited to be here at Moxy,” Cantrall told ELLE before taking the stage. “I love doing these little underplays and acoustic shows, because it’s a different side of myself that I feel like people don’t usually get to see.”

Ahead of her performance, Cantrall sat down with ELLE to discuss Valley Girl Problems, her acoustic set, and new music on the horizon.

Full production, dancers, props. It’s always very razzle-dazzle, Kylie the performer, but I feel like this is more Kylie just the singer and songwriter. I like getting to showcase that aspect.

Honestly, I get nervous for it all. I think I put so much thought and care into every show, so there’s always a pressure I put on myself. But I feel like once I get out there and see the cuties’ faces, it feels chill. I know so many of them. We have more of a friendship bond.

It’s everything. What’s really special about me and my fan base is that we’ve really grown up together, because a lot of them are my age now. They had maybe started watching me from YouTube or from my first Disney show when I was 13 and have stayed along with my journey. It’s crazy. Our bond is kind of familial at this point.

I think they’re quite different. It’s so different playing a character. Of course, with someone like Red, I’m very grateful I got to play a character that I felt like had a lot of me in it already. But, Kylie the musician, Kylie the artist, that’s completely, 100 percent raw me. I have so much freedom to take risks and experiment. With Valley Girl, I feel like that perfectly encapsulated the person I was a couple months ago when I wrote it. I’ve changed so much since then. I’m just excited for the next project that’s going to reflect who I am now as a 21-year-old. I want all of my bodies of work to reflect the person I am in whatever chapter that is. It’s like a time machine. It’s very powerful in that way.

No. I listen to it so much when I’m creating it, and obviously it’s just nonstop before it comes out. The minute it comes out, it feels like I have no control over it whatsoever. It’s not mine. It’s for everyone else. I have to have that kind of separation. If I am still listening to it after it’s been out, I’ll start to pick things apart and be like, “Oh, I wish I did that. Oh, I could have done this differently.” Then, you have people’s criticism or opinions also in your brain when you’re listening to it, which can taint the way you view it. I was so proud when I finished it. I knew how I felt once I submitted it to the label and was like, “Okay, this is the final product. Here you go.” I just want to remain in that place.

It’s always the vocal fry—holding the ends of my words for a long time. Definitely the way I talk. I guess hiking and matcha are also kind of a worldwide thing at this point, but for some reason growing up I always felt like that’s such a Valley thing. In the Valley, it’s either matcha cafes or Pilates studios and fruit carts on the corner, and that kind of encapsulates the Valley for me.

So excited. At the top of the year, I wrote in my little manifestation journal, I’m going to perform at Jingle Ball this holiday. It was before it was even a topic of conversation. I just threw it out there because I was like, I really want to do Jingle Ball just to be a part of a lineup. I’ve watched every single Jingle Ball since I was seven years old. The fact that I’ll get to share the stage with Katseye, Olivia [Rodrigo], the Jonas Brothers, Adéla, these are all artists that I look up to. I’m just excited to be in that orbit.

I was probably enjoying my days off for the first two days when I got back home from tour. Then, the third day I was like, “Okay, I’m ready to get back out.” I’m already thinking in my head what the new set list is going to be with the new songs that I’m recording at home right now and getting ready for the next album. My wheels are turning.

New York had a crazy energy. The New Yorkers know how to pull up and show some love. And, of course, L.A. I got to end the tour here, so there was something that felt very full circle about getting to come home and celebrate with my friends and family that all came out to the show, and a lot of my L.A. fans who are here tonight at the Moxy. It’s also just so fun anytime I get to do anything in L.A. It’s all my closest fans, and all my first pop-ups I had ever done were always here in L.A. A lot of my foundational fans that have been with me, they’re all here.

I’m just taking a lot of inspiration from my life right now, as a 21-year-old who’s going out and just kind of embracing this next chapter. It feels like I have all the freedom in the world. I’m just excited to kind of show people maybe a little more of a wilder side. A little more messy, a little less curated. Kylie, outside of the work I’ve done or the image, the narrative, whatever that may be. Here’s who I am right now. I like to have fun. I like to go out. I’m going through it all the time, but I get to pour it into my music, whether it’s just discovering my kind of identity in this era, dating, or all the things I’m learning about myself. It’s all going to be poured into this record.

I’m a Cancer. I’m very in touch with my emotions at all times, and music is my chance to fully just, without censoring anything, explore that.

This interview has been edited and condensed for clarity.

#6933. Why Travis Kelce Didn’t Join Taylor Swift at the 2026 CMA Awards

Taylor Swift returned to the CMA Awards for the first time in 10 years this evening, but her husband, Travis Kelce, wasn’t seen there with her. Though the singer posed on the red carpet solo, she wasn’t alone during the ceremony—she was spotted beside her friend Brittany Mahomes, a fellow Kansas City Chiefs WAG. (She is married to Kelce’s teammate Patrick Mahomes, and they’re expecting their fourth child together.)

Kelce is in the middle of his football season. His next game is at Arrowhead Stadium on Sunday, against the Arizona Cardinals, which could explain his absence this evening.

This past weekend, Kelce left the field for part of the team’s game against the Atlanta Falcons due to an ankle injury. Postgame, he gave USA Today an update. “I’m all good,” he said. “I probably could have went more than I did when I came back, but it was the direction the team wanted to go, so I was right there with them. We had a lot of young guys step up, so it was really nice.”

While he and Swift are supportive of each other’s careers, the pair hasn’t made their red carpet debut at an awards ceremony yet. Last March, they attended the iHeartRadio Music Awards together but skipped the red carpet. Kelce remained by Swift’s side throughout the show.

In an October 2025 episode of their podcast New Heights, Kelce’s brother Jason asked him about being Swift’s creative muse for The Life of a Showgirl. The tight end coyly replied, “I’m just the lucky man that gets to be, you know, the support system for Taylor while she drops one of the coolest albums that I’ve ever listened to, that the world’s seen. I’m excited for her. I’m happy for her. Everything seems to have gone perfectly, all this planning.”

He detailed what it was like watching her make the album, revealing that he got to listen to it before it was released. “You see it from start to finish,” he said. “She started this thing in the middle of her [Eras] Tour in Europe. To see it all unfold and come together and her connect the dots—from the strategic planning to how the album was all going to look—then to listen to the music, and everything comes together and her finally getting that 12th song and being like ‘I think it’s done.’

“From that point on, it’s like her and her label are just working their ass off to make this one of the biggest releases of all time,” he added. “I’m just happy as hell for it because she’s happy with it. She’s proud of this and obviously I’m proud of her for going through the hoops to live out her dreams.”

#6923. The Art of Breakfast: What American Hotels Can Learn From Europe

From Gstaad to Lake Como, the best hotels treat the morning meal as the main event.

There are few things more revealing about a hotel than its breakfast. A property can have beautiful rooms and a marble-clad lobby, but the morning meal is where hospitality is really put to the test. And while American hotels have become very good at building elaborate breakfast buffets, there is often something missing: the sense that anyone has given the experience that much thought. Too often, guests are greeted with scrambled eggs under a heat lamp, a few slices of wet bacon, and a $38 bill. In Europe, meanwhile, breakfast can involve silver cake stands, homemade jams, excellent coffee, and a real sense of place.

At Gstaad Palace, the grand Swiss hotel, breakfast is, like everything else, a dignified affair. The buffet is beautiful but not extravagant, and the selection spans house-made juices, jams, and pastries, eggs cooked to order, and service that runs more smoothly than a Patek Philippe. And yet, despite the formality of the space, the experience feels wonderfully relaxed.

“Guests should feel like they are in their castle or palace, and they should be welcomed like it’s their house and it’s their best friends serving them,” said Andrea Scherz, general manager, whose family has owned the hotel since 1947. “It shouldn’t be a procedure.”

Scherz recently had breakfast at an American hotel that left him less than impressed. The food wasn’t necessarily the problem—it was the little things that were missing, the gestures that make a guest feel looked after rather than processed. A hostess took his name, checked it off a list, and asked him to wait while she set a particular table, despite there being an abundance of available tables already set. It was nearly half an hour before he was sipping his humble cappuccino. “They didn’t go the extra mile; they didn’t even go the extra meter,” he recalled.

That gets to the heart of what American hotels often get wrong about the most important meal of the day: they confuse abundance with luxury. More eggs, more bacon, more potatoes—but bigger isn’t always better. “Great breakfast is often about something less complicated,” said Nick Hatsatouris, owner at Caffé Tusk, a European-style café inside the Evelyn Hotel in New York City. “A great breakfast is a simple menu with quality ingredients, the ritual of the service, and the atmosphere of the room.”

There’s also the question of price. In the United States, breakfast is frequently an additional à la carte charge, even at hotels where the room rate can feel steep. At most European hotels, the charge is folded into the room rate in advance; for guests booking through a travel advisor, it is often waived.

“For American hotels, I think the lesson is about more than including breakfast in the rate,” said Amy Brandwein, the James Beard Award-nominated chef-owner of Washington, D.C.’s Centrolina. “It’s about recognizing breakfast as an essential part of hospitality and an opportunity to express a hotel’s identity, its connection to local producers, and its commitment to the guest’s well-being.”

Beyond price, the best hotel breakfasts in Europe also reflect where guests are. At Gleneagles in Scotland, for instance, the Scullery puts a distinctly Scottish spin on the morning buffet with local charcuterie, house-made sausages and haggis, and freshly prepared eggs and pancakes. There’s a Bloody Mary station, too, as well as undeniably cozy and humble Scottish porridge. Adare Manor in Ireland serves its breakfast of Irish breads, cheeses, and hot dishes in a 132-foot-long gallery. And at Passalacqua on Lake Como, guests can take breakfast in the garden or beneath frescoed ceilings, with fresh fruit grown in the property’s gardens, eggs from the property’s own chickens, and 11 varieties of regional cakes arranged on silver tiered stands.

Of course, not every hotel needs a sprawling buffet or a kitchen turning out elaborate dishes. Smaller, more thoughtful changes can go a long way.

Brandwein remembers European breakfasts for their balance: beautiful cappuccinos, whole-grain breads, oats, flaxseeds, fresh fruit, and excellent local honey. “I find the typical American hotel breakfast rather heavy and unimaginative,” she said, pointing to the usual emphasis on eggs, potatoes, bacon, and sausage. She would like to see more whole grains, fresh produce, and lighter preparations. The ingredients don’t need to be complicated; in fact, the simpler they are, the better. Quality and presentation go a long way.

A hotel breakfast doesn’t need 40 items on a buffet or a pastry chef working through the night. It needs good coffee, something fresh and local, and a staff who seem happy to have you there.

#6914. NRCC Chairman Richard Hudson loses reelection in North Carolina

NRCC Chairman Richard Hudson, right, here at a hearing in June, lost his reelection bid in North Carolina's 9th District. (Tom Williams/CQ Roll Call)

North Carolina Rep. Richard Hudson, the chairman of the National Republican Congressional Committee, has lost his race for reelection, a stinging defeat for the man charged with preserving the House majority for the GOP.

Hudson fell to Democrat Richard Ojeda, a former West Virginia state senator who was leading  51 percent to 49 percent Thursday afternoon  when The Associated Press called the race in North Carolina’s 9th District.

Hudson had already conceded the race Wednesday, when it became clear that his path to victory was closing off.

“[The] voters of North Carolina’s 9th District made their choice and I respect it,” he said in a statement. “Since 2013 I’ve worked for North Carolina families. That includes the soldiers and families at Fort Bragg, who sacrifice more for this country than most people will ever know.”

Hudson is now the second party campaign chief to lose his seat during a midterm election this decade. In 2022, New York Rep. Sean Patrick Maloney lost reelection while serving as chair of the Democratic Congressional Campaign Committee.

Republicans across North Carolina experienced devastating losses on election night. As of Thursday afternoon, Democrats had also flipped the seats of Reps. Addison McDowell, Brad Knott and Tim Moore, as well as the open seat of retiring Rep. Chuck Edwards. The races for five other GOP-held seats remain uncalled, while Democratic Rep. Don Davis was reelected despite being targeted for defeat under a new GOP-drawn congressional map. Republicans also lost control of the North Carolina House and lost seats in the state Senate.

North Carolina’s 9th District, which includes the Eastern Piedmont region and the Fayetteville area, was far from the House battlefield for much of the midterm cycle. President Donald Trump carried the seat by 16 points in 2024, according to calculations by Inside Elections with Nathan L. Gonzales, and Hudson won a seventh term the same year by nearly 19 points. Hudson also held a financial advantage over Ojeda, raising $4.2 million to Ojeda’s $2.9 million through mid-October.

But in the waning weeks of the campaign, signs of Republican concern grew and Hudson’s campaign began spending on ads.

Ojeda was first elected to the West Virginia Senate in 2016. He ran for the U.S. House two years later as a Trump-voting Democrat but lost that race to Republican Carol Miller. He then briefly ran for the 2020 Democratic presidential nomination and also challenged GOP Sen. Shelley Moore Capito later that cycle but lost in the Democratic primary.

Ojeda moved to North Carolina in 2020, he told CityView, a local magazine, and he announced his challenge to Hudson last year.

Throughout the campaign, he attacked the incumbent over his support for Trump’s agenda, particularly the war in Iran. Ojeda is a retired Army major and the district is home to Fort Bragg, the nation’s largest Army base by population, where the Democrat was previously stationed for more than a decade.

“We did the impossible,’’ Ojeda told supporters Tuesday night. “We took on the chair of the National Republican Congressional Committee, a man with political connections, the billionaire donors, and the entire Washington establishment standing beside him.”

Hudson, 55, is finishing his seventh term in the House and his second term as NRCC chair. He had no primary opponent this year and had grown used to winning reelection by comfortable margins.

As a legislator, Hudson focused much of his policy agenda on health issues such as federal pandemic preparedness programs. He serves on the House Energy and Commerce Committee and chairs the panel's Communications and Technology Subcommittee.

#6896. A West Virginia County Could Soon Become a Nuclear-Waste Dump Against Its Will

The state’s energy office decided not to consult with residents, which is just stellar governing all around.

(Permanent Musical Accompaniment to This Post)

Being our semi-regular weekly survey of what’s going down in the several states where, as we know, the real work of governmentin’ gets done and where the rifleman’s stalking the sick and the lame.

Our semi-regular weekly survey this week is very heavy on people who want to build things of which many other people want no part.

We begin in the Commonwealth. (God save it!) South from Worcester on Route 146 lies the charming town of Sutton. Back in the day, Sutton’s Pleasant Valley Country Club was a regular stop on the PGA and LPGA Tours. (The last PGA event at the course was in 1998.) There also was a drive-in that showed films of ... ahem ... a sophisticated nature. Anyway, I used to drive through Sutton on my way to my job in the state forest. Route 146 got progressively more rural the further you got from Worcester. Now, Bob Kraft, the owner of the New England Patriots, would like to drop a data center on the town. From CBS News:

The idea of dropping a power-draining, water-gulping ten-building campus on a place as small as Sutton is exactly why data centers have become the hot-button local issue all over the country. It is sheer oligarchical bullying over which the country has raised its hackles. This would be the fourth-largest data center in the United States. And, as WGBH points out, some sports oligarchs are leading the parade on these projects, which is reason enough to give every one of these projects a serious side-eye.

It’s good to be rich in this country. You may not have noticed.

Elsewhere, Erik Loomis of LG&M points us to another oligarch’s dreamscape, this one in the beleaguered state of West Virginia. From The Washington Post:

Pause for a moment and roll that business-school candy corn around in your mouth for a second. A Nuclear Lifecycle Innovation Campus. A glow-in-the-dark euphemism for the good people of Jefferson County.

We’re back in the three-card-monte game of “Jerbs!” that we all had to play in relation to our old friend the Keystone XL pipeline. We have to be careful how many of these jobs are temporary construction jobs and how many are the strippers who come to town to entertain them.

(Jesus, Louisiana? Really? Are you prepared to move aside one of your eleventy-million chemical plants and refineries to make room for nuclear-waste disposal facilities? Take a knee on this one, okay?)

You mean communities are anxious about becoming repositories for poisonous waste with a half-life of up to 24,000 years? What is wrong with people anyway?

Energetic local community activism on issues like these was one of the most underrated sources of energy behind the historic Democratic sweep two weeks ago. People are starting to enjoy fighting the power again.

In that spirit, we conclude, as is our custom, in the great state of Oklahoma, whence Blog Official Overseas Operative Friedman of the Algarve brings us yet another story about toxic waste. From Tulsa Flyer:

Delightful. And who was it that tried to kill Bell Cow Lake?

It is going to take decades to get clear of all the damage these clowns have done, if we even can, which I doubt more seriously every day.

This is your democracy, America. Cherish it.

#6893. At the Races: After the deluge

Tuesday’s rout by Democrats has led one group dedicated to electing more Republican women to Congress to warn of a “crisis-level decline” of female GOP members on Capitol Hill.

Danielle Barrow, the president of Women for Women Action Fund, drew parallels between this week’s results and the 2018 blue wave.

“After 2018, when Republicans lost 40 seats and elected only one new Republican woman, the party made a real commitment to recruiting and investing in women – and it worked,” Barrow said in a statement Wednesday, adding that women accounted for 11 of the 15 House seats the GOP picked up in the 2020 election. But she cautioned that the party needs to sustain that focus across election cycles.

“Republicans cannot only make this investment after a wipeout election and abandon it when things improve,” she said. “It is time for party leaders to treat this as a permanent strategy, not a crisis response.”

The ranks of House Republican women won’t plummet to as low as after the 2018 elections, when the caucus had just 13 female members. At least 18 Republican women have won House races so far, according to The Associated Press, and two others are leading as of Thursday afternoon. The conference is likely to see two new female members: Florida’s Sydney Gruters, the wife of RNC Chairman Joe Gruters, and Washington state’s Amanda McKinney, who holds a comfortable lead as votes continue to be counted.

Still, those figures are worlds apart from House Democrats, whose caucus will have more than 100 women next year.

“We have always known that when women run, women win,” Florida Rep. Lois Frankel, chair of the member-led Elect Democratic Women PAC, said in a statement Wednesday.

Those incoming new members span the ideological range of the party, including progressives from New York and California who scored big primary wins this summer, to more moderate Democrats who flipped seats in Virginia and Pennsylvania.

House Democrats had set a public goal this cycle to push their female membership past 100. Former Speaker Nancy Pelosi, who is retiring at the end of the year, said electing Democratic women had been her “passion” since she joined the House in 1987.

“We made a decision to make more room for women at the tables of power – and generations of candidates, organizers and supporters have carried that work forward,” she said.

Blue breakthrough: Democrats flipped the House and Senate this week, giving the party one of its biggest nights in decades. On the Senate side, they wrested seats from Republicans in Texas, South Carolina, Kansas, Iowa, North Carolina and Ohio, while finally flipping Maine. Meanwhile, on the House side, Democrats were on track to not just win every race rated a Toss-up by Inside Elections but also pick up seats in deep-red territory that weren’t considered competitive until late in the cycle. Mary Ellen and Daniela summed up their initial election night takeaways, including how Democratic moderates and progressives both cheered the results and why many of Trump’s picks flopped at the ballot box.

Not going anywhere: The top Senate Democrat, Charles E. Schumer of New York, is confident he will be elected majority leader by his caucus, especially after the party’s resounding midterm wins. Several of his incoming members have promised to support others, our colleague Savannah Behrmann reports, but the reality is there’s no real plausible alternative. Schumer’s own seat is up in 2028.

Redistricting rout: Republicans’ redistricting gambit hasn’t paid off as they’d hoped, the latest election results show, with the GOP appearing poised to flip only a handful of redrawn seats. Democratic incumbents who sought reelection from their reconfigured seats appeared poised to all win, although the race for Rep. Darren Soto’s Central Florida seat remained uncalled as of Thursday afternoon.

Taking the chair: Among the bruising defeats that North Carolina Republicans suffered this week was National Republican Congressional Committee Chairman Richard Hudson’s loss to Democrat Richard Ojeda, a former West Virginia state senator. Hudson is now the second party campaign chief to lose reelection this decade after New York’s Sean Patrick Maloney , who lost in 2022 while serving as the DCCC chair.

New senators: The incoming Senate Democratic class includes a returning senator, a former governor, two lieutenant governors and a congressman, a notable amount of governing experience. Meanwhile, five Republican House members are moving across the Rotunda after winning open Senate seats.

Member-to-governor pipeline grows: Five newly elected governors will be departing Capitol Hill to become the top executive of their state or territory, Savannah reports. Additionally, two former members who also served in the Biden administration were elected governor, though a trio of House Republicans fell short in their gubernatorial bids.

Comeback season: Roll Call’s Nick Eskow reports on the former members who are poised to return to the House or Senate next year, with at least seven winning comeback bids Tuesday.

The firsts: Several candidates made history this week with their elections to Congress or the governorships of their state. Roll Call’s Andrew Menezes rounds up the milestone wins.

D.C. in the House: Robert White, D.C.’s newly elected nonvoting delegate, told our colleague Nina Heller that he plans to lobby fellow Democrats next year to support D.C. statehood. A potential selling point: The party could tackle affordability concerns better with two Democratic senators from the deep-blue District, he said.

Keeping count: As of 4:30 p.m. Thursday, Democrats had flipped 42 seats in the House and seven in the Senate, according to the AP. Republicans have so far picked up two House seats, both through redistricting. Twenty-eight House contests and two Senate races (Alaska and Mississippi) remain uncalled as of publication time. Check out Roll Call’s results tracker for updates as the races are called.

Indie losses: Independent Senate hopefuls running in deep-red states did not find success on Tuesday, even in races without a Democrat on the ballot. In Nebraska, former union leader Dan Osborn came up short in his second Senate bid. In Idaho, former state legislator Todd Achilles failed to dislodge Sen. Jim Risch, while Navy veteran Brian Bengs couldn’t topple South Dakota Sen. Mike Rounds. In Montana, where the Democratic nominee did not drop out, former university president Seth Bodnar lost to Republican Kurt Alme. On the House side, Alaska GOP Rep. Nick Begich comfortably leads commercial fisherman Bill Hill, though below the 50 percent threshold needed to avoid a ranked-choice runoff. With votes still being counted, the AP had yet to call the race at publication time.

Not just Congress: The blue wave that broke Republican majorities on Capitol Hill also washed over state legislatures. Democrats are poised to hold new majorities in 12 legislative chambers, including the North Carolina House and Texas House, according to the Democratic Legislative Campaign Committee, although some results aren’t official yet. The party also won governing trifectas in Michigan, Minnesota, Nevada, Pennsylvania and Wisconsin. Meanwhile, in blue states, Democrats tightened their grip on government: In Rhode Island, Republicans are on track to hold just 10 seats in the entire 113-member legislature, while next door in Connecticut, the 36-seat Senate could have as few as three GOP members.

Blame game: As Republicans take stock of what went wrong for them in the midterms, Vice President JD Vance was quick to point to the Iran conflict as an insurmountable albatross. “The reason we lost is the war,” Vance said in a lengthy X post. “If you'd like to argue it was worth it, that losing the Senate for the next 6 years was worth it, then do so. … But everyone knows why we lost.”

But the tariffs: A pair of Republican former senators, Arizona’s Jeff Flake and Pennsylvania’s Patrick J. Toomey, both also urged their party to focus on the impact of Trump’s tariff policies. “Arbitrary and capricious tariffs directly raised costs for all American consumers,” Toomey wrote Wednesday on X. “Many voted for Democrats yesterday.”

Staying put: Pennsylvania Rep. Brendan F. Boyle says he will remain in the House, where he’s poised to become the next Budget Committee chair, rather than launch a Democratic primary challenge to Sen. John Fetterman next cycle.

Bayh Bayh Bayh: A third generation of Indiana’s famed Bayh family won political office this week, with Beau Bayh’s election as secretary of state. Bayh, a Marine Corps veteran, follows in the footsteps of his father, Evan Bayh, who won the same post 40 years ago before going on to serve as governor and senator, and his late grandfather, Birch Bayh, who won three terms in the Senate. Indiana Democrats had a breakthrough election this week, defeating GOP Rep. Victoria Spartz, winning a second statewide office, unseating the speaker and breaking the GOP supermajority in the state House.

A win and a loss: Nebraska Republican Sen. Pete Ricketts won reelection, but his wife, Democrat Susanne Shore, lost a special election for a seat on University of Nebraska Board of Regents, which governs the university system. Shore, who lost to a fellow Democrat in the officially nonpartisan race, was making her first bid for elective office.

Bans on the ballot: Voters in Arizona, Nebraska and Washington state have approved new measures barring transgender athletes from participating in girls sports. A similar proposal in Colorado also appears likely to pass, although the AP had yet to call it as of publication time.

Roll Call elections analyst Nathan L. Gonzales of Inside Elections shares 15 initial lessons from the midterm elections, from the president’s “political mortality” to candidate quality and the power of personal brands. And, Nathan writes, as bad a night as it was for Republicans, it could have been even worse.

As Maine goes: The New York Times caught up with with Republican Sen. Susan Collins, who lost her bid for a sixth term in Maine. Collins, who had survived tough cycles before, spoke of getting “caught up in the Trump tsunami” and how she finally had her “Margaret Chase Smith moment.”

“These are extinction-level numbers”: Texas Monthly sat down for an interview with former state House Speaker Dade Phelan, who anticipated a down-ballot GOP rout but not the full extent of it. “MAGA needs to be dead,’’ said the Republican, who led the push to impeach state Attorney General Ken Paxton in 2023 and then was targeted for defeat by Trump. He opted against reelection this year.

Corruption a top issue: The Associated Press reported that its 2026 voter poll found corruption at the top of the list of concerns for voters who helped sweep Democrats into power on Capitol Hill.

Tar Heeled: Among the states where Republicans suffered their biggest defeats Tuesday, perhaps none was more stunning than North Carolina, where they've so far lost five U.S. House seats and are trailing in one another and lost control of the state House. The (Raleigh) News & Observer looks at how maps “designed to deliver strong Republican majorities [failed] so spectacularly at that goal.”

Support and discord: Michigan Sen.-elect Abdul El-Sayed’s sharply critical views toward Israel roiled the Jewish community in Michigan. But exit polling suggests most Jewish voters backed him, albeit in smaller numbers than they might have had a different Democrat run, Religion News Service reports.

That's how many House districts won by Trump in 2024 have been called for Democratic candidates by the AP as of 4:30 p.m. Eastern time on Thursday.

Twenty-eight additional Trump-won districts remain uncalled at publication time, and Democrats are currently leading in eight of those races.

The figures stand in sharp contrast to the start of the cycle, when there were just 13 House Democrats in districts that backed Trump in 2024.

Since then, several Republican-led states redrew their congressional maps in an effort to increase the number of Trump-won districts and improve their party’s chances of holding the House.

In large part, this effort appears to have backfired in 2026, with the Democratic wave cresting high enough to wash over dozens of Trump-won districts. Twenty-one of the 52 Democratic wins so far have come in states that redrew their congressional lines: Texas (six Trump-won seats), North Carolina (six), Ohio (five), Florida (three) and Alabama (one).

Congressional Republicans are holding leadership elections next week as they transition from the majority to the minority party. Speaker Mike Johnson is not seeking his conference’s top post, setting up a clash to be the next House minority leader. On the Senate side, South Dakota’s John Thune hopes to continue leading his party in the chamber.

#6891. Of Course Trump’s Judicial Nominees Are a Bunch of Election-Denying Yahoos

At a Senate Judiciary Committee hearing on Wednesday, seven fools who could soon have federal seats refused to admit that Joe Biden won in 2020. Yikes.

The Republicans in Congress are scrambling to fill as many government positions with unqualified replicants as they can find before the clock runs out on their majorities. The giddyap on these nominations has impressed Democrats as being unseemly. From Nomination Notes on Substack:

This has resulted in some hilarious testimonial follies and at least one near hooley between the two senators from Texas. On Tuesday, it was a panel full of judicial nominees, and there were some real lulus in there. The Democrats on the committee decided to test the nominees’ independence. Who, they asked, won the 2020 election?

This is just equal parts silly and insulting by now. Just say it, people. Joe Biden won the 2020 election.

The real dustup came over the nomination of one T. Elliot Gaiser, the head of the DOJ’s Office of Legal Counsel, to a seat on a federal district court in Texas. Outgoing senator John Cornyn refused to return the “blue slip” on Gaiser’s nomination, at least in part for the reason that Gaiser is from Ohio, which makes sense to me.

Cornyn lost the Republican primary for reelection to the inexcusable Ken Paxton, who had the support of the president and the president’s poodle, Senator Ted Cruz, Cornyn’s colleague from Texas. So I feel pretty safe in speculating that they’re not prom dates or anything. Cornyn blue-slipped Gaiser. Then, on Wednesday, this happened. From The Washington Sun:

Turns out that there are reasons enough to be dubious about putting Gaiser in a lifetime seat on any court. From Just Security:

I will grant you that the draining of Congress’s war powers to the executive branch is a horse that left the barn decades ago; in fact, on Thursday, the Senate killed a war-powers resolution as regards the Iran war. But sooner or later there has to be a test case, and it might as well be Gaiser.

And, yes, Gaiser was yet another person who could not say who won the 2020 election.

Come Thursday, a panel of nominees for an assortment of administration jobs met the Senate Homeland Security Governmental Affairs Committee. Senator Richard Blumenthal once again climbed on the merry-go-round. He went up and down the panel asking who won the popular vote in 2020, and he once again was handed the rote bafflegab. Blumenthal was particularly irked by Michael LoSapio, a former Palantir (!) executive who been picked to head the Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency. Blumenthal changed things up a bit and asked LoSapio if there had been an “attack” on the Capitol on January 6, 2021. LoSapio’s response smacked gobs all over Capitol Hill.

The next two months are going to be such fun.

#6885. One Martini to Rule Them All

The case for ordering a vodka martini, always.

At a dinner a few years ago, my fiancé’s mother, an elegant former Wall Street banker, ordered a vodka martini, dry, with three olives. I grew up in New Orleans, where I was weaned on sickeningly sweet, sticky cocktails, but I was always fascinated by the martini’s sophistication. I’d seen the drink in the hands of characters like Roger Sterling from Mad Men, who would expertly balance his V-shaped glass as he dropped a cynical witticism. So that night, I told the waiter to make it two. Ever since then, I have favored vodka martinis over gin, the base spirit for the by-the-books recipe.

I’m not the only one. Bartenders in New York have started to notice a trend away from gin. Chris Amirault of Bar Chimera in Manhattan says that he makes four vodka martinis for every one gin martini. “Vodka pays the bills, and it’s what a lot of people prefer,” says Brian Evans of the Lobby Bar at the Hotel Chelsea, also in Manhattan.

For those that insist that a true martini must only be made with gin, Tristan Stephenson, host of The Curious Bartender podcast, says, “History isn’t on the side of purists. The martini has been a moving target since the 1880s. It started sweeter, with maraschino, orange curaçao, and sugar syrup making appearances. Then it dried out over decades, then had the vermouth all but exorcised from it. Then came olive brine and everything since.”

Why do many martini drinkers prefer vodka? Ti Adelaide Martin, coproprietor of New Orleans staple Commander’s Palace, succinctly explains one reason: “Vodka is the more approachable spirit.” Unlike gin, whose botanicals impart noticeable flavors to a martini, vodka doesn’t overpower other ingredients. It’s more of a blank canvas, which is why it’s used for variations like the espresso martini, which is now the fifth-most-ordered drink at bars, according to Drinks International. Instead of strong flavor, vodka provides “texture, weight, and a kind of icy austerity that can be genuinely beautiful when the vodka is good and the drink is properly cold,” says Stephenson.

And, let’s be honest, we also drink vodka martinis for the booze. “Vodka tastes bracing,” says Esquire editor-in-chief Michael Sebastian. “I want to take a sip of my martini and get the feeling that it’s strong and it's cold, and it goes through your whole body and you feel better and loose immediately.”

Now I order my vodka martini with a splash of vermouth, just dirty enough to make me thirsty, and shaken so that thin ice shards float on top. You might prefer yours vermouth forward, citrus focused, or dirty as swamp water. It’s all fair game. And that’s the beauty of a vodka martini: It’s a personal drink. My future mother-in-law has her order, and I have mine. What’s yours going to be?

#6877. As Democrats look to next era, the veto and the filibuster await

Senate Minority Leader Charles E. Schumer greets House Minority Whip Katherine Clark at the Center for American Progress Action Fund’s “Results to Roadmap” event on Thursday. (Tom Williams/CQ Roll Call)

As Democrats continue their victory lap after their big wins on election night, outside groups and incoming freshmen are already turning up the pressure — and laying out competing visions of what the next two years should look like.

For Senate Democratic leader Charles E. Schumer, it bears repeating that “the Democrats aren’t all exactly the same.”

“The most important lesson of this whole election, and one we all need to internalize if we’re going to truly build a durable majority, is populism and moderation are not mutually exclusive,” he said at a Center for American Progress Action Fund event on Thursday.

Voters are expecting results as Democrats claim a mandate to address costs and corruption. “Our majority will be measured against one test and one test only: Have we made your life better?” Schumer said.

He acknowledged, however, that their plans could run into a President Donald Trump-sized wall: the presidential veto.

“Will everything pass? Will Trump sign all of it into law? Knowing Trump’s and Republicans’  intransigence, of course not. But I will promise the American people: The Democratic Party will fight to make your life more affordable, your government more competent, your capital less corrupt, bill by bill, brick by brick, for as long as it takes for you to feel the difference,” Schumer said, hinting the road could be a long one.

But several progressive lawmakers and groups say the next Congress cannot be business as usual.

Indivisible, a progressive political organization that has described the Trump administration as a “fascistic clown show,” sent a memo to Senate Democratic offices Thursday morning urging them not to “squander” their momentum and to “fight at a level that matches the public’s demand for action.”

“Who leads the party in this era must be an open question, not a coronation. It’s time to define what the next era of the Democratic Party will be,” the memo reads.

The group has been encouraging voters to speak up and demand their senators hold “a contested leadership election, not a cleared field for Schumer.”

Meanwhile, Schumer has projected confidence he will keep his spot atop the caucus, especially after he helped pave the way for Tuesday’s blue wave. “I’ll be reelected as majority leader,” he said Wednesday. But the pressure campaign points to some tough math and harsh realities for the top Democrat — including some that mirror the woes of the outgoing Republican Majority Leader John Thune.

Among the demands outlined by Indivisible is one with a troubled recent history: “Breaking the filibuster on the first day of the new session.”

Democrats need to leverage “every procedural tool” if they “want the public to believe their campaign promises weren’t just more talk from politicians who are just like the old politicians they hated,” the memo reads.

The group isn’t alone in floating a new approach to the chamber’s 60-vote threshold for invoking cloture and ending debate. Some current and incoming lawmakers have signaled they would be open to going “nuclear” and abolishing the legislative filibuster, following in the footsteps of past majorities in both parties who have similarly changed how other Senate business is handled, like judicial nominees.

Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., argued in an op-ed in Time this week that Democrats could in some cases use existing tools, like a party-line process known as budget reconciliation, to pass their priorities. For non-budget items, like “bills to ban stock trading by federal officials or crack down on Trump’s corruption in plain sight,” Democrats should start by putting them up for a vote on the floor and letting “Republicans decide if they want to stay tied to such corruption.”

“And, if they do, then let’s clear away Senate filibuster rules and pass the laws anyway,” she wrote.

Sen.-elect James Talarico of Texas told NBC News on Monday ahead of his victory, “I believe the majority has the right to govern in this country, and the filibuster, which is not in the Constitution and is a Jim Crow relic, is leading to gridlock in our nation’s capital.”

Schumer ignored a question about nuking the filibuster at Wednesday’s event, saying instead, “The first thing we’ll do is reduce costs and work the legislative process.”

It would take 51 votes in the Senate for Democrats to go “nuclear” and establish a new precedent, a math problem that Thune knows all too well. The Republican from South Dakota faced loud criticism from conservatives online and hard-liners in his own party who said he wasn’t doing enough to try to pass a sprawling voter ID bill known as the SAVE America Act. But Thune repeatedly insisted he didn’t have the votes to change the chamber’s filibuster rules.

Many senators fear that blowing up the 60-vote threshold would fundamentally change the role of the Senate and its ability to tap the brakes on legislation that doesn’t have durable bipartisan support. Democrats went through a similar debate over the filibuster in 2022, during Schumer's first stint as majority leader. Even as others in their party pushed to pass sweeping voting rights legislation by any means necessary, Kyrsten Sinema and Joe Manchin, then moderate Democratic senators, held firm against filibuster changes.

And Schumer will have at least one Democrat on his hands who has already spoken up in defense of the filibuster, reversing his previous position. “I now recognize how important the filibuster is and the state our country would be in without it,” Sen. John Fetterman of Pennsylvania wrote in a Washington Post op-ed earlier this summer, praising Sinema and Manchin as “courageous” holdouts who were wrongly “vilified” for holding the line.

None of that points to an easy ride for the incoming Democratic majority leader. And if fights over the filibuster weren’t enough, the caucus is also bracing for a different kind of intraparty angst, this time over impeachment.

The next likely House Judiciary Committee chairman, Rep. Jamie Raskin, D-Md., told reporters on Thursday: “I have not had a single member of Congress or even a candidate say we’ve got to start out by impeaching Donald Trump.”

“For us, impeachment is not a fetish. It’s certainly not a taboo. We’ve impeached the guy twice,” he said, referring to Democrats’ efforts during the first Trump administration. “The problem has been over on the Senate side, and for us, it is a tool in the constitutional toolkit.”

Even if the House were to impeach Trump, a conviction would require a two-thirds supermajority vote in the Senate. And some Democrats are eager not to waste their hard-earned political capital on an impeachment quest that could alienate moderate voters and do nothing to bring down costs for everyday Americans.

Illinois Gov. JB Pritzker, who boosted candidates in battleground races and recently released a roadmap for the new Congress, said at the CAP event on Thursday that lawmakers should “hold oversight hearings and expose the corruption of this administration. I’m not suggesting that that necessarily leads to impeachment. Maybe it does.”

Sounding more certain for Democrats in both chambers is their desire to impeach Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth over the war in Iran.

But that could be dealt with in the lame duck if Rep. Thomas Massie’s, R-Ky., resolution prevails next week. His proposal includes eight articles of impeachment, including allegations that Hegseth violated the 1973 War Powers Resolution in his prosecution of the Iran war and acted illegally in military campaigns in Venezuela, Yemen, the Caribbean Sea and the eastern Pacific Ocean.

Hegseth said in a video on Thursday of impeaching: “Didn’t work against President Trump. Won’t work with me. You can rest assured of that.”

Indivisible’s memo names impeaching Hegseth as a priority, as well as blocking a “business-as-usual” National Defense Authorization Act in the lame duck and refusing to fund the Department of Defense if “it doesn’t cut off money for the war.”

But it’s not clear that Democrats in Congress are ready to play that kind of hard ball. As they weigh their approach to the next era, they’re still half-stunned themselves by their larger than expected wins — and trying to put themselves in the best position ahead of the next elections in 2028.

GOP Rep. Christopher H. Smith of New Jersey told reporters on Thursday at the Capitol he hopes Democrats don’t hold up the NDAA “for the good of the country.”

“The NDAA is such an important bill for our national security, and it’s usually very bipartisan. I hope it is,” he said.

John Donnelly and Jacob Fulton contributed to this report.

#6873. Robert Pattinson, I’m Begging You For a Quirked-Up James Bond

Our long international nightmare is finally over: two-time Meme of the Year nominee Robert Pattinson has been chosen as Daniel Craig’s James Bond successor, bringing an end to an exhausting series of “reports” and “leaks” about Amazon’s search for a new 007. Finally we can stop the debates and fan-casting and let Patts and Denis Villeneuve cook until they’re ready to present their new vision. But that would require a level of restraint that the internet doesn’t typically exercise. Immediately after the protracted process of casting Bond concluded, the internet moved on to naming its dream picks for Bond Girls and Bond Villains (sometimes, to brilliant effect.) I can’t say I have a dog in either fight, at least not right now. My Bond WishList is of a different magnitude altogether: can we make James Bond silly again? Just a little, even, 15 percent goofball?

In the hands of another (read: more limited) actor, I’d never suggest such a risky swerve. But the best thing about Pattinson is his range. Sure, if Villeneuve and co. want to render Bond as the stoic and taciturn gentleman he’s generally been this century, Rob has proven he can do that, as every Tenet truther has been gleefully preaching for the last 24 hours. But more often than not, Pattinson is keen to get weird, whether that’s going Full Freak, as he does in Primetime, or approaching certain archetypal parts with a silly, goofy mood (like his conniving suitor in The Odyssey). He can do a “voice” (Mickey 17), he can do scumbag grime (Good Time), he can do hapless screwball (The Drama). Why not make what’ll be his biggest role to date a little cocktail of all his best elements? (Okay, he can leave the Mickey 17 voice behind.)

20 years ago, Casino Royale was a refreshing reset and a crucial corrective to the overflowing ridiculousness of the Piece Brosnan era. But in the past two decades, the pendulum has swung back—the gritty reboot has become studios' stock-in-trade. I feel the same way about Batman; the actual risky choice would be to make something less like Nolan’s series that, instead, hewed a little closer to, say, Batman Forever, or even the gothic camp of Tim Burton’s two films.

Of course, Robert Pattinson was at the center of a Batman reboot as well, and the result was a movie that felt more like a spiritual cousin to David Fincher’s Se7en; audiences ate it up so much that the hunger for a sequel persists even after endless delays. When it comes to franchises as high-stakes as Batman or Bond, you can’t trust these increasingly sauceless execs to make anything but the basic choice. There’s no reason to expect Jeff Bezos isn’t texting Denis screenshots from Skyfall for reference from his megayacht at this very moment.

But if there’s anybody capable of putting a little English on the handle while still giving the mainstream what it wants, it’s Big Bob Pattinson. He could easily make his James Bond a little off, or he could go full seductive cad who smiles like Neil Tenet, has a ki with Q, and charms women with some playful banter instead of simply brooding them into submission. I’m not looking for invisible cars and sharks with lasers, or a Lothario like Roger Moore who speaks in puns. All I’m asking is that we give camp a chance. Because ultimately, the most exciting thing Villeneuve and Pattinson can do to set their Bond apart would be to loosen him up a little.

#6870. Upgrade Your Basics: Best Undershirts for Men in 2026

Just because you don't see a building's foundation doesn't mean it isn't holding everything up. In much the same way, the best undershirts for men are the humble base layer of countless great outfits. Many GQ staffers, in fact, wear-test theirs daily, and yet they're often overlooked, even though they've long-perfected their role: to be the protective layer between your gross body and your steezy, attention-hungry shirts and polos.

And while they're certainly good for some added warmth in the winter, there's a common misconception that they can make the wearer too hot. In fact, lightweight, moisture-wicking undershirts made from fabrics like modal, or cotton blends can actually transfer the sweat away from your body, speeding up your cooling down. All that's to say: Undershirts are more than just men’s underwear. They’re the cornerstone of a hall-of-fame fit.

An undershirt is an undershirt is an undershirt. If you want the most straightforward version, the kind that you’re picturing in your head is most likely from Calvin Klein. It’s a classic crewneck undershirt that’s made of a lightweight jersey that’s 100% cotton which has a smooth, very slightly brushed hand that feels worn-in in the best way possible. The slim fit won’t cause any weird bunching or bulging and it’s got enough length to stay tucked even when you’re out on the town cutting rugs.

My colleague, GQ senior commerce editor Avidan Grossman, has been wearing CK's genre-defining undershirts since his high school years. (He could do without the logo at the hem, but has made his peace with it, given that you never see it once it's tucked.) Otherwise, he prizes Calvin Klein's riff for its simple, pared-down design and blessed lack of modern-day frills. After all, not every shirt needs to moisture wick or odor block. If you just want an undershirt that gets the job done, don't overthink it.

Sometimes, sleeves are just too much undershirt. That’s where tanks come in. Also known as A-shirts, the humble, sleeveless tank has been rocked by every heartthrob and neighborhood grandpa as far back as anyone can remember. And while the low neck and lack of sleeves means you’ll practically never see them underneath a button-up, the irony is that they look great shown off with an unbuttoned camp shirt or on their own.

For the money, we think Hanes takes the cake with their multipack of undershirts. The stretchy ribbed jersey is made of 100% cotton and fits snug with a low neck that’s slightly squared off compared to most tanks. They may cost just under twenty bucks for a pack of six, but they look like a million bucks. “I’ve had buyer's remorse over many a menswear garment over the years,” explains GQ editor Jordan Bowman, "but the one thing that has never let me down is the humble tank top from Hanes. They nailed the fit and the material, and the great thing is, you can buy a pack just about anywhere."

If every part of your wardrobe has to be fit for a king, down to the most basics of basics, then CDLP is what we’d recommend, my liege. The reality is that most luxury tees don’t offer much more than the regular stuff. It’s diminishing returns after a certain price point, but CDLP is the notable exception. My colleague, our contributing editor Louis Cheslaw, has put his same CDLP cottons through the ringer year after year, and says they still haven't lost their luster.

It’s a pricey undershirt, for sure, but the high-quality fabric has a silky hand and lightweight feel that’s unparalleled. (It really feels like you’re almost wearing nothing.) Thanks to a wider neckline, you’re a lot less likely to see the tee poking out, too. And if you feel like such a nice T-shirt is wasted as underwear, don’t worry—the cut and shorter length means it looks great untucked, and can double as your regular T-shirt too.

Mack Weldon’s silver series of garments is what happens when you get nerdy about chemistry and fashion (in the best possible way). The Ionic+ technology embeds silver charged silver ions which are naturally antimicrobial into the fabric itself. Essentially, that means bacteria and odor is neutralized, which means you can take it easy on the deodorant. Beyond that, the tees are made of super long staple Supima cotton and a smidge of lycra for an extra silky fee, comfortable stretch, and long-lasting wear.

The main bummer is that they’ll cost you more than most tees, so stocking up for the week can get pricey. Still, it’s one of those undershirts that makes you realize you’ve been settling for squalor—plus, you can bundle up and get 10% off to help offset the sticker shock.

Not everything at Uniqlo is a great value. But when it comes to upgraded basics at an affordable price, you’d be hard-pressed to find brand that does it better. When you need an airy, breathable undershirt that's truly lightweight, the Japanese mega-retailer’s AIRism tees are legendary. They’re crafted from a smooth and breathable cotton-polyester-spandex blend that dries quickly, feels cool to the touch, and fights odors—a triple threat that’ll have sweaty days in a headlock. I personally wear these—they're a failsafe way to save my expensive dress shirts from dreaded pit stains, but they’re also perfect for wearing as a gym shirt. And at just $15 per tee, there’s no other undershirt that can do as much for the price.

GQ contributor Evan Malachosky was always a little skeptical of Skims, but the brand's tees won him over. He found that Skims’s proprietary fabrics are silky on the skin, and are neither too thin (and therefore exposing), nor too thick (and therefore too hot). Pleasingly, the brand has applied just as much care to its undershirt, even though—or perhaps because—it's designed to look invisible under your clothes. (The design team even thought about cleaning up the seam lines, so nothing would show through.) Made from 100% cotton, and available in an incredibly wide range of sizes, our favorite detail is that it's a little bit longer than the brand's standard tees, so that there's no risk of any unsightly seams being visible at the hemline, either.

If the purpose of an undershirt is to fly under the radar, then it stands to reason that a V-neck one—which remains invisible even when you unbutton your shirt collar—might be the most efficient of all the styles here. You'll still want to trust the classic makers here, which is why our favorite comes from Calvin Klein. Like the Calvin Klein crewneck option above, the fabric is smooth, slightly brushed, and 100% cotton. It's also affordable, and available in not just white, black, and gray, but also a multipack comprising all three. Lastly, CK's trademark slimmer fit means no bunching or bulging risk.

Are these “undershirts” far heavier than the usual ones? Yes. But they never lose their shape, and if you live in a bitterly cold climate, you'll want even your undershirt to max out its warming properties.

The luxury car of undershirts, Falke's premium undershirts go on just as smoothly (and last just as long) as the beloved German brand's famous socks.

You heard it here first: Ralph Lauren's Pony has become the coolest label in menswear again.

When James Bond—well, Daniel Craig as James Bond—needs an undershirt, he wears Sunspel's.

Kirkland Signature is somewhat of a fashion industry secret. These are made of soft, long-staple Egyptian cotton, and they're tube knit, meaning you don't have to worry about any uncomfortable side seams.

Made from rayon and polyester, Hanes’ moisture-wicking undershirts are fast-drying, cool to the touch, and silky smooth, all for just $5 a pop.

If you wanna let your clavicles shine uninhibited by any undershirt peek-age, a V-neck is the best way (short of a tank) to do it.

Cut from a premium cotton, this lightweight undershirt is going to feel a little thicker and warmer than others on this list, purely because Reigning Champ's premium cotton fabrics tend to lean towards the beefier end of the spectrum.

Though Mack Weldon’s 18-Hour Rib Tank doesn’t come with the microbe-smashing odor technology as their Silver crew neck undershirt, it does have an even silkier fabric and plenty of stretch.

The first thing I noticed when I met Wythe's founder was not his jeans, or amazing western shirt. It was actually this ribbed tank.

Undershirts are meant to be that intermediate layer between your outermost layers and your body, a protective guard that keeps your star garments fresh and free of sweat. Therefore, it's materials, construction, and fit that are the main components that determine how good an undershirt (and, well, every garment) actually is.

Above all else, your undershirt's fabric should be breathable, soft, comfortable, and, ideally, sweat-wicking. We primarily love undershirts and tanks that are made from high-quality 100% cotton, which has many of those characteristics naturally, but we also feel like there’s a time and place for synthetics, which are often used for their ability to wick away moisture and dry easily—especially for those who run particularly hot. And note: Stretchy materials like Spandex are helpful for comfort, but their elasticity will eventually degrade over time.

Undershirts should almost always fit closer to the body than your regular T-shirts. This is because they’ll be layered underneath another shirt or sweater, and can bunch in odd ways if too baggy. They don’t need to be skin-tight, but they should be slim enough to layer comfortably and long enough to tuck into your shirt. In fact, Peter Middleton, founder of Wythe, even told us that they should fit slim to start, since they are cotton and are knit, and will therefore stretch a little over time. If they are loose to start with, they are only going to become looser and saggier, which is really not the point.

Style is subjective, we know—that’s the fun of it. But we’re serious about helping our audience get dressed. Whether it’s the best white sneakers, the flyest affordable suits, or the need-to-know menswear drops of the week, GQ Recommends’ perspective is built on years of hands-on experience, an insider awareness of what’s in and what’s next, and a mission to find the best version of everything out there, at every price point.

Our staffers aren’t able to try on every single piece of clothing you read about on GQ.com (fashion moves fast these days), but we have an intimate knowledge of each brand’s strengths and know the hallmarks of quality clothing—from materials and sourcing, to craftsmanship, to sustainability efforts that aren’t just greenwashing. GQ Recommends heavily emphasizes our own editorial experience with those brands, how they make their clothes, and how those clothes have been reviewed by customers. Bottom line: GQ wouldn’t tell you to wear it if we wouldn’t.

We make every effort to cast as wide of a net as possible, with an eye on identifying the best options across three key categories: quality, fit, and price.

To kick off the process, we enlist the GQ Recommends braintrust to vote on our contenders. Some of the folks involved have worked in retail, slinging clothes to the masses; others have toiled for small-batch menswear labels; all spend way too much time thinking about what hangs in their closets.

We lean on that collective experience to guide our search, culling a mix of household names, indie favorites, and the artisanal imprints on the bleeding-edge of the genre. Then we narrow down the assortment to the picks that scored the highest across quality, fit, and price.

Across the majority of our buying guides, our team boasts firsthand experience with the bulk of our selects, but a handful are totally new to us. So after several months of intense debate, we tally the votes, collate the anecdotal evidence, and emerge with a list of what we believe to be the absolute best of the category right now, from the tried-and-true stalwarts to the modern disruptors, the affordable beaters to the wildly expensive (but wildly worth-it) designer riffs.

Whatever your preferences, whatever your style, there's bound to be a superlative version on this list for you. (Read more about GQ's testing process here.)
