#6866. Grand Theft Auto VI Review: Better Than the Hype

There are moments in GTA 6 that’ll have you levitating off your sofa. Unprecedented in its scope and production, it was created by a team of almost 5,000 developers over nearly 10 years. Its budget is rumored to rival the construction of Dubai’s Burj Khalifa skyscraper; its first trailer set records with 93 million views in a day. Every social media brand account, from the White House to BMW, has jumped on the hype, while economists predict a national sick day as millions play hooky to game. When you do, you see the time, talent and flair on the screen. But, at its heart, Grand Theft Auto VI follows in series tradition: an epic American crime story, set in a stunning parody of the US that riffs on film, music, politics, style and satire.

Fronting the first franchise entry in more than 13 years are Jason and Lucia. Developer Rockstar attempted a multi-character arc in Grand Theft Auto V, but those three leads learned to loathe one another. Instead, VI tackles love, a game design challenge most developers have sworn off with good reason. Not a parent/child relationship like The Last of Us or God of War, but a mature, romantic, adult one. You can call or text each other at pretty much any point—to do missions together, commit robberies, explore, meditate, work out, fly, or just to coexist under the Vice City sunshine.

This isn’t some kind of ChatGPT fantasy to indulge in. Neither Jason or Lucia exists at your beck and call as some kind of hyper-realistic plaything. You have a role in their relationship, both in the way they speak to each other and where the story takes them. You can cruise down the Vice City waterfront together in your souped-up ’70s muscle wagon with one of you driving, the other scrolling through your Snapmatic feed (the in-game social media channel), before grabbing a drink and carving up the sidewalk with an assault rifle five minutes later. You just don’t get these detours in Call of Duty.

More than a car: in GTA VI, your vehicle is a mobile base of operations to stash your guns, fits, money, and drugs.

It’s a different pace from previous Grand Theft Autos too. Rockstar changed its dynamics with 2018’s Red Dead Redemption 2—a more thoughtful take on the outlaw tale that added friction to the action and a conscience to the narrative. For a developer that once experienced the spotlight of US Congress during 2005’s infamous “Hot Coffee" controversy (when a sexually explicit mini-game meant as an inside joke between developers was figured out by savvy users), this kind of nuance was revelatory. With VI, Rockstar takes Red Dead 2’s ethos and applies it to a modern city with 1,000 x the population. The result is staggering, but still an absolute blast to play. That is to say, the greatest hits of GTA—driving cars, shooting guns, and stealing money, drugs and more (all to spend on a better Beretta, G-Wagon replica, and impeccably modeled Air Jordan dupes)—but with added depth to how you play. Weapon slots are finite; you can no longer run amok with an entire arsenal stashed in your jacket pocket. Money is vulnerable; get killed or caught with cash in your possession, and you’ll lose it. And if you jack a car that’s fitted with a tracker, the police will find you quickly.

Should you get into a bust-up with the law after a jewelry store smash-and-grab gone wrong, the game’s action is frenetic. Roads are much narrower, traffic is denser, and you can’t see police on your minimap. That $40,000 in cash sitting on the back seat had better be worth the hassle. As such, the game supports your decision to play chaotically, but truly immerses you in playing methodically. Jason and Lucia each have a “criminal profile”—be unnecessarily violent and you’ll get a “psycho” rating—that adds replayability and roleplay. Still, I had the most fun when planning missions: using a silenced pistol, shooting to maim not kill, and wiping security footage to leave no trace. It’s the closest GTA has honed in on the art of crime. The feds will surround the store you’re robbing. “Come out right now with your hands above your head!” Do things right, and you’ll have prepped your partner with a getaway car out back. You’ll leave the scene anonymously as backup blares past you on the other side of the road.

Every club, pub and dingy dive bar in GTA 6’s Leonida has a distinct vibe—from the music to the decor to the sweaty Floridians inside.

The game kicks off with an electrifying setup accompanied by an original song from composers Justice. It perfectly sets the tone: VI has the ’80s flamboyance of Vice City made new and fresh. It takes its opening hours steadily, sending you on odd jobs to make a little money here and there. Gradually—then suddenly—you’re thrust into the most high-octane campaign in Grand Theft Auto history. Tense hospital escapes, daring bank heists, cross-country chases, and more. Its set pieces are balls-to-the-wall bravado, rivaling what you’d see in Nolan’s The Odyssey or Villeneuve’s Dune, upping in intensity through its chapters. Tonally, it’s the most “of the now” Grand Theft Auto game yet, as your two in-love criminals race to find a better life in an unfair world. No previous GTA has given you a savings account—it’s like playing the millennial curse. The only dings are some pacing in the game’s second chapter as the game wrestles with its story while still introducing mechanics. Think of it as VI’s version of RDR2’s Guarma, albeit with bargain bin stores and grimy strip malls instead of exotic Caribbean fauna.

And then to Leonida, with which Rockstar has created the most technically and artistically impressive game world ever made. The pre-release trailers promised beaches bustling with vacationers and nightclubs packed with dancers. That is the reality. From sea to sand to sky, the world is alive. Vice City alone could be an entire game in its own right, complete with distinct districts, an explorable international airport, and more. Yet, Rockstar spoils us with a massive expanse of varied towns, high-class beach resorts, lush green national parks, murky swamplands and sun-kissed island keys. All packed with people that range from useful, to funny, to completely unsavory (shout-out to the old woman who took one look at my Jason and said “I’mma take my teeth out and suck the skin off that dick”). The density and fidelity of it all often stretches the limits of even Sony’s PlayStation 5 Pro. Frame rate dips don’t spoil gameplay, but the slowdown is noticeable when you’ve punted a couple of grenades into passing cars to set off multiple explosions at once.

NPCs don’t just feel like backdrop mannequins anymore, but real people with an incredible range of faces, body types and outfits.

Every frame a photograph. Stop anywhere in GTA 6, and you’ll likely find something enticing to see, hear or interact with.

As with Red Dead Redemption 2, the most impressive of the game’s many achievements is how everything seems to live on without you. All it took to make this world was Rockstar founding an entire studio to work solely on Leonida’s population. I’ve seen a woman throw herself out of a cop car window as I scooted along Ocean Beach, and car thieves burst through the glass windows of a car dealership while the alarms howl. I saw a furry taken down by armed police, staged perfectly with a helicopter spotlight. During one 3 a.m. wander through the rough neighborhood of Crosstown, a man approached me to warn me of the local gang and tells me they have a nearby base. I accidentally tapped the button that brings up your in-game phone. “Ohhhh! You don’t just believe what I say” the man says, responding to my actions. “You do your own research!” In more than 70 hours of play, I have seen only one moment of repetition.

There is so much more besides. A cast of interesting, funny and believable characters; off-the-beaten-path secrets that can lead to side quests both big and small; perhaps the best radio track list of any GTA spanning from Travis Scott to Lana Del Rey; podcast episodes; golf to play; gators to hunt; dozens of supercars to steal and customize; weapons that feel punchy and satisfying; multiple racing series; a litany of actually interesting shops and bars and clubs to explore and buy things in. I’ve seen brilliant horror vignettes, startling science fiction, and laugh-out-loud comedy just by exploring. Rockstar has created a world that rewards you for spending time in it. Expect to spend 100 hours if you want to see everything.

As you’ll likely have seen and heard—in everything from Miami Heat takeovers, to Netflix launches, to Selfridges pop-ups—that this is the most-wanted entertainment property in the world. In a way, all it had to be was Grand Theft Auto V: The Second. But, on the largest and most scrutinized stage imaginable, Rockstar takes creative risks with its story, protagonists, and mechanics, and makes big leaps in what games can be. It’s unlikely other developers will catch up soon. In 2002, Grand Theft Auto: Vice City reshaped and refashioned pop culture to give us a playable version of a place and a time we already knew. A quarter of a century later, VI shows Rockstar at the peak of entertainment in its very own right.

#6864. Hailey Bieber’s “Blue Slushy Manicure” Is Fall’s Most Unexpected Nail Trend

Sorry, burgundy, but there’s a new color du jour for fall

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

As the seasons shift from summer to fall, nails start shifting from brighter hues to moody colors like burgundy and brown. But maybe there’s a place for vibrant color in the colder months after all? At least, Hailey Bieber thinks so: The beauty mogul just debuted “blue slushy” nails for fall.

This past week, Bieber attended back-to-back business events in New York. First, at the 2026 DealBook Summit at Lincoln Center—a conference that unites some of the biggest leaders in tech, business, and politics—Bieber came to the stage in a black-and-white ensemble to talk Rhode in a spring/summer 1998 outfit from Yohji Yamamoto. Famously worn by Carolyn Bessette Kennedy, the minimalist look served as the perfect neutral backdrop for her electric blue nails. Just one day earlier, Bieber was seen with the same blue manicure at the WWD 2026 Beauty Inc. Awards—this time paired with a black-and-white dress and contrasting brick-red lip.

The Rhode founder has long been a big fan of chrome manicures, and her current mani appears to have a hint of shimmer. This isn’t the first time she’s rocked blue on her nails. While in New York this past summer, she was seen with a similar shade—but instead of the electric “blue slushy” color, this one leaned more powdery.

Bieber also loves to sport blue on her toes. In July, she posted a photo showcasing how the Rhode pimple patches can be used to heal blisters. Seen alongside the pastel-colored pimple patches was her sea-blue pedicure.

Blue has been on Bieber’s mind lately (not to mention “Blues” is the middle name of her son Jack). Her latest collection for Rhode, which officially launches November 20, pegged to her 30th birthday, features navy colorways for the first time, both for the product packaging and on the brand’s first-ever wearable merch. The collection includes the brand’s first matte blush in a mini size, a reversible windbreaker, a phone case accessory, and a new variation of its Peptide Eye Prep patches and Peptide Lip Boost—all in a rich shade of blue.

#6862. Meeks vows Iran votes as he seeks Foreign Affairs gavel

Rep. Gregory W. Meeks, D-N.Y., and staffers cheer as House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., takes the stage during a election night watch party in Washington on Tuesday. (Tom Williams/CQ Roll Call)

Rep. Gregory W. Meeks, D-N.Y., the presumed next chairman of the House Foreign Affairs Committee, plans to bring resolutions to the floor to “immediately” end the war with Iran when Democrats take control of the House in January, he wrote in a letter Thursday seeking his colleagues’ support to become chairman.

The letter, which officially announced his bid for the chairmanship, lays out his oversight agenda for the committee, which will also see considerable turnover on the GOP side of the dais.

Meeks and other Democrats have repeatedly forced votes this year on war powers resolutions seeking to end the Iran war, with a Meeks-sponsored one clearing both chambers of Congress.

But those resolutions have been nonbinding concurrent resolutions, since the 1973 War Powers Resolution only lays out a fast-track procedure the minority party can wield for concurrent resolutions in the House.

With a House majority, though, Democrats could bring binding joint resolutions to the floor.

Beyond war powers votes, Meeks also promised public hearings on the war. He also vowed hearings on a range of topics including the administration’s Venezuela policy and “repeated defiance of Congress's constitutional authority over war powers and trade.”

“As chairman in the 120th Congress, oversight of this administration’s lawlessness and corruption will be at the top of the agenda,” Meeks wrote. “I will continue to advance a principled foreign policy grounded in our values, including support for Ukraine against Russian aggression.”

In addition to Iran measures, Meeks also promised legislation focused on “clawing back Congress’s proper role in the making of American foreign policy” and a State Department reauthorization bill that puts guardrails on the administration and protects career diplomats.

Meanwhile, the GOP side of the committee is poised to lose at least a dozen lawmakers — or about half of its members — next year between retirements and election losses.

The departures include longtime members such as former Chairman Michael McCaul, R-Texas, who opted not to seek reelection, and Rep. Scott Perry, R-Pa., the Freedom Caucus chair who lost his reelection race.

Several other Republican committee members remained locked in races that were still too close to call as of Thursday afternoon, according to The Associated Press, potentially gutting the GOP committee bench further. They include GOP Rep. Young Kim of California, the East Asia and Pacific Subcommittee chair who was trailing her in race against fellow California Republican Rep. Ken Calvert.

Rep. Christopher H. Smith, R-N.J., who chairs the Africa Subcommittee, lamented the turnover Thursday but predicted a lot of interest among remaining House Republicans to fill the void.

“There will be many who will want to be on it,” Smith told reporters. “We lost some really great people. Yesterday I can't tell you how sad I was about friends who are now not coming back.”

#6861. How Celine’s Michael Rider Quietly Seized Fashion’s Attention

You can tell a lot about a designer by the scene backstage after a show. Usually, it’s sweaty, manic, crammed with hangers-on—the fashion equivalent of an airport lounge during a blizzard. But on the unseasonably warm October day of Michael Rider’s most recent Paris runway show, the space was alive with Champagne toasts and bubbly conversation.

The co-ed summer 2027 runway was a blowout party hosted by Rider with models channeling twitchy new-wave dancers, preppy ’80s croquet players, and the heroic DIY divas from Paris Is Burning. Rider showed new tailoring shapes for men, specifically loose, floppy blazers and slim cigarette-leg pants, cropped confidently above the ankle. A showstopping look of gleaming pink separates somehow avoided silliness. Rider’s signature styling took the edge off. A silver shimmering suit was mildly de-glammed by a jacket tied around the waist. A jaunty black hat topped off an outfit with a white long-sleeve shirt and shorts. Improbably, these wild ideas felt coherent—a hard-won sleight of hand.

In the garden of the National Archives Museum, friends of Rider like Hannah Einbinder, Tracee Ellis Ross, and Dan Levy milled around waiting for the designer to finish telling critics about his “intuitive” approach to fashion. Like something out of Lewis Carroll, a spread was unfurled on a long outdoor table. Waiters poured Champagne into a tower of coupes; editors licked homemade popsicles and bit into crunchy sandwiches of jambon-beurre. No one seemed like they had anywhere to be. This was the place to be.

In less than two years, Rider has cleaned the slate following Hedi Slimane’s shrunken, skinny rock era at Celine. He’s brought more accessible, artfully styled fashion to the stores. He’s delivered at least two hit shoes: the dirty white jazz lace-up, and, this past September, an immediately sold-out collaboration with Reebok that reissued the 1982 Freestyle Lo sneaker in decadent leather, in shades from oxblood to buttery yellow.

In the process, his Celine has become a favorite among fashion-minded men. Ebon Moss-Bachrach, the tall Girls and The Bear actor, told me backstage that Rider’s menswear feels “masculine without any kind of footnote.” Youngjae, from the K-pop band TWS, explained the allure of Rider’s vision. “The Celine man is very free,” he said—just as the wild-haired designer burst outside for a smoke, laughing and relieved.

Wearing a standard-issue, backstage-friendly black crewneck sweater, Rider wasn’t exactly channeling the improvisational look of the brand he’s re-engineered in only a handful of shows. He assumed the role of artistic director in 2025, succeeding Slimane. Before taking Celine’s reins, he put in time under Nicolas Ghesquière at Balenciaga (2004–08) and at Phoebe Philo–era Celine (2008–17). But the secret to the Rider Effect—his unique ability to conjure clarity from chaos—was a lesson he picked up during his tenure at Ralph Lauren.

Rider worked for the all-American designer as creative director of women’s Polo Ralph Lauren from 2018 until he decamped for Celine. Early on, he threw himself into staging his first “rig room.” A very Ralph tradition, the rig room is an installation of a fashion collection designed to be presented to Lauren internally—a fully immersive world complete with music, decor, props, and reference photos. Rider worked on his first one incessantly for two months. When Lauren arrived, he looked around in silence while Rider stood beside him, sweating in a navy blazer.

At last, Lauren asked, “Why do you want to confuse people?”

“I think about that a lot,” confesses Rider when we speak in a circular antechamber at Celine’s Paris headquarters a few days after the show. Disappointing Lauren was a turning point for Rider. He had gotten the job designing Polo after 14 years in the upper echelons of European fashion. “Mixing references and confusing people was part of the game,” he remembers. “You elevated yourself and your brand through adjacencies and pastiche. Ralph was the total opposite.”

“I definitely appreciated going back to the States and hearing from a master: Be clear,” says Rider. The ongoing tension of his current work lies in resolving his tendency toward anarchy with a desire for legibility. His Celine is layered and unfettered and fun. But it is also as clear, as the French say, as water on rocks.

Sitting in the same room where he and Philo “dumped our bags and met people” during his first stint at Celine, Rider looks calm and perfectly at home. Now that he’s at the helm of the 81-year-old LVMH maison founded by Céline and Richard Vipiana, the “rotonde” room has a few framed New Yorker cartoons propped on a wicker bench, some personalized thank-you cards to send, a pack of cigs, Evian, and not much else. Twinkly and alert, with no phone in sight, Rider wears a blue button-up, dark khaki pants, black canvas trainers, a gold Cartier watch, and some chunky rings. His hair sticks straight up like one of his inspirations: Isaac Mizrahi.

Mizrahi’s Unzipped documentary, which recently celebrated its 30th anniversary, was one of the references for the summer show. So, too, was another canonic ’90s documentary, Paris Is Burning, about the queer ballroom scene in late-’80s Manhattan. Both films look at the human side of fabulosity, something that interests Rider. Brands are the creations of curious, restless minds, and the best transcend desire, attracting something close to faith. Rider himself has been one of those believers, lusting after Ghesquière’s Balenciaga during his first real job, when he was just an underpaid teacher. (“I would have killed for something from there,” he says.) In his show notes, he wrote about “our desires and frustrations. Memories, moments that made us love fashion. Moments where you get a glimpse of possibility.”

In practice, that looks like something you want to buy, but that has soul. Rider, like Lauren and Mizrahi, is a designer who makes clothing and accessories that feel just familiar enough to strike a nostalgic chord, but fresh enough that they can’t be replaced by trawling The RealReal. In his first Celine collection, shown in July 2025 under a sudden Parisian downpour, the looks bore a healthy pinch of Polo-honed prep: red blazers, diagonal-stripe ties, pressed jeans, loafers. Now that style is officially everywhere, with every middle-class menswear brand and fast-fashion factory churning out their own versions.

What separates Rider’s Celine is that it gets men excited to dress, says Nordstrom men’s fashion director Jian DeLeon: “It triggers that lizard-brain impulse that inspires them to shop. They didn’t realize they wanted a bright-blue military jacket, and then when they see it in store, Rider’s design nuances—like oversized, ruched sleeves and slightly different blocks of color in the fabric—further satisfy the kinds of bells and whistles that men’s fashion enthusiasts nerd out about.”

In his first solo men’s presentation this past January, Rider brought his Ralph-perfected merchandising flair to Celine for a rig-room-esque installation of styling combinations that shouldn’t have worked—like a paisley tie layered beneath a red-string, hippie pendant necklace—and yet, did. Loafers in every shade played to an audience of ’90s kids who lusted after stacks of Benetton sweaters. Army jackets became accessories, belted around waists. It was American mall culture whipped through a blender with ready-to-wear refinement.

Rider’s Celine made an impact even earlier with his very first outing, the co-ed spring 2026 collection, shown in mid-2025. Soon after that range was available, Paul Anthony Kelly, who played nouveau John F. Kennedy Jr. in Ryan Murphy’s hit FX series Love Story, wore a Kennedy-ish Celine look: a white Oxford shirt, red-and-navy striped tie, navy blazer, jeans, white socks, loafers. The message was clear: Kelly had arrived—and so had Rider. Kelly’s stylist, Warren Alfie Baker, says he got hundreds of messages from people saying, “Oh my God, I need to go and buy that.” Why did he think it hit so hard? “What Michael does really well is it feels relatable,” Baker explained. “Everybody wanted to replicate it or have a version of it.”

Baker, who styles classic leading-men types like Glen Powell, Andrew Scott, and Andrew Garfield, says Rider’s work was heating up in Hollywood ahead of the fall awards season. “When you see a runway with 45 looks, and you’re like, ‘I could actually use 40 of them,’ that’s the dream scenario.”

Slimane had introduced menswear to Celine in 2018 for the first time in the brand’s history with skinny black jeans, skinnier black ties, and shrunken leather jackets—an aesthetic driven more by rock-and-roll than red carpets. When he brought the brand to Los Angeles in late 2022 for an 87-look fashion show-cum-concert at the Wiltern, rockers including Iggy Pop, the Strokes, Interpol, and the Kills wore Slimane’s indie-sleaze uniforms to perform. “Celine’s past has been very skinny. Maybe some guys wouldn’t have known that they could wear it,” conceded Baker.

Rider’s Celine offers more elbow room, literally and otherwise. “It’s not such a rigid idea of how a man should dress,” says fashion commentator and stylist Nicky Campbell, who has styled Mark Ronson in several Rider looks, including a custom leather jacket for this year’s Brit Awards. “Other menswear brands are too rigid: They want you to be this really formal, masculine man, or they want you to be super androgynous. He lands in this in-between space.”

Born in 1980 to two do-gooder attorney parents in Washington DC, Rider was raised in an intellectual, progressive household well outside the fashion bubble. Musing on why he’s still drawn to things that are a little off, and the stalls at the flea market with “the mangiest, strangest assortment of clothing,” he says. “My parents are not materialistic in any way. There are nice things around, and they make things. My mom’s an incredible cook, there’s needlepoint, my dad [told] great stories. But it wasn’t a situation where aesthetic perfection was the goal.”

“I was always in my own strange lane,” says Rider. “Everybody who seemed cool to me was on their own path.” His older cousin introduced him to the Grateful Dead, a lifelong love. Rider was, naturally, first drawn to the T-shirts—“the merch always brings me in”—but then became fascinated by the totality of Deadhead culture. “I was very attracted to the experimentation and freedom and fewer rules,” he says. “I like color, I like people who customize things, and I like weirdos.”

The Dead were a gateway into an adolescence full of experimentation. “I had all the phases,” Rider says, from Birkenstocks worn with Thorlo hiking socks, to hip-hop streetwear, to skate style that he finessed in shops like Green Barrel in Georgetown and Stüssy in the suburb of Potomac. He also dabbled in the all-American look he’d later help shape at Polo. “We went to public school and were proud of it, so preppy was a bad word, but everybody at my school worshipped Polo. They just wore it differently, and we mixed it all.”

Rider’s still mixing. “It’s always been a lot of fun for me to get dressed,” he says. When he lived in New York City while at Ralph Lauren, he had standing movie nights at the Battery Park City cinema with the photographer Tommy Ton and designer Trevor Ballin (who works with him now at Celine), both friends. Ton, a street-style savant, loved seeing the outfits Rider would pull off at the cinema, like ankle-chopped Uggs with an Hermès shawl, or Grateful Dead bear–embroidered shorts. Levy says he could see Rider’s own vacation wardrobe in the women’s looks of the summer collection. “He shows up to dinner in caftans and sandals, or harem pants.”

Which can be hard to square with the fact that Rider went to Brown University with the intention of becoming a teacher. He moved to Oakland after graduating and taught ninth grade at a charter school. “Everyone was bigger than me,” says the petite designer. Living in the Bay Area, going out a lot, and experimenting with clothing, he eventually felt a pull to the creative world that he couldn’t shake, which would lead to him interning with his then hero, Ghesquière.

Rider, the former teacher, remains a perpetual student. He tells me, with a glance at his PR minder, that the kind of fashion profiles he loves—like Judith Thurman’s unvarnished 2005 New Yorker story on Rei Kawakubo—aren’t really possible anymore. In the post-Galliano-meltdown, recession-hardened luxury landscape, publicly traded conglomerates protect their creative directors from criticism like the precious assets that they are.

So he painstakingly protects his distance—but doesn’t sacrifice his sense of humor in the process. You’ll see it in the pop of outrageous purple, a woolly outdoorsy fleece, those shimmering suits. There’s a sense that being too serious is just kind of sad. He likes when people talk about Celine as “good-time clothes” that they wear when they go out. “I would be mortified if anyone thought silliness was on the menu,” Rider says. Still, “Things are very funny to us, and that has to be felt somehow. I think humor is really stylish.”

Eons have passed, seemingly, since Rider interned in the menswear department of Balenciaga in the mid-aughts. “Fashion for men was very fringe then,” he says, remembering that the men’s collection at the label totaled maybe a dozen looks. At the time, Slimane’s strict silhouettes for Dior Homme were in; Rider wore some of that, along with the stiff selvedge APC jeans that became part of the ascent of #menswear. He recalls Ghesquière being obsessed at the time with making the ideal men’s wardrobe, from button-ups to parkas.

“Menswear has completely opened up now,” Rider says. “It’s a different ball game. Because it’s so much broader now, and people have phones; people everywhere have access to so much and are engaged in more conversations. Not always for good, but it moves. The rules can move, and the goalposts can move.”

Rider, after an almost Forrest Gump-y run of consistently being at the center of gravity—at Balenciaga, with Philo, and at Ralph Lauren—without having tried so hard to be there, is now the one doing the goalpost-moving. And he has no interest in hard rules or self-inflicted limitations.

“I think a lot of people who do this thing end up living, or trying to live out, the same story over and over,” he says.

He resists sheeplike notions of what’s considered cool—“this artist, this pottery, this lamp, this show space collaboration,” as he puts it. And he’s careful not to follow the flock too closely. “Paris is amazing, but Paris is insular,” says Rider. “Everyone’s eating at the same canteen, gossiping about the same thing. There’s a gigantic world out there.”

So Rider keeps doing his own thing. He revels in American pop culture—he’s just as passionate about going to the multiplex near his weekend home in Normandy to see the latest Wicked as he is paging through those old World of Interiors magazines. He’s started weight-lifting, in the evenings. Learning Arabic is on his bucket list. And often he finds himself going back to his roots for inspiration.

Every summer, he returns to Martha’s Vineyard, where his family has had a house since the 1970s. He loves its isolation, and its realness: “Against all odds, it has remained what it is. There’s no bridge, the airport’s still tiny, 85 percent of it is protected. There’s no Balenciaga.” On Martha’s Vineyard, he likes being out on the water.

“The most American thing about me is that I like to paddle,” he says, laughing. “I’m my best self in a canoe.” ✦

#6858. Your Exclusive First Look at the Official 'Cameron Winter at Carnegie' Hall Merch

“I will keep rolling down until my best shirt rolls off,” warbles Cameron Winter on “The Rolling Stone,” the opening track of his 2024 solo debut Heavy Metal. That album later became the basis of the Geese frontman’s historic December 2025 performance at Carnegie Hall, which was filmed and subsequently turned into a captivating concert film by Oscar-winning director Paul Thomas Anderson. A year later, Winter—and perhaps you—will now have a new best shirt to roll off.

PTA’s Cameron Winter at Carnegie Hall, which was shot on 35mm and 16mm film, will hit select theaters nationwide on Friday. As a reward to the Geeseheads and steadfast cinephiles who attend the 35mm screenings, distributor Janus Films produced a limited-edition run of merch (many such cases!) that can only be purchased IRL at select participating theaters. Which is to say, acquiring this merch…takes miles.

But if you’re looking at the Cameron Winter at Carnegie Hall T-shirts ($40) and baseball hats ($35) and thinking, “Wow, those look a lot like the maximalist, erudite wares from Boot Boyz Biz,” that’s because they were in fact designed by two of the Chicago brand’s current outposts, Now-Time and Mike’s Deli.

In an email, the Now-Time/Mike’s Deli team said that they drew inspiration from Carnegie Hall’s history and architecture. “We looked to the building itself as a visual archive to draw from, seeking forms that resonated with the performance across time,” they wrote. “Familiar architectural details took on a new heightened significance, knowing what was happening inside. In Carnegie Hall’s classic horseshoe layout we saw the camera’s panoramic intent. Outside on the 19th-century facade we found typographic elements that paralleled the energy of the event.” A very Winteresque explanation, in a way. Ideal for any enigmatic T-shirt enthusiast.

If you, too, would like to participate in the seeking forms and panoramic intent of Winter’s moving piano stylings on one winter’s night in Manhattan, you better start a-walking to the nearest 35mm-equipped cinema near you.

GQ staffers Jordan Bowman and Gerald Ortiz testing out the merchandise.

#6854. Noah Wrapped the Converse Chuck 70 in Menswear’s Favorite Tartan

When Converse and Noah collaborated for the first time earlier this year, the most surprising thing was that it’d taken so long for them to link up. The sneaker OGs and New York menswear mavens make as much sense together as peanut butter and jelly—both brands flow effortlessly between worlds, embraced by punks and preps and hoopers and skaters alike. Thankfully, the duo have been making up for lost time, kicking their partnership off with a couple of killer Chuck 70s in the spring before putting out an understated banger of a spin on the Louie Lopez 2 over the summer. Now, they’re capping off their banner year with one last go-round—another jazzed-up Chuck Taylor that’s about as holiday-ready as it gets.

Just in time for turkey season, Noah has wrapped the Converse Chuck 70 Low in one of the most beloved patterns in menswear: Black Watch tartan. The green, navy, and black plaid was originally worn by squadrons patrolling the Scottish Highlands in the 18th century, with its subdued tones meant to help wearers blend into their surroundings. Over the past several decades, it’s been embraced by fashion at large, but particularly Trad Ivy enthusiasts, who tend to sport the striking tartan on everything from sport coats and scarves during the colder months.

In addition to the sharp Black Watch canvas uppers, Noah made a handful of other subtle changes to the classic silhouette, adding its branding to the “license plate” on the shoe’s heel, removing the wing tongue stitch, and swapping in a woven tongue tag. The kicks feature the standard white laces but also come with a mustard yellow pair for an extra boost of color.

If you’re looking to dress festively but aren’t all that into freaky patterned sweaters, the latest Converse x Noah Chuck 70 Low might just be the ticket. The shoes drop tomorrow, November 19, for $112 via both Converse and Noah.

#6850. Why Men Are Posting Thirst Traps in Red Light Face Masks

Gonzalo Fernández Escribano knew all about the benefits of red-light therapy for his skin. Fewer wrinkles, a brighter complexion, minimizing acne—he’d experienced it all firsthand when he tried out LED masks during his facials. In March 2025, he purchased one for at-home use. Little did he know it would come with an extra benefit as a provocative prop for a sexy photo.

"I use my red-light mask right before bed, so I’m already shirtless,” says Escribano. “Let’s just say the sexy-looking picture is easily convenient to take.”

Escribano is one of many men online who’ve embraced the red-light thirst trap. More and more, image-conscious guys with followings built around some combination of beauty, fitness, wellness, travel, are pairing their red-light routines with the occasional shirtless selfie. The trend is especially prevalent in gay-male internet culture, where faceless torsos shots, sometimes featuring beefy chests and six-pack abs, have been part of the visual language of dating apps and social media for years. A shirtless red-light-mask pic is a variation on the theme: The body and the anonymity are still there—only now there’s a skincare routine attached.

Jayson Stacy, a celebrity groomer, noticed his friend wearing an LED in his Instagram profile picture three years ago. At the same time, Stacy had recently gotten a mask himself and decided to follow suit, posting a photo of his own after Mardi Gras under the guise of some much needed recovery.

“It’s almost like a sexy Halloween costume, but you don’t have to wait until October,” Stacy says. Escribano agrees, saying it makes him look like a superhero. Strong shadows, glowing skin, and a Sith-like red glow mix together in a thirsty trifecta meant to slow someone’s scrolling.

For Stacy, red-light masks strike the perfect balance between wellness and plausible deniability. “You’re supposed to use the mask after you wash your face, so most are fresh out of the shower before using,” he says. “It’s like, ‘Oh, look at my morning routine,’ which gives the perfect excuse to be mostly unclothed. Then you have the mask on, you feel like someone can’t see your face, so it can feel like a layer of protection.” In other words, the skin care routine becomes the reason for the thirst trap, even though it’s only half the motivation.

Red-light therapy is a noninvasive treatment that uses low-level LED light to stimulate cellular activity and boost collagen production. The technology has become especially prominent over the last few years thanks to its ability to reduce inflammation, soften fine lines, and promote skin and muscle recovery and hair growth. (That is, as long as you are willing to invest in high-quality equipment and use it every single day).

Celebrities have been documenting their LED routines for years, from January Jones to Kim Kardashian. But those posts were mostly framed around the masks’ intended purpose: improving your skin. Somewhere along the way, though, a certain breed of extremely online men—trainers, models, artists, content creators, and guys who simply know their angles—found a second purpose for the masks: as an accessory for a hot photo. The masks are turning up in bathroom mirror pics, bedroom shots, and buried several slides deep in otherwise unrelated photo dumps, often without any mention of skin care at all. And judging by the comments—fire emojis, heart eyes, and the occasional “yummy”—their followers seem to be noticing something other than the mask.

The thirst traps are also surfacing on social media at a time when curiosity around red-light therapy is booming. A 2025 study analyzing Google Trends data found that search interest across red-light-therapy-related terms increased 118% after February 2024. And by 2025, Google reported that searches for “red light therapy” had reached an all-time high. As these futuristic devices have rapidly flooded our feeds, maybe it was only a matter of time before they made their way out of the skin care selfie and into something a little thirstier.

In some ways these images are being ushered into well-trodden territory. In 2021, TikTok’s viral Silhouette Challenge had users transform themselves into barely distinguishable, body-forward silhouettes under a red filter, turning the color itself into a visual shorthand for sex appeal. Meanwhile, for gay men, shirtless photos are hardly a novelty, especially on dating apps like Grindr and Sniffies, where headless torsos are practically part of the experience. “The headless torsos definitely all blend together at this point,” says John Arthur Hill, who compares swiping through hookup apps to looking into “a lukewarm batch of Chili’s skillet queso” and trying to figure out what’s cheese, meat, or a bell pepper.

And even when the most impressive six-pack can get lost in an endless doom scroll, red light can add just enough intrigue to get someone to look twice. For Adam Pent, an OnlyFans creator who’s no stranger to posting thirst traps himself, that curiosity is part of the appeal. “It [a red-light-mask thirst trap] is definitely more interesting because I’d be curious about who’s behind the mask,” he says. To Pent, there’s something creative about the whole thing. “I guess it makes the photo a bit more artful too? ‘Art thot’ vibes I guess, unless it looks like sponsored content.” It also makes him want to shop. “I’m less focused on the body and more like, ‘Should I just get one already?’”

The red-light thirst trap also holds a certain mysterious appeal. “I’ve been posting ‘masked’ selfies for quite some time because I think it’s a really hot aesthetic and such a cool fantasy,” Michael Anthony, a celebrity makeup artist, says. For him, it’s not just about looking good in the photos. “The fantasy of anonymity and pleasure, while doing something for yourself that will make you look and feel better—it’s all so sexy.”

An LED face mask is a luxurious way to get an at-home glow, and Shark's is one of our favorites. It helps to fight acne and fine lines, and it can help depuff around the eyes for those long nights.

Tattoo artist Alvin Archibald sees a similar appeal. “There’s a subtle tension between vulnerability and control that makes it interesting,” he says. “The mask takes away your facial expressions, so the body becomes the focus. It creates this interesting dynamic where you’re both exposed and concealed at the same time. There’s something almost intimate about it, like you’re getting ready for an audience without necessarily admitting it.”

#6849. How 4 states voted on election-related ballot measures

Maryland voters fill out ballots at a polling site in Bowie on Tuesday. (Tom Williams/CQ Roll Call)

More than 100 statewide ballot measures went before voters this week, including a handful that could shape future elections in their states.

Here’s a look at how those measures fared, including one in Alaska that hasn’t been called as yet:

Voters in Maryland on Tuesday approved an amendment to the state’s constitution that will make it easier for the state’s Democrat-controlled legislature to redraw the congressional map for the 2028 elections.

The outcome was a victory for Democrats, who could potentially eliminate the state’s lone Republican-held seat – the 1st District on Maryland’s Eastern Shore, represented by Rep. Andy Harris, the chair of the hard-line conservative House Freedom Caucus.

The Old Line State emerged as a bright spot in Democrats’ plan to counter the redistricting wars initiated by President Donald Trump and turbocharged by the spring Supreme Court ruling that diluted Section 2 of the Voting Rights Act and set off a slew of Republican-led map-remaking across the South.

But those GOP-led efforts appeared to backfire this week, with Democrats holding many of the redrawn seats in states such as Texas, Florida, Alabama and North Carolina.

Still, Maryland Gov. Wes Moore told reporters Wednesday that he was “ready to engage” with the legislature on a new map, citing the threat of further redistricting in Republican states.

“Donald Trump is not done. He has already been very clear about his intention to go after additional states, to go after places like Mississippi and South Carolina and Indiana,” Moore said, according to video posted by WBAL-TV 11 News.

Following an unsuccessful attempt to redraw the Maryland map in February, Moore called a special August legislative session during which state lawmakers voted to put the redistricting amendment, known as Question 3, on the November ballot.

The move faced immediate legal challenges from Republicans. Two Maryland courts ruled to block the measure from appearing on the ballot, finding that lawmakers had missed the legally imposed deadline to refer the ballot measure. The state’s Supreme Court, though, allowed the redistricting amendment to go before the voters, with changes to the ballot text.

State and national Democrats campaigned heavily for Question 3 as some public polling pointed to skepticism among voters. Former President Barack Obama even appeared in an ad urging voters to approve it.

Voters in the Show-Me State overwhelmingly rejected a Republican-drawn congressional map that sought to dismantle a safely Democratic seat in the Kansas City area.

Missouri Republicans enacted the new lines in 2025, aiming to give them an advantage in seven of the state’s eight House districts, including the 5th District held by Democrat Emanuel Cleaver II.

A protracted legal and political tussle over the map ensued.

The group People Not Politicians Missouri started a citizen petition to put the adoption of the map to a statewide vote. While the petition effort was pending, the state held its August primaries under the new lines. But in September, the state Supreme Court ruled that the 2025 map could not be used in the general election because of the pending ballot measure and ordered state officials to hold November’s election under the previous boundaries, first enacted in 2022.

Attempts to challenge the ruling in federal court ultimately proved unsuccessful, with the U.S. Supreme Court issuing an order that reinstated the 2022 map.

Groups on both sides of the ballot measure, known as Proposition A, reportedly spent millions to persuade voters. But St. Louis Public Radio reported last week that while opponents of the 2025 map were actively campaigning against it, organized efforts in favor of Proposition A appeared “nearly nonexistent.”

Republican efforts to try again and enact a new congressional map for 2028 could be complicated by a constitutional amendment, approved by voters Tuesday, that curtails the power of state legislators to override the result of a voter-approved initiative.

Voters in Massachusetts opted this week not to join states such as California and Washington and adopt a nonpartisan primary system.

The ballot initiative, known as Question 3, would have replaced the state’s partisan primaries with an open primary under which all candidates would appear on the same ballot, with the top two vote-getters, regardless of party, advancing to the general election.

The outcome was a victory for the chorus of labor unions and progressive groups, including the Working Families Party, that opposed the measure. Holding so-called jungle primaries, the groups argued, could make it harder for candidates without financial resources or high name recognition to run for office.

A bipartisan coalition that included the NAACP of New England and Republican former Gov. Charlie Baker supported the measure, arguing that open primaries would give voters more choice and create more competitive elections in a state dominated by Democrats.

Alaskans weighed in this year for the second time as many cycles on a ballot measure to revert to partisan primaries and repeal the state's top-four ranked choice voting system.

With over 70 percent of the vote in, The Associated Press had yet to call the ballot initiative, known as Measure 2, which would also roll back certain campaign finance disclosure requirements. As of Thursday evening, the “no” side, which supports keeping the current election system, was narrowly ahead. The state has until Nov. 18 to finish counting outstanding ballots.

Under Alaska’s voting system, all candidates run on the same ballot during the primary. Then the top four candidates, regardless of party, advance to the general election, which is decided by ranked choice voting if no one takes a majority of the vote.

In 2024, Alaska voters narrowly voted to keep the current system in place.

National Republicans, though, have largely opposed ranked choice voting, including Trump, who has characterized Alaska’s voting system as a “scam.”

The state’s use of open primaries and the top-four ranked choice system has drawn fresh scrutiny this year.  Outside groups spent big sums trying to boost underdog candidates during the Senate primary in an effort to hurt the front-runners. And several candidates in the Senate, House and governor’s races dropped out before the deadline to finalize the general election ballot, again trying to prevent splitting votes with their party front-runners.

Then last week, the Senate race was upended by a New York Times investigation that revealed operatives aligned with the Senate campaign of Democratic former Rep. Mary Peltola helped recruit a candidate with a similar name to GOP incumbent Dan Sullivan in an attempt to stack the ballot with two Republicans with the same first and last names. The revelations have fueled an ongoing debate in the state about whether ranked choice voting encourages this kind of alleged campaign chicanery.

“We would not be having this conversation at all if we didn’t have a thing in place called ranked choice voting,” Mike Dunleavy, Alaska's outgoing Republican governor, told Fox News last week.

Dunleavy has ordered an investigation into the revelations, as has the Justice Department. The DOJ also sought to block the certification of the Senate result if the incumbent Sullivan loses. Last weekend, a federal court dismissed the lawsuit.

Alaska and Maine are the only two states that currently use ranked choice voting in regular federal elections, along with the District of Columbia.

Michael Macagnone contributed to this report.

#6847. The Best Holiday Palettes for Every Vibe (Including Your Own)

Minimalist, maximalist, or somewhere in between? Find your holiday makeup match at Ulta Beauty.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Are you a five-minute-face person, or does your beauty routine involve three eyeshadow brushes, a playlist, and absolutely no rushing? Ahead, discover the best holiday makeup palettes and gift sets at Ulta Beauty, whether you're treating yourself or shopping for someone on your nice list.

Sweep the pale pink matte shadow all over your lids, blend a little of the deeper brown into your eyelid crease for definition, and tap the champagne shade onto the center of the lid for a hint of sparkle. The secret to a glowing complexion is combining finishing powders, blushes, and highlighters that deliver a healthy sheen without piling on heavy layers. If your beauty goal is less disco ball, more just-got-back-from-a-tropical-vacation, the Hourglass Ambient Lighting Edit Unlocked Palette in Wish is right up your alley.

The limited-edition face palette contains six powders that work together to blur the appearance of imperfections and give skin a flattering, diffused finish. Unlike chunky glitter, the finely milled formulas reflect light evenly to make your complexion look naturally radiant. For a glowy face, the versatile finishing powder doubles as powder foundation. Sweep it across your face to start, then add blush for a pop of color, and concentrate highlighter on the high points of your cheeks for a little extra definition.

If you happen to be more of a "there's no such thing as too much sparkle" person, top the look off with Fenty Beauty Limited-Edition Midnight Diamonds Diamond Bomb All-Over Diamond Veil, which leaves a fairy-dusted effect on cheekbones, collarbones, and anywhere else you want to sparkle.

The ultimate gift for the beauty chameleon includes a mix of eyeshadows, mascaras, and lip colors to create entirely different moods. For the friend who changes her makeup as often as her outfit, Tarte You Know You Love Me Collector's Set has plenty to keep her entertained.

The collection includes two mini Amazonian Clay eyeshadow palettes—one with warm golden shades and the other with cool rosy neutrals—so she can go from bronzed and sultry to pretty in pink without buying another palette. She’ll also get two mascaras (Tartelette Tubing for length and Maneater for volume) plus two Maracuja Juicy lip products to give lips a glossy, plumped-up appearance.

Each eyeshadow palette comes with its own reusable wristlet clutch, giving you options when it comes to gifting. Split the set into two presents for your beauty-obsessed besties, or keep the whole collection for yourself. We won't tell.

#6845. APC Just Dropped a Pair of Incredibly Clean Casios

We’re in a great era for affordable fashion-collab watches. Timex is leading the way: its link-ups with Noah, Todd Snyder, and J. Crew are masterclasses in adding personality to classic designs without going OTT. But don’t count Casio out of the race. The Japanese giant has just followed up its 2024 collab with APC. The pair are perfectly matched: Casio watches are known for being beautifully functional and understated—adjectives that fit APC’s spartan aesthetic too.

The first collaboration between the two brands saw them rework the hallowed A1000 digital-display watch. That release stripped back a lot of the original’s markings and added the APC logo on the dial. This time around, it's the AQ-240 getting a makeover. The case is squarer than the rectangular A1000, and while there’s a narrow window for a digital display, the watch maintains the traditional analog format with hands and hour markers. Here, you get the best of both worlds. The watch is sold out on APC's website but still available through Casio, so act fast if you want one. The silver variant is $110, and the gold goes for $135.

What about the APC-ness of it all? That part is the opposite of loud: as with the previous collab, there isn’t much giving away the brand's participation. We’ve got the APC logo on the dial and the buckle, plus the brand’s guitar-and-dagger insignia engraved out of sight on the caseback. Casio already offers the regular A1000 in a bunch of different colorways, but these two new ones are made specifically for the APC collab. The release includes a silver case with a dark-green dial and another in gold on gold. Both of the pieces have a brushed sun-ray effect on the dial. The vibe is refined and stripped back; this is a fashion watch that almost feels anti-fashion.

Maybe the best way to think about it is as the reverse side of the coin to the excellent new G-Shock 2500 Series. Both come from Casio, and both are minimal and functional. But the G-Shocks are big and bulky, with a design that seems to tip its hat to Patek Philippe’s Nautilus. These new ones are slim and nearly dressy. Put the two new releases together, and you’ve got pretty much all bases covered. And if those aforementioned Timex collabs felt a bit too classicist for you, then the Casio-APC partnership takes things in a more modern direction. Far from just being a cash grab, the “fashion watch” is now one of the best routes into affordable, on-point horology.

#6842. Are You a Super Ager? This Competition Will Be the Judge of That

Alan Krepack stands with his hands on his hips and takes a deep breath as he sizes up the Over Under: an obstacle with a high wooden box and low wooden beam spread 10 meters apart. Music booms over the speakers and other competitors shuffle around the cavernous room of the Armory Track in Washington Heights, New York, but Krepack stays laser-focused, thanks to years of meditation practice.

As soon as the volunteer says “go” he sprints to the box, plants his hands, and hoists his feet up and over. As he lands on the other side, he sprints to the beam, dropping down into an army crawl. “Take your time, take your time,” his son yells from the sidelines. Krepack reaches the far end, rushes back, and repeats the sequence again before crossing the line in 51 seconds. To cool down, he paces back and forth with his hands on his head. “I think I’m a sprinter, I’m a jumper, but then it’s like, fuck, no, I’m 73 years old,” he says, catching his breath.

The challenge is just the first of eight trials Krepeck will tackle throughout the day alongside his sons, Harrison and Aaron, at the Super Age Games, a fitness and mental competition designed to assess abilities associated with healthy aging. On November 7th, the inaugural day-long event drew 530 athletes from 42 states and 15 countries, and about 70% of participants were between the ages of 40 and 64. Krepeck agreed to let me shadow him to understand this new event through the lens of someone who is, in fact, aging, and would like to do so gracefully.

The name of the Games comes from a buzzword that originated in the longevity world: super ager. The term was originally coined by Northwestern University researchers to describe adults over 80 whose memories rival those of people 30 years younger. These days it’s thrown around on podcasts and in books to describe anyone who defies both the typical trajectory of both cognitive and physical aging.

The inaugural Super Age Games drew 530 athletes from 42 states and 15 countries.

From crawling across a cargo net to reading microexpressions, the challenges in the competition measure longevity markers like functional strength, aerobic capacity, balance, and cognitive skill. At the end, athletes see where they rank among their age group and receive their “Super Age,” learning how their body’s physical fitness stacks up against the general U.S. population’s.

In an era where your average middle-aged midwestern dad is training for Hyrox, and a new, outlandishly extreme challenge is being invented every few months, the Super Age games are a low-key counterpoint to raw, stunty, athleticism, born from the metric-obsessed fitness subcultures who prize longevity above all. And now that fitness trackers like Whoop or Apple Watch can track VO2max, heart rate variability, and resting heart rates, that group continues to grow. Along the way, these stats have become status symbols on par with owning a Lamborghini. And now that overall health is quantifiable, fitness values have become less about just how fast you run or how much you lift, but how slowly aging.

It follows that, despite its name, Super Age Games isn’t just for aging athletes. The crowd there leaned middle-aged, and the youngest person at the competition was 21. As we stood by the dead hang station, Krepak pointed across the room at a brolic young man who looked like he could be an NFL prospect: “That dude’s a serious athlete,” he says, as we watched him run around the track cradling a sandbag on his shoulder.

Unlike competitions like Hyrox, where athletes race through a sequence of workouts against the clock, the Super Age Games allows competitors to rest as long as they need between trials. In fact, Junior Kennedy, the fitness program director for the Games, encourages it. “There's no rush,” he says. “The goal is to get through each event as intentionally as you can. We need to understand what it means to recover.”

Each trial was developed with a certain longevity marker in mind, and developed with input from researchers at the Buck Institute for Research on Aging, a leading nonprofit research center based in Novato, California dedicated to understanding aging. The Over Under, for example, tests agility by judging how fast you can change levels and switch directions. Meanwhile, the Synapse, a challenge where participants track moving balls on a screen and identify them after they've been shuffled around, is meant to measure cognitive processing.

Challenges in the competition measure longevity markers like functional strength, aerobic capacity, balance, and cognitive skill.

Krepack, a psychologist and lifelong athlete from Mill Valley, California, first heard about the event through its founder, David Stewart. Stewart is a media founder behind two niche longevity platforms, AGEIST and Super Age, where he writes about longevity, wellness, and redefining aging (one of his website testimonial’s says he has made aging “cool and aspirational.”). Krepack’s generally a skeptic of the profit-driven modern wellness industry—“I've always had a jaded view of all of that stuff,” he says—but he has followed both Stewart and the Buck Institutes’ work for years.

The competition appealed to Krepack, in part because he’s spent decades cultivating a consistent exercise routine he’s proud of. He played baseball in college and has spent much of his life skiing, cycling, and playing other sports. These days, his schedule revolves around a handful of rituals: bike rides on Mondays and Fridays, a Saturday hike with a friend, gardening, yoga, and Zen meditation most days. When he signed up for the Games in June, he altered his exercise routine, lifted more weights, practiced hanging from a bar, and experimented with the Neurotracker cognitive test online.

Yet all that training didn’t prepare Krepack for the biggest surprise of his day. After scoring well on both the Inferno (the assault bike VO2max test) and Synapse, next came the fourth trial: the “Tightrope,” a balance challenge that requires competitors to walk along a low beam while carrying a water-filled bag, taking as many consecutive steps as they can in two minutes without falling. He had expected to excel, thanks to years of skiing and yoga. Instead, he struggled to keep steady as the water shifted inside the bag. As his foot slipped off the bar, his eyes got wide. “I can’t believe my balance,” he says. “That’s my one disappointment in life now.”

Alan Krepack's sons Harrison and Aaron during the “tightrope” challenge.

Krepack was still hard on himself as we walked over to the dead hang trial. But after beating both his sons’ times, he seemed to get his competitive spirit back. He pushed through the remaining challenge determined to finish strong. And he did: By the end of the Games, he had placed second overall, with scores adjusted for age.

A few days after the competition, I checked in with Krepack to ask him how he’d processed the event. He responded with screenshots of his scores. His highest score came from the cargo net, which he credited to sheer determination rather than any particular finesse: “due to my will and overcoming an unconventional and desperate style!” he texted me, “Plus my sons were great support and focus partners.”

But the Games were also a reality check about aging: “I left there feeling both young and old,” he tells me on a phone call. As he watched his sons rocket through the obstacles, he was forced to confront how much of his athleticism he’d lost. “They just flew over it. They were so fluid, and so fast,” he says. “I’m not 30 or 40 or 50 or 60 anymore.”

Despite all the longevity industry's promises of maintaining youth, Krepack sees little value in denying the inevitable. Aging well, he says, is about “accepting, improving, and doing your best. It’s not a denial of who you are at this point in life.” The goal, he says, is to make the most of what you’re still capable of.

#6840. Pride and Prejudice’s Rufus Sewell Is the Most Dashing Man on Television

It’s possible that Rufus Sewell is having too much fun right now. Between playing morally ambiguous Vice President (and husband to the U.S. Ambassador to the Court of St. James’s) Hal Wyler in Netflix’s frothy, prestige-y series The Diplomat and the beloved but deeply flawed Mr. Bennet in the streamer’s upcoming adaptation of Pride and Prejudice, he’s cornered the market on lovable rascals. He thrives on “exercises in gray” and relishes “when it goes wrong” for a character. “It’s the opposite of what you want in life,” he tells me. “You want drama, you want mistakes.” These roles offer plenty of both.

Wyler, of The Diplomat, is navigating how to work with the increasingly autocratic and bloody-minded President Grace Penn (Allison Janney) thousands of miles away from his wife Kate (Keri Russell), who is starting to exhibit some of his own manipulative behaviors she claims to abhor. Two centuries prior, Mr. Bennet is reckoning not with geopolitical crises and egomaniacal heads of state, but with the challenges his wife faces in marrying off five eligible, if dowry-poor, daughters.

Sewell sat down for a long chat with GQ about the two series, the value of blending high silliness with high stakes in the same scene, and how bodily functions are an under-appreciated key to unlocking a character’s specificity.

Rufus Sewell: The interesting question for me, for the second half of the season, was how much is Hal truly aware of everything that Kate and everyone else are noticing and concerned about with Grace's behavior. How much does he see? How much is he choosing to pretend not to see in order to stay in the room? He's always believed that access is all, but how much do you compromise in order to stay in the room? Where’s the point when you realize you stayed in the room too long without saying anything, and the moment’s passed, and you are just an accomplice? I found it very difficult to play his seeming unctuousness and obsequiousness with Grace this season, and to not be trying to protect my character in the wrong way, because I felt he was failing.

He’s overestimated his ability to effect change with Grace. Hal was prepared to appear to be supporting her, when he believed in his ability to steer her in a better direction, and I think he goes past that point. When I read the final episode script and saw where he ends up walking out, it gave me the courage to really lean into how much he goes wrong, by his own standards earlier in the season. He begins to lose his way in a way that he never has done before. But I embraced it, knowing that the real gift of the part was getting to play him going wrong and then seemingly correcting himself. We actually shot a speech that he made, just to give us a sense of where the scene was headed and for me to know how to play it. They might end up using some of it in the next season, or they could end up completely changing what was written. Hal coming out of the summit is a momentous decision in terms of what that does for the future and I've got no idea what’s next—I can't wait to start filming season five to find out.

I was always shocked when this show was described as a thriller because I always thought it was a kind of romantic sex comedy with extremely high stakes, because what it reminded me of firstly was some of the comedy of divorce screwball films of the 1940s. The characters may be divorced after a very tempestuous relationship, but they’re interlinked in a way that can never be undone. There's a romance to all of that history.

The other thing that it reminded me of was the kind of films that were made in the '70s—French films where halfway through an argument one of the characters would sit down on the toilet, you know what I mean? The people inside these films remind you of your own relationships. Your long-term relationship might not go to the same extremes, it might not be in the same setting, but I think people are people. What I love about their relationship is their shared wonkiness, and the getting into the nitty-gritty of the politics and taking someone down and hatching a plot whilst fighting over pastries and getting crumbs on the bed—all of that is part of their foreplay.

That is what the show is about, as far as I'm concerned. These are people who wear suits that might stand at a lectern and be on TV, but the fact is, how do you scratch an itch? Do you smell? Do you need to go to the toilet? These are the things that the script leans into a lot, and it might put some people off, but I don't care about that. That's the thing that makes it feel real, and it's so refreshing to play.

He'll flirt with anyone. We all know people like this. It's not even necessarily sexual, he just engages with people on that level and it's a natural thing for him.

I would say Hal’s flirtatiousness being a problem is open for debate, because a lot of what we understand of him is via Kate’s descriptions of him and his behavior. I can and often do see it in a different way. Part of the story of seasons three and four is Kate’s realization that a lot of stuff she thought of as his personality was in fact the mantle of the job that he'd taken on, because we see her doing the same kinds of things now that she is an ambassador. They both have a lot of the same qualities. They're both killers in a sense, and it's been very convenient for her and great for the show for her to introduce the public to Hal through her descriptions. And yet, every time it seems like he's changed character, in fact, it is revealing something from a different perspective.

We learned pretty early on that we were going to be doing some very, very long scenes, and we were given the advantage of seeing the scenes very early so we could slowly gestate the performances. We needed that because Windsor Castle in the show was comprised of several different buildings and locations, some of them with oceans between them, so we’d be shooting some parts the next day, and other bits in four months’ time.

Especially with a two-hander, with such long takes, you can go in and out of being relaxed, unrelaxed, free, slightly bound, and actually you get to the point where you stop worrying about it. It's like being on stage, because you end up too tired to be bad all the time and end up inhabiting the moment and things just happen. I love that all of it was a surprise to us.

I think what is most important in this particular iteration of the relationship between Mr. and Mrs. Bennet is that he comes to realize which ways he's failed his daughters. When Mrs. Bennet says to him in this version, it's easy for you to say that I’m silly or frivolous, but you try being a woman at this age with no means—this is the fight of their lives that he is making these jokes about. And yes, of course he's funny and right in certain ways, but he's also guilty of negligence of his family for the sake of an easy life for himself. The reason they have no money is that so much money has been spent on bonnets. Money could have been saved, but in order to have a quiet life, he's ducked out of fights in order to avoid responsibility.

Exactly. It was a gift in this role to have that moment when he feels that, admits that, and gets to be slightly humbled in this idea of himself that he's been cultivating. He didn’t actively or consciously avoid having this revelation, but that’s the effect of staying in his study, and now he’s facing the consequences. To use very modern terms, he has a revelation of what his pattern has been. It made sense!

One could be forgiven for thinking that Mr. and Mrs. Bennet are two people that used to be attracted to each other and now have nothing in common and don't like each other. I think it's born out in the book, though, the idea that there is still a love there, and especially a shared humor between them, and a bit of a spark remains.

I love the fact that we get to get drunk together and stuff like that. Olivia Colman and I worked on The Father together a few years ago with Anthony Hopkins, which was a very different kind of gig, but as is often the case with things that are very heavy and intense, we had a hoot doing it. I loved working with her, and was very excited about the chance to work with her again. I love the scenes between Mr. Bennet and Mrs. Bennet. They were such a treat for us.

#6836. Foster bill would bolster tech use to monitor AI financial risks

"We cannot afford to rely on outdated systems to oversee an increasingly complex financial sector," said Rep. Bill Foster, D-Ill., (Bill Clark/CQ Roll Call file photo)

A leading Democrat on the House Financial Services Committee introduced legislation Thursday aimed at helping regulators use technology to better spot financial stress turbocharged by artificial intelligence and around-the-clock payment systems.

The draft bill, from Rep. Bill Foster, D-Ill., would require the Federal Reserve to work with other regulators to develop, test and publicly release model software that would improve the ability to assess market conditions in real time and respond quickly to cybersecurity and other threats.

The bill is designed to help regulators keep pace with new risks "driven largely by the development of state-of-the-art AI models," according to a summary.

The legislation is intended to help regulators provide financing to banks during crises and wind down failing banks. The model software would reduce the amount of manual compliance work that smaller financial institutions would have to do by providing standard data formats for regulatory reporting and tools to manage and transmit the information to regulators, according to a Foster spokesperson.

“With advances in AI creating new cybersecurity threats and financial risks, it's more important than ever that we modernize the way we monitor the health of our financial institutions,” Foster said in a statement. “We cannot afford to rely on outdated systems to oversee an increasingly complex financial sector.”

Foster, who has a Ph.D. in physics and has been a leader on AI issues on the Financial Services panel, is in line to become chair of the Financial Institutions Subcommittee next year.

He has been mulling a challenge to ranking member Maxine Waters, D-Calif., to chair the full committee in the new Congress. But Foster is now “leaning toward not running,” a person familiar with his thinking said.

Waters already has a confirmed challenger in Rep. Sean Casten, D-Ill., who on Thursday circulated a "Dear Colleague" letter seeking the support of House Democrats for his bid.

Casten touted his “career at the intersection of business, science and public policy." Before entering Congress, he worked in clean energy and co-founded a company that converted industrial waste energy into usable power.

In his letter, Casten cited the need to lower housing and insurance costs, protect consumers from fraud, address risks posed by cryptocurrency and prediction markets and guard against the next financial crisis. He also promised to hold the Trump administration accountable for ethical lapses.

He's got a tough road ahead of him, however. Every Financial Services Democrat except Casten, Foster and Rep. Sam Liccardo of California signed an Oct. 21 letter to House Democrats in support of Waters. That show of support came after Casten made it known he was interested and it was clear that Foster had been considering his own bid.

Although Foster may not compete for the chairmanship, he has recently talked to Waters about changing how the committee operates.

“He wants a variety of members to play a role in legislation brought up in committee,” the source familiar with his thinking said. He’s also seeking more input on witnesses who testify at subcommittee hearings.

The draft bill he released on Thursday covers a topic that Foster is likely to pursue throughout the 120th Congress. He often asks the most questions about the use of AI in financial services at hearings and offers the most amendments related to the issue at markups.

Under the bill, model software would be developed for banking records and operations, regulatory reporting, stress testing, anti-money laundering and fraud screening, AI risk monitoring and other activities.

The software must be open source, publicly available and tested for reliability and accuracy, according to the summary. It would be tested using synthetic or legally permitted historical data rather than actual customer accounts. Regulators would not take financial or supervisory actions during the study.

The software developed during the study would not have to be adopted; rather, it “should be viewed as a starting point for further modernization,” the summary says.

All software models would have to be published within one year of enactment of the bill and a final report assessing feasibility, costs and savings, competition and other criteria, would be due to Congress within two years.

#6835. Highly Specific Gifts for the Pickiest Menswear Guy on Your List

Maybe it’s the precise weight of his selvedge jeans, the flat-felled seams of his wool overshirt, or the exact province in Japan responsible for his bespoke glasses. It doesn’t matter who he is or how you know him—brother, bestie, boss—he’ll probably tell you about it at length, entirely unprompted. (He means well, really.) That discernment is kind of endearing under most circumstances, but it makes buying him the right gift an absolute frickin’ nightmare.
