#6831. How to Grow a Beard

So you want to learn how to grow a beard. First off, you should know that it helps to have the right genetics. Second, you have to stay committed to the endeavor. Too often, beard-growing attempts end before they’ve even had a chance to establish itself.

“I’d say give it at least three to four weeks of solid growth before you even think about touching it with clippers,” Victor Mendoza, co-founder of beard care brand Viking Revolution, says. “Your beard needs time to actually show you what it’s going to look like, and that growth period lets you see the natural direction everything’s growing in. Plus, some hairs take longer to come in than others, so patience is key.”

There’s a sweet spot between letting your facial hair grow out and knowing when it’s time to take the trimmers for the best results possible, even if you can’t go full lumberjack. These are the best tips to maximize your hair follicles’ potential whether this is your first beard or your fiftieth.

There are many different beard styles to choose from; you don’t need to stick to neatly manicured, uniform look. Let your growth be influenced by the style you want to achieve.

“Your genes determine hair thickness, growth rate, density, and color,” Mendoza says. “If your dad or granddad had a thin beard, you’re probably dealing with the same hand, and that’s just life.”

If your beard is patchy, or if it has a unique growth pattern (sparse on the cheeks, full on the goatee, for example), then you should set your expectations properly. This goes for beards big, small, and medium alike. And while nutrition is important for hair growth, you can’t cheat genetics with your diet.

Patience is a virtue. Not only can a little time help fill in some patchiness, but it’s also requisite for almost any style. A beard transforms into something new every week. So, if it’s seeming like you’ll never reach your goal—in particular if it’s a long beard—then you honestly might wake up one morning and realize that you’ve arrived at the destination.

Mendoza says to wait at least two weeks before you start to do a cleanup, but only around the ends, i.e. the hairs that aren’t within the perimeter in which you want your beard to grow. That’ll help you figure out your beard’s natural shape.

One of the tenets of beard maintenance is to keep it defined. In short: shave the neckline and clean up the cheek lines. These keep it more beard-like and less neckbeard-like—unless, for some reason, a wooly stoner beard is your aim. In that case, let it grow wild and free.

The second way to keep things looking intentional is to sculpt and trim your beard as it grows. It might feel counterproductive—and counter to #2—but it’s the same way you need to get haircuts while growing out your hair. As Mendoza says, this helps direct the hair exactly where it needs to be: Fuller in some spots, less so in others. The styling products in the next tip can of course help influence things, but you foremost want to keep hairs in check as needed: This can mean spot-checking strays, snipping away split ends, or simply taking out some bulk. You’ll want a beard trimmer or a sharp pair of scissors.

There are a handful of beard products you should consider picking up if you’re serious about proper care and maintenance. A few drops of beard oil will absorb quickly and give your hair the right nutrients (plus a pinch of shine), while also making it less wild and frizzy. You can also swap in a beard balm or cream, something slightly heftier than an oil, to help style the beard and keep it in place all day. What’s important is that you clean your beard every day with an actual beard wash and not just a regular shampoo, which Mendoza says will dry out your beard and your face.

Weekly maintenance will consist of a deep condition, which you can do in the shower. “If you’re doing those things consistently, your beard is going to look way healthier and fuller than most guys out there,” he says.

Beard brushes help exfoliate the skin beneath the beard, for starters, which lifts dead skin cells and prevents future flaking. Moreover, brushing your beard at the end of each day will ensure its health and tame-ability in the long haul. This distributes the natural oils produced in the skin, which otherwise collect at the base of the shaft and only nourish the bottom of each hair. This oil distribution prevents breakage, frizzing, and split ends. (This is a great time to apply a few drops of beard oil as a booster, especially after a shower.)

Once your base has grown out—this can take months—it’s time to really make it yours. One of our favorites is to snip the bridge between the mustache and the chin, thus marooning the mustache. Or, pare back the beard itself, leaving a fuller mustache. You have lots of options, and it’s your privilege as a beard grower to flaunt it.

#6828. How to Play GTA VI All Weekend Without Destroying Your Body

The day has finally arrived. Grand Theft Auto VI is here, and you’ve taken that “sick day” to cause chaos on the streets of Vice City. After more than 10 years of waiting, you’re finally back in one of the franchise’s most classic locations, this time with an open world to explore. It’s not hyperbole to call this the most highly anticipated game in history. With more than 17 million preorders, that’s a lot of people who will be running from the police while trying to avoid (or rack up) six stars.

If you’re one of the many fans who’ve cleared out their schedule to binge the game this weekend, you’ll want to make sure you emerge from your haze with extremities that still function.

“The metaphor we like to share with pro players or casual gamers is the concept of the health bar,” says Dr. Matthew Hwu, owner of physical therapy clinic 1 Health Point in Monrovia, California. “The muscles and tendons of your wrist, hand, thumb, as you move the analog stick, only have so much capacity or ‘max HP’ that they can handle throughout the day.”

Below, we spoke with Dr. Hwu and other veteran gamers about how to keep your “HP” bar full for your whole weekend without developing carpal tunnel.

Playing a game for long hours puts the soft tissues in your hands and arms into overdrive. When you ramp things up, you’ll feel sore and fatigued. The most common version of this is “tendon-based problems of the wrist and hand,” says Dr. Hwu, who has spent more than 11 years working with e-sports players to help them recover from injuries. As GTA VI will be available on PlayStation 5 and Xbox Series S and X, most people will be using a controller, which puts the thumbs under a lot of stress as people “control the analog stick to flip left, up, right, down; press A, B, X, Y.” The most common gaming injuries are gamer’s thumb and tennis elbow, according to the Cleveland Clinic.

So, don’t forget to take breaks. Dr. Hwu recommends at least a five-minute break for every 90 minutes played, though younger players are more resilient. If you want to push it, the key is taking longer breaks afterwards. “Two hours, 10-minute break, three hours, 15-minute break,” Dr. Hwu says. “If you want to be degenerate, you can be a smart degen.” You can also be strategic about scheduling more downtime after sequences with higher APM (actions per minute), that are more intensive on hand muscles. In essence, go touch grass after a high-stakes bank robbery.

That said, after more than a decade of waiting for what our reviewer described as “the most technically and artistically impressive game world ever made,” your instinct may be to lock in. So it’s wise to plan for breaks ahead of time. Set alarms on your phone or watch. If one goes off in the middle of a mission, commit to taking a break after crashing that helicopter. Make sure you eat and stay hydrated, and move your body, whether that’s a neighborhood walk or just a short twirl around your apartment before your next mission.

“It’s basically just your muscle and tendon tissue recovery, it’ll restore a little bit of HP over time,” Dr. Hwu says. “When you go out and walk outdoors, that can help with your overall circulation, nervous system as well. So you don’t feel like that meme where you come out of the cave and you’re like a ghost.”

Just like in other forms of training, stretching is a key part of your self-care arsenal for long hours of gaming. To loosen up for a long stretch of car-hijacking, do a few stretches before turning on your console, and during cut scenes. Your body will tell you what areas feel tight and could use some attention. But don’t forget those thumb muscles. “Bring your thumb away from the next finger, stretching it down,” Dr. Hwu says. “Every time you’re flicking back, left, or right, you might be using these muscles.”

You’ll also want to stretch your index fingers after long hours of beating ass with a crowbar while riding your motorcycle. “Every time you press your trigger,” Dr. Hwu says, “you’re using the extensors, the top side of your fingers, but also the flexors to push down.” You can also work on relaxing your forearm muscles, by extending your arm out and pushing your palm downward.

After 13 years, and a budget rumored to rival that of Dubai’s Burj Khalifa skyscraper, the new Grand Theft Auto is finally here. Our critic's verdict? It's a tour de force that reimagines what a video game can be.

Don’t forget your neck and upper back, which can feel stiff from holding onto stress after high speed chases and parachute combat. It’s so easy to load tension onto those upper traps as you shrimp further into your recliner while bird watching, or scoring a pair of black half-rim sunglasses and Eris sneakers (definitely not Nikes) for Jason (no judgment here).

“I will do small things: roll my neck from side to side, stretch my traps by sitting on one hand and pulling my head [in the] opposite direction,” Calliope Ryder, platform director at GYLD, previously a producer on Tales of the Shire, says. “[I] tense my calves in spurts to help with blood flow, or stand up and let my body fold at the hips while swaying side to side with my arms crossed. Again, alerts to remind me to do it are necessary or I'll go hours without noticing.”

Meanwhile, using a heating pad or an ice pack on a sore area (like your lower back) can help with any nagging dull pain you’re feeling. Heat loosens up stiff muscles, while ice can help dull the achy sensation. They won’t build your long-term strength, but they’ll make you feel better in the moment.

If you’re old enough for back pain, then you’re old enough to have to consider video game ergonomics. Wherever you’re gaming, Dr. Hwu’s recommendation is simple: Sit with your legs at a 90-degree angle, feet planted on the ground, back appropriately supported. Then put a pillow on your lap and rest your arms on it. This helps prevent forearm strain and hunching over. You can even haul your expensive Aeron from your office into the living room, rather than rotting on the sofa and ruining your back.

There are also a number of helpful gaming peripherals. Maddy Myers, editor in chief and co-founder of video game publication Mothership, uses a specific gaming pillow from a brand called Mechanism. “The pillow allows you to have the handheld device suspended in the air in a comfortable, adjustable spot so that you don’t tire out your arms from holding it up,” she says. “You can also put a controller on there.”

Using the device helps her get through play stretches of eight hours a day on the weekends, “while taking breaks to eat, do chores, stretch.” It’s been especially invaluable during high-intensity work periods. “I have to play GTA 6 for my job, so I am really going to be relying on these tools to keep my arms and body feeling as close to normal as possible,” she says.

Don’t let a terribly designed controller be the reason you develop carpal tunnel. Julia Lee, section lead for video game guides at Polygon, plays at least two hours of games a day, and will sometimes fit in 10-hour sessions. Her wrists started hurting and “those flat [Nintendo Switch] Joy-Cons were directly to blame,” she says. “I ended up getting a Nintendo Switch Pro Controller (which has a much more ergonomic shape and grip), and that helped alleviate pain a lot.” She also reminds herself not to hold the controller so tight, as it causes pain.

As Dr. Hwu explains, some controllers have staggered sticks, while others have an inline design. It all comes down to individual comfort and muscular endurance. He’s helped pro Call of Duty players modify their analog sticks, and has coached people to avoid death-gripping the controller. “If you use a claw grip position, an awkward grip, or a modded controller, that might lead to more stress for certain muscles in your wrist and hand,” he says.

If your hands are really killing you, there’s also the option of switching to another controller style altogether. “Moving two to four of my most awkward hand-based actions from my hands to my feet allows me to play longer,” John Warren, chief marketing officer and cofounder of Glydr, a foot-based video game controller, says. Warren has minicore disease, which causes muscle weakness. “Anytime you have an opportunity to stop overloading your hands while you play, you give your hands a little reprieve,” he says, “it makes your high-precision hand actions more accurate.”

Lastly, if you have existing injuries, and your medical provider has prescribed a brace, actually follow their guidance for how and when to use it. For my part, I use thumb braces called the Push Metagrips, to help support my floppy thumb joints anytime I plan on playing video games for long stretches.

So you’ve done all this work to binge GTA 6, and you’re feeling solid but a little sore. But you want to play for longer periods, snipe with greater accuracy, and fuck around with less fatigue. Consider going to the gym.

Doing exercises for those small muscles and tendons can help you build up that tolerance, like any other form of weight and endurance training. “When you rest, ice, massage, kinesio tape, you know, passive interventions, bracing, all of these things can restore your HP,” Dr. Hwu says. “It does nothing to change the size of your health bar, which represents the tissue’s capacity or your endurance. The way we do that is you can do specific exercises to build up or create a buffer for how much you’re going to handle.”

For any chronic pain, injuries, or conditions, it’s important to work with a physical therapist. I’m currently working with one for repetitive stress injury recovery, and doing exercises specific to playing video games. I do slow wrist curls with a two-pound weight, rotator cuff exercises with a resistance band, and squeeze an egg-shaped stress ball for thumb and forearm endurance. I also train my shoulders and core.

For those who aren’t managing any injuries, training those finger and arm muscles make it easier to game for longer periods. Consistency is key, and so is having reasonable expectations. Dr. Hwu recommends starting once a day, three to four times a week, “on a day where you’re not doing a lot, and then you’ll learn how your body responds to doing exercises within the context of your specific life, and you can make modifications to scale up the aggressiveness or scale down,” he says. As for seeing progress, “it takes four to six weeks for muscles, eight weeks for tendons.”

And don’t forget the stabilizer muscles in your shoulders, which hold up your upper body and support your arms. “I focus on ‘chest opening’ exercises that strengthen your back,” Ryder says. “I tend towards curving forward so I consciously strengthen the 'opposite' muscles so they pull my shoulders back a bit.” She also does chin tucks, to strengthen her deep neck flexors.

Strength training is also a great way to tackle back pain, another common complaint after gaming for hours. “A few years ago I started having extreme back pain when I sat in my office chair,” Gita Jackson, cofounder of video game publication Aftermath, says. They play one to two hours of games a day, but often play marathon sessions when new games come out. “The pain was excruciating, and sometimes my legs would get so stiff I couldn’t walk. Recovery started with physical therapy, and then a new ergonomic chair,” and now they regularly attend pilates.

“I really had no idea that if I wanted to continue playing noodley strategy games without pain that I would have to be a person who works out multiple times a week,” they say.

Maybe you’ve done a little too much, and your arms start to feel heavy as bricks, with thumbs that ache for hours. Even with the best precautions in place, it can happen to any of us.

“If it gets to a point where cumulatively, over days, symptoms are getting worse, maybe reflect and identify what you could change,” Dr. Hwu says. “If things are consistently sharp, or you start to feel some weakness, where when you go and try to pick up something, you’ll want to drop it. That’s a sign that it may be time to make a change in some of the variables that you can control.”

In 2002, weeks before the release of Grand Theft Auto: Vice City, the journalist David Kushner met Sam and Dan Houser, plus Rockstar co-founder Terry Donovan, at their SoHo loft. It would be their last and only in-depth sit-down before they decided that the world’s most successful video game required little explanation.

This is when paying attention to your daily habits is key. Instead of freaking out over hand pain, and deciding you need to quit playing cold turkey, you can instead think about what you did during the day that might have contributed to your discomfort. “How much you’re playing, what intensity you’re playing, are you taking breaks, are you exercising, how are you responding to the pain?” Dr. Hwu says. These are all factors in why you might be having pain, and all things to consider modifying. If it truly is tendon fatigue, then the key is finding the right volume of use, rather than quitting altogether.

There are also some signs to look out for that may require medical intervention. Dr. Hwu pointed me to 1 Health Point’s website, which suggests seeking a doctor’s opinion if your wrist looks swollen or bruised, if you can’t move or bear any weight, if your symptoms worsen or spread with rest, or if you experience constant hand numbness, especially if it comes with weakness.

Gaming for long hours ultimately comes down to using your common sense alongside your precious free will. Pay attention to your habits. Commit to taking breaks and stretching between bank robberies and doomscrolling through Snapmatic (in-game social media). Do some preventative exercises while planning out your next mission—with a soundtrack that includes Bad Bunny and Dua Lipa, as well as some ’80s bangers, you’ve already got the music for it. Your hands and arms will thank you, even if the regular people of Leonida probably won’t.

What Is HDL Cholesterol and How Do You Raise It?

How to Catch Up on Sleep the Right Way, According to Experts

How You'll Use It: If this was your Member Choice, save this one for when you want to make an entrance. Think date nights, work parties, or any occasion that calls for making an impression. This is definitely a scent that enters the room first and leaves after you do.

How You'll Use It: The formula melts into your skin, staying close to the body, which makes it perfect for tapping on directly after a shower. The sleek, pocket-sized container easily slips into your pocket or gym bag for quick reapplication throughout the day.

Why You'll Love It: Paris, 1899. Champagne is flowing, the cafés are full of artists and writers, and every night seems to hold a little intrigue. This fragrance captures that moment. #6824. Longtime West Virginia Sen. Rockefeller dies

Sen. John D. Rockefeller IV, D-W.Va., questions witnesses during a Senate Finance hearing on health care on June 23, 2011. (Scott J. Ferrell/CQ Roll Call)

Former Sen. Jay Rockefeller, a five-term West Virginia Democrat and champion for the rural poor, has died at the age of 89.

Rockefeller, who retired after the 2014 elections, played key roles on the tax-writing Senate Finance Committee, where he helped write landmark children’s health insurance legislation, as well as the Commerce, Science and Transportation and Intelligence panels which he chaired.

A staunchly progressive lawmaker, he represented a deeply conservative state that turned further to the right in his later years. Rockefeller, scion of a famous New York family renowned for its wealth, never wavered in his convictions and retired rather than face a potential backlash from voters upset by the then-Obama administration’s attempts to rein in the coal industry.

Sen. Shelley Moore Capito, R-W.Va., who challenged Rockefeller for his seat before he decided not to seek reelection, released a statement Friday praising her predecessor’s legacy.

“Jay dedicated his life to serving West Virginia and our nation, leaving a lasting legacy. He was always a true gentleman who treated me with respect, kindness, and encouragement,” Capito said. “Over the years, we developed a wonderful friendship that transcended politics, and I will always be grateful for that.”

West Virginia’s Republican Gov. Patrick Morrisey — himself a transplanted Northerner, with roots in Brooklyn and New Jersey — lauded Rockefeller’s record on health care and aid for coal miners and veterans in their adopted state.

“His work helped shape our state and left a lasting mark on the lives of generations of West Virginians,” Morrisey said.

Rockefeller was born in New York City on June 18, 1937, the great-grandson of Standard Oil Co. founder John D. Rockefeller, who was once considered the world’s richest man. Rockefeller attended Harvard University, graduating with an A.B. in 1961. His passion was for the Far East and he spent three years studying at the International Christian University in Tokyo.

He returned to the states and was appointed by President John F. Kennedy to the National Advisory Council of the Peace Corps in 1961, spearheaded the Peace Corps initial program in the Philippines. He worked for a brief time in what was then known as U.S. Department of Far Eastern Affairs before deciding to embark on a public service role of a more personal nature. He enrolling as a VISTA volunteer, in the program which would later be known as AmeriCorps, and moved to the small mining community of Emmons, W.Va., in 1964 at age 27.

In West Virginia, Rockefeller found his life’s work. He described the experience as being “reborn,” and spoke often the poverty and hardship he encountered living in the small town. “I could not leave,” he said of West Virginia. “I [had] become so devoted to those people and the unfairness."

He had planned to remain only one year, but decided to stay and became active in local politics. He broke from his family's long tradition of Republican loyalty and ran successfully for the House of Delegates as a Democrat in 1966. Two years later, he was elected secretary of state.

Rockefeller lost a bid for governor in 1972, but he became the president of West Virginia Wesleyan College and continued to place roots in the state. He married Sharon Percy Rockefeller in 1967 and they started their family. When he ran for governor again in 1976, he won, and served two terms before winning election to the U.S. Senate in 1984, ultimately serving five terms before retiring in 2014.

Rockefeller was determined to increase economic opportunities for West Virginia, for whom he felt was forgotten and abandoned by the rest of the country. He called the people of his state “the hardest-working people I know,” and believed that bringing more business opportunities would help lift the state out of severe rural poverty and the dependence on the coal industry.

Rockefeller traveled to Japan and worked tirelessly to bring a Toyota manufacturing plant to Buffalo, W.Va, one of over twenty Japanese companies he’d brought to West Virginia. He fought for fair trade policies and targeted tax relief to protect West Virginia industries such as steel and chemicals.

Coal mining had long been the economic backbone of West Virginia, but Rockefeller felt more must be done to protect miners, many of whom worked in unsafe conditions or fell ill with pneumoconiosis, or black lung disease, after years of inhaling coal dust.

But defying the coal industry came with a political cost. When Rockefeller originally ran for governor in 1972, he had opposed mountaintop mining, also called strip mining, or the practice of removing large amounts of earth and rock in order to access underground seams of coal. When he ran again in 1976, he reversed his position and won.

But even as he ascended to the Senate, Rockefeller remained both friend and foe to the coal industry.

Despite growing up on the Upper East Side of Manhattan, figuratively and literally hundreds of miles away from coal mines, Rockefeller understood its deep roots in West Virginia’s economy and culture. He believed it was the miners — rather than industry — for whom he was defending.

He hung a century-old photo of a coal miner in his Senate office in the Hart building, and would point to the photo and remark to visitors that “this is why I’m here and who I work for.”

Rockefeller fought to secure pensions for miners, especially as coal companies would file for bankruptcy and immediately restructure to do away with pension plans.

In 1990, Rockefeller was one of only 11 senators to vote against the bipartisan Clean Air Act, which he felt disadvantaged workers in West Virginia. But those close to him said the decision weighed heavily on him, and Rockefeller privately worried that the lack of such regulations would pollute the air and destroy waterways, the brunt of which would be born by the same disadvantaged people whom he vowed to protect.

He believed strongly in the deliberative aspects of the legislative process, consuming great amounts of reading material to familiarize himself with the issues. During a time of increasing partisan rancor, Rockefeller still reached across the aisle to find a willing partner, even when there was not one to be found.

In 2010, as the Environmental Protection Agency’s greenhouse gas regulations were coming to fruition, Rockefeller came up with a compromise to delay implementation for two years.

He viewed himself as a broker between industry interests and the hard working people from coal-states like West Virginia. He had hoped that his years of standing up for coal miners and West Virginia interests would foster enough goodwill among industries primed to oppose any compromise that was less than an all-out rejection of the existing EPA statutes.

But industry walked away from Rockefeller, preferring a hard-line opposition to EPA regulations, and lobbied other senators to vote against Rockefeller’s measure. Furious, Rockefeller took to the floor of the Senate to denounce the coal industry and its leaders, an unprecedented action for a popular coal-state senator.

“The reality is that many who run the coal industry today would rather attack false enemies and deny real problems then find solutions,” he said on the Senate floor in June 2012. “Scare tactics are a cynical waste of time, money and, worst of all, coal miners’ hopes. But sadly, these coal operators have closed themselves off from any other opposing voices and few dared to speak out for change — even though it’s been staring them in the face for years.”

His fury against the coal industry was heard loudly, many speculating that he would retire rather than face reelection.

He had won reelection to the Senate handily during his five campaigns. In 2008, his final election, he was reelected with nearly 64 percent of the vote. But six months following his fated coal speech, in January 2013, Rockefeller announced he would retire at the end of his fifth term.

Rockefeller’s legacy may best be known for his fight to expand health care for vulnerable populations, including children, the economically disadvantaged and the elderly. Rockefeller served as one of the chief congressional champions for President Bill Clinton’s proposed health care overhaul, and later he played a critical role and was a loud progressive voice in writing and passing the Affordable Care Act in the Senate.

Rockefeller believed strongly in a public health insurance option, offering it as an amendment in the Senate Finance Committee during the ACA markup. A public-run insurance plan, he said, would put patients before profits, and help those people, especially West Virginians, with high health care costs and poor coverage who were left “out in the cold.”

"These are people," Rockefeller said, visibly emotional during his closing arguments to the Senate Finance Committee. "Eleven-year-old kids. These are families, and we have to respect them. And you respect them by giving them a choice."

The amendment was rejected. Rockefeller pivoted to strengthening Medicaid and Children’s Health Insurance Program provisions, believing that poor and disadvantaged families could seek refuge within those programs. CHIP had been one of Rockefeller’s signature legislative victories, helping to write that program initially in 1997 and later working on a major expansion that became law in 2009.

Despite his initial concerns and later disappointments — at one time saying publicly he could not support the measure as it stood — he later added his support to the ACA, citing it as a win for all Americans and actively lobbied his colleagues to support it.

Rockefeller was also known for his role on the Senate Intelligence Committee. A former chairman, he was critical of the nation's mismanagement of the Iraq war and clashed frequently with Republicans over the treatment of detainees. After he retired from Congress, Rockefeller served as a distinguished fellow at the Council on Foreign Relations.

Respect for Rockefeller ran deep on Capitol Hill. He was beloved by staff and known as a thoughtful, deliberative lawmaker. He had a fondness for classical music and served as a board member of the Bach Consort, an organization in Washington devoted to the study and performance of the music of Bach and his contemporaries. He admired art and Asian culture, and served as a board member of the Asia Society.

Rockefeller is survived by his wife, Sharon Percy Rockefeller; four children: John D. Rockefeller V, Valerie Rockefeller, Charles Rockefeller and Justin Rockefeller; and nine grandchildren.

Disclosure: the author served as a staffer in Rockefeller’s Senate office from 2009-11.

#6821. We Found The 6 Strongest Drinks At Starbucks, Ranked By Caffeine

Each of these bevvies delivers a serious buzz.

I would love nothing more than to be the type of person who jumps out of bed with the sunrise—without the lure of a steaming hot and very caffeinated cup of coffee waiting for me. But I need to smell the freshly brewed espresso before I can muster the strength to get out from under the duvet.

Even as decaf this and half-caf that have become all the rage in our high-cortisol society, you can pry the double cappuccino out of my cold, dead hands! Sometimes you just need a 350-milligram latte to keep you chugging through the day (unless your doctor says otherwise, in which case, ignore me).

In the spirit of staying fueled, I analyzed the Starbucks menu and narrowed it down to the most caffeinated bevvies you can buy in a grande size. However, everything is customizable and may differ slightly in caffeine content—you can always ask for more or fewer espresso shots, or opt for a larger size.

There’s a reason you might feel extra jittery after a morning Cold Brew. It’s packed with caffeine because it uses almost twice as many coffee grounds as regular brewed coffee. It is diluted with water, though, to temper the buzz.

A Caffè Americano—hot or iced—packs a similar punch as a Cold Brew. It’s not all that different, TBH, except instead of brewed coffee, it combines water and espresso to give it a littleeeee more edge.

If you love a sweet latte but want more caffeine, the Iced Brown Sugar Shaken Espresso is your ticket at roughly 255 milligrams for a grande.

If you’re craving a Cold Brew with a kick, go Nitro. It uses a higher coffee-to-water ratio in the cold steeping and dispensing process, and it is served without ice (so, again, less dilution). The result: bold flavor and about 280 milligrams of caffeine.

There’s a reason I’m a drip-coffee loyalist, and it’s not just the flavor. It’s also about the 310 milligrams of caffeine that a grande Pike Place delivers, which earns the medium roast coffee a solid second spot on this list.

Drumroll, please! The most caffeinated beverage on Starbucks’ menu is the Blonde Roast Brewed Coffee at 360 milligrams. Why does it differ from the medium and even the dark roast? It’s pretty simple: Blonde usually tests higher because Starbucks brews it with a little more coffee, and lighter roasts can pack more grounds into the scoop. That means more caffeine makes its way into your cup.

#6820. Charles Melton Is Still the King of Gigantic Pants

Charles Melton has long loved big, baggy pants. Back in 2024, the actor landed on GQ’s annual list of best pants thanks to one extremely voluminous pair from the Japanese label Sillage. And if his fits this fall are any indication, Melton won’t be stepping away from the assertively wide bottoms anytime soon. In fact, he keeps wearing one fantastically huge pair after another.

The Her Private Hell star’s latest giant-trouser run kicked off in mid-October at the star-studded Academy Gala in Los Angeles. While A-list dressers like A$AP Rocky and Jacob Elordi showed up in respectfully roomy pants, Melton upped the ante in an ERL tuxedo that left no doubt about his dedication to the slouch. His cropped-front tailcoat was neat as a pin and fit like a glove, but his slacks were dramatically roomy and artfully rumpled—parachute pants gone black tie.

Later that same month, he popped up in Texas for the F1 Grand Prix in a very Fashion Dude outfit: Louis Vuitton workwear jacket, sporty sunglasses, bucket hat, and some choice Wales Bonner x Adidas Sambas. And on his legs? A twisted-seam silhouette billowy enough to make a scrum of downtown stylists and creative directors blush. ​

But Melton saved his biggest look of all for last week in New York. At a VIP dinner for the South Korean eyewear label Gentle Monster, he donned a matching set from Viviano, the Tokyo-based label from designer Viviano Sue. Cut from gabardine as dark as a moonless night, both his Harrington jacket and balloon trousers looked as though they’d been blown up to the size of a weather balloon and then deflated, leaving behind all the crinkles and creases. He contrasted the dramatic ensemble with a pair of tiny oval sunglasses, which capped the whole thing off just right.

Even as menswear’s ideal pant fit moves towards a relaxed straight leg, some guys who dove deep into the baggy waters aren’t quite ready to give their roomy fits up just yet. Melton clearly loves the look and wears it well. And when your gigantic trousers help you stand out in a crowd as effortlessly and stylishly as his do, why bother changing?

#6818. Plestia Alaqad Sees the World

The photojournalist talks about the making of her latest book, The Eyes of Gaza

“The hardest part was having something as intimate and personal as a diary become public,” says Plestia Alaqad of her decision to publish her diary-memoir, The Eyes of Gaza. The book details the harrowing first weeks after October 7 in Gaza as a young Palestinian journalist. Alaqad studied new media and journalism at Eastern Mediterranean University in Cyprus before returning to her home in 2022. Intending to educate the world about her city, she did not know she would end up reporting the first stages of the genocide.

Alaqad’s reporting situated herself as a voice for the ordinary. In the first few days, she spoke to her neighbors and local children. Her reporting appeared mostly on her Instagram account, which grew to a little over 2 million followers, with many of her images republished by the BBC, The New York Times, PBS NewsHour, and The Washington Post. Even through the chaos and the expulsion of over 100,000 Gazans in the first few days, according to UNHCR, which Alaqad also recounts in her memoir, there are moments of tenderness caught by her and everyday Gazans she would meet and talk to. Her style of reporting is grounded in a sense of familiarity, narrating the everyday in extraordinary circumstances. She made connections with people in a way that makes you feel like you were alongside her, drinking tea with the local tetas and jiddos, watching the violence and the world collapse around you.

Alaqad left Gaza for Lebanon, where she is completing her master’s degree. During the course of our interview, she spoke openly with me about the loneliness of the isolation of being a witness, forever changed by the devastation.

One of my favorite things about Gaza is the people and the sense of community. For example, the relationship between my neighbors and I. I’ve been living in the Al -Waleed Building since I was almost four years old, and I know all my neighbors. Like Dana, who lived next door next to me—it was like having a sister and best friend nearby at all times. Whenever I needed anything, I just knocked at Dana’s door. If I needed help picking out an outfit, if I needed help replying to messages, even if I was just bored and I wanted someone to hang out with, it was always Dana who I turned to. There’s something about Gaza that just is home to me. There is simplicity and a love for life in Gaza that I haven’t seen elsewhere in the world. So being born and raised there always meant having loving and supportive people around me.

There’s more to us than being occupied. We’re full of talent and art we never got the time to make, because we’re too busy worrying if the people we love will still be alive tomorrow. That’s what pushes me to keep writing.

I’m interested in writing and telling stories of people. I recently interviewed Renad Atallah and wrote a piece about her for The New Arab. Also, one of my favorite pieces I wrote was an interview with Alaa Abu Diab, a one-of-a-kind comedian, for The National.

As of recently, I started my newsletter on Substack, where I share a personal essay every week on Gaza, girlhood, friendship, and everything in between. My ultimate hope would be to tell stories about a free Palestine one day. And as a journalist, I’ll always see my work as incomplete without going back to Gaza and continuing to tell Palestinian stories from the ground. And hopefully work on an international level and tell stories of people who suffered because of war and conflicts in other countries.

I enjoy reading nonfiction books and memoirs because I love learning about people and their experiences. I remember reading Anne Frank’s diary in middle school.… I remember how it made me feel and the impact it had on me.

I have also always been drawn to personal writings, like Franz Kafka’s letters and the letters between Ghassan Kanafani and Ghada al-Samman. I think I’m a very curious person by nature, and I’m interested in understanding what is happening in people’s minds, and if I get the chance to read strangers’ diaries, I wouldn't say no. That curiosity is something that inspired me.

Growing up, I always had a diary. It was me and my purple diary against the world.… For me, journaling has always been my safe space in the world. It’s the one place where I can write whatever I want without feeling judged. That’s honestly why I wanted to create Neya [Alaqad’s creative brand], which is an Arabic word that means intention. The first product will be a journal, of course, and it will come in four different colors. Each journal has a small, kind reminder on the hardcover and little sweet quotes throughout the pages because I wanted to create something that is personal and safe, something you would want to come back to and share your thoughts in. For me, journaling has always been where I can reconnect with myself. I hope Neya can become that for other people too: a small space where they can pause, reflect, and reconnect with themselves.

The hardest part was having something as intimate and personal as a diary become public. Something that was once only mine suddenly was in the hands of people from all around the world. I always try to think about the impact it could have on others, and I’m grateful that this time we get to tell our own stories rather than having our stories told on our behalf. I’m also grateful for the readers around the world who have read The Eyes of Gaza and are making the effort to educate themselves about Palestine.

I often feel like I am living in two worlds. On one hand, I have the opportunity to do things that many people back home cannot access freely, such as pursuing my education and continuing my master’s degree. On the other hand, I carry the weight of knowing what is happening to my people and my land, without having any superpower to stop it.

I always try to stay honest and true to myself, and I try to take people through this journey with me online. I share the contradictions, the pain, and the moments of hope. What it’s like trying to process the experience of having your homeland bombed, your city erased, and your people murdered, while also surviving and trying to make sense of the world outside. It is something that is difficult to put into words.

It feels like time has frozen in Gaza, while the rest of the world continues moving forward. And for those of us away from home, we are constantly trying to live between these two realities.

Gaza is a place where contradictions exist side by side. You see unimaginable grief, but you also see people trying to find meaning and strength in the middle of that suffering.

The Eyes of Gaza captures these contradictions. On one page, sadness may take over, while on another, hope emerges. Ultimately, I believe that when people experience powerful emotions, they are often moved to take action.

#6817. 16 Best Cashmere Sweaters for Men in 2025, According to GQ

The best men's cashmere sweaters are a lot like that guy Bryce from high school. Remember him? The one who was valedictorian and the star quarterback and the lead in the musical, and still always really nice to you. Hell, he probably also volunteered on weekends. Well, the best men's cashmere sweaters are the Bryce of autumnal staples.

Cashmere is the strongest and warmest and softest and swankiest form of wool there is, and it’s here to take your dreary cold weather wardrobe to bold new heights of handsomeness and coziness. The very best cashmere sweaters instantly make whatever you happen to have on that day—a T-shirt and denim, say, or a button-down and blazer—look miles more considered, sophisticated, and polished.

Make sense? Good, because now comes the fun part: settling on the right cashmere sweater for you. Are you after a basic cashmere pullover? A svelte cashmere turtleneck, perhaps? How about a currently-trending cashmere cardigan? To help make that decision as simple and streamlined as possible, we found 16 of the very best men's cashmere sweaters on the planet—from the gigantic flexes worth dropping a paycheck on to the budget-friendly crewnecks you should cop in every color. You'll look even better than Bryce did the night he was crowned prom king. Take that, Bryce.

In 2025, Banana Republic hawks no shortage of discreet office-ready staples, but tucked in amongst them is one of the best cashmere sweater propositions you'll find today. Essentially, this is the platonic ideal of an affordable cashmere sweater: Soft as anything, snug at the wrists and hem, and still roomy enough in the body and shoulders.

But the folks at Banana Republic didn't down tools once they'd nailed the fit and fabric. (And that's exactly why they've earned the top spot in our rankings.) Instead, they paid a ton of attention to their color choices, turning out a range of dignified, unexpected, and expensive-feeling shades, from the dusty blue above to a spotted brown, reddish pink, and classy ecru—alongside your typical black, gray, and navy. No wonder 205 happy customers took the time to give it a five-star online review.

After its supreme softness, cashmere’s best-known attribute is its high price. But thanks to the forces that be (globalization, capitalism, etc.) cashmere is shedding its high-falutin’ price tags. The quality of more affordable cashmere sweaters aren’t up to par with the truly nice stuff, of course, but some options are better than others.

For us, the best of the budget bunch is Uniqlo. For just a hundred bucks, you get a satisfyingly soft pure cashmere sweater in a classic, relaxed fit, available in an impressively wide range of colors. If you want an upgrade from merino wool without the massive price jump, Uniqlo’s your best bet. The style is timeless and the cashmere is softer than you’d expect for the pricepoint.

We’ve said it time and time again: long-sleeve polos make you hot. And in winter, the best long-sleeve polos are made from luscious cashmere. And have the right fit. And a really good drape. And also a collar that’s not too small nor too thin.

All of that is usually one spinning plate too many for most brands, but Buck Mason is the Cirque du Soleil of knitwear and its California Cashmere Polo sweater is its harrowing act. The fabric is a 12-gauge jersey knit made from long-staple cashmere that has a pillowy hand and an elegant drape that, unlike many flimsy cashmeres, has plenty of structure. The neck hits the bullseye and is deep enough for a tee to peek out, but not so deep to reveal the insane graphic on said tee. It also hits that middle-tier price point oh so well, and even out-classes some higher-priced options.

You don’t have to do much to a cashmere sweater for it to make a big impression. The fabric itself speaks volumes, so even the most basic of knitwear options can instantly make an outfit feel a bit luxer, more considered, and altogether elevated. (Just think about how put together guys like David Beckham and Daniel Day-Lewis look wearing plain ol' pullovers.)

At the same time, though, a little visual flourish never hurt anybody, which is why we love these cashmere crewneck sweaters from the new J.Crew. They still let the cashmere do the talking, but the classic cable knit gives it a bit of visual interest and keeps it from falling flat. The quality cashmere is more than solid for the price and looks great solo with jeans and sneakers, but cleans up real good with a button-up peaking out and a blazer over top.

There isn't too much to say here: You're either a cashmere hoodie person or you aren't. The ones who are, though, would tell you they make flights (and movie nights on the couch) a cosy, incognito dream, and allow you to get away with wearing a hoodie all kinds of other places that they wouldn't typically be welcome. And when it comes to where to find one, Todd Snyder is here, as always, with a handsome, clean silhouette that makes the cashmere hoodie's refined nature even more so. It also comes in three nice colours. Thanks Todd.

Most cashmere stuff feels very East Coast prep—all straight-laced and solid-colored—which makes sense, because the East Coast is where it’s cold. Back in 2007, though, LA designer Greg Chait flipped that on its head: under the moniker The Elder Statesman, he started making cashmere blankets, then sweaters and ponchos and socks and stuffed animals, with a distinctly Californian vibe.

The stuff is often tie-dyed and dip-dyed, sometimes emblazoned with palm trees or magic mushrooms or the checkerboard pattern on your trusty Vans for a jolt of Justin-Bieber-exiting-Sushi-Park swagger. But here, Chait and co. are playing the hits, rendering their cult-favorite sweaters in six different straight-forward, appealing colors.

Working on an Irish isle, Inis Meáin has woven traditional Aran craftsmanship with modern techniques for decades. But, crucially, it still only produces its knits in small batches. Which means this is a rare, rare waffle.

Quince makes one of the best affordable cashmere sweaters on the market. It’s hard to disagree with the idea that a good piece of knitwear shouldn’t equate to a down payment on a home. It might not be as luxe as some of our other favorite options, but it’s still ultra soft, which means you can swaddle yourself comfortably without worrying about the damage this sweater has done to your bank account.

Whoever this supposed “Jordan” guy is, we’re guessing that Alex Mill named its cashmere sweater after the famed basketball legend because it also has it all: thick-gauge yarns, a fluffy texture, elegant drape, classic fit, and a perfect crew neck collar.

While some DTC cashmere brands focus solely on finding the luxest fibers, Everlane remains focused on its pricing also. The cashmere crewnecks use a blend of new cashmere and recycled cashmere for a reduced environmental impact, and still feel incredibly soft in the hand.

Mr P.—Mr Porter's in-house line—normally makes excellent, quiet basics. We're proud of the fictional man's decision to go off a little here.

Ghiaia Cashmere’s has expanded over the last few years but the brands initial focus is quite literally in the name. If there’s one place you can turn to when you’re in search of beautiful cashmere knits, then it’s Ghiaia. It’s knitted in Italy, and it has raglan sleeves with subtle ribbing in the sleeves and neck.

The cardigan du jour is not the tight-fitting knit of trends gone by, but rather a thicker, looser variety. In fact, it's sort of looks just like this.

Extreme Cashmere's designs come in only one, inclusive size, so if you're a small person who likes a close fit—move on. But if you're anything else, swaddle thyself.

A deep cut from our Italian friends across the Atlantic, Fedeli is the go-to cashmere brand for many exacting Europeans.

Another polo sweater here, but this time from the luxury gods at Connolly.

Cashmere is a kind of wool that’s made from the undercoats of goats. While goat’s outercoats are thick and coarse to protect them from the harsher elements, the undercoat remains ultra soft and fine. The fibers are actually hollow which makes them great for insulation which is why cashmere is known for its super smooth hand and high warmth to weight ratio. What separates cashmere from other types of wool is its superior silky hand and fine fibers which can vary between 14 to 19 microns in thickness—thinner than even the finest sheep’s wool. For reference, human hairs are only as fine as about 50 microns.

Here's the catch: high-quality cashmere will usually cost you a pretty penny. For the price, you should be getting a form of cashmere made from long staple fibers spun into multi-ply yarns and knit into a tightly-tensioned fabric. Those factors all contribute to cashmere's pedigreed reputation as a material that's much less prone to pilling and stretching than its woolier counterparts. (That reputation, rightful or not, also informs its notoriously high price tag.) Cashmere is graded by its thickness and staple length with Grade A being the highest quality, followed by grades B and C. The higher the grade, the softer the yarns, the warmer the fabric, the better the durability.

Over the last few years, demand for good quality affordable cashmere sweaters has transformed the erstwhile rich-guy signifier into a bona fide cold weather staple you don't have to drain a 401(k) to afford. With one small caveat: supply chain transparency is infamously hard to suss out in the fashion industry, but don't expect a $100 cashmere joint—no matter how tony its source of origin—to hold up as well as a $500 one.

Style is subjective, we know—that’s the fun of it. But we’re serious about helping our audience get dressed. Whether it’s the best white sneakers, the flyest affordable suits, or the need-to-know menswear drops of the week, GQ Recommends’ perspective is built on years of hands-on experience, an insider awareness of what’s in and what’s next, and a mission to find the best version of everything out there, at every price point.

Our staffers aren’t able to try on every single piece of clothing you read about on GQ.com (fashion moves fast these days), but we have an intimate knowledge of each brand’s strengths and know the hallmarks of quality clothing—from materials and sourcing, to craftsmanship, to sustainability efforts that aren’t just greenwashing. GQ Recommends heavily emphasizes our own editorial experience with those brands, how they make their clothes, and how those clothes have been reviewed by customers. Bottom line: GQ wouldn’t tell you to wear it if we wouldn’t.

We make every effort to cast as wide of a net as possible, with an eye on identifying the best options across three key categories: quality, fit, and price.

To kick off the process, we enlist the GQ Recommends braintrust to vote on our contenders. Some of the folks involved have worked in retail, slinging clothes to the masses; others have toiled for small-batch menswear labels; all spend way too much time thinking about what hangs in their closets.

We lean on that collective experience to guide our search, culling a mix of household names, indie favorites, and the artisanal imprints on the bleeding-edge of the genre. Then we narrow down the assortment to the picks that scored the highest across quality, fit, and price.

Across the majority of our buying guides, our team boasts firsthand experience with the bulk of our selects, but a handful are totally new to us. So after several months of intense debate, we tally the votes, collate the anecdotal evidence, and emerge with a list of what we believe to be the absolute best of the category right now, from the tried-and-true stalwarts to the modern disruptors, the affordable beaters to the wildly expensive (but wildly worth-it) designer riffs.

Whatever your preferences, whatever your style, there's bound to be a superlative version on this list for you. (Read more about GQ's testing process here.)

#6815. The GTA Files: My Long-Lost Interview With the Brothers Who Founded Rockstar

“You can’t fuck with Keith Richards.” That’s what Sam Houser told me when I asked the inspiration for his company name, Rockstar Games. “I’m a huge fan of the classic golden era of rock stars—both the music, but also the life,” he went on. “You can't argue with Keith Moon riding in a Rolls Royce with a blowup doll hanging out the back and a woman in there screaming, ‘I'm being raped.’ The guy is the original punk rocker. And if we can bring a fraction of that to games, then we’re doing something.”

It was October 2002, just a few weeks before the release of their latest blockbuster, Grand Theft Auto: Vice City. I’d come to their glossy loft in Soho, Manhattan to interview the elusive 30-year-old Houser and Rockstar co-founders, his younger brother Dan Houser and managing director Terry Donovan, for Rolling Stone. It would be one of their first, last and only in-depth sit-downs together before they decided that the world’s most successful and controversial video game required little explanation from them at all.

I continued reporting on them and their games during the raucous and turbulent years to follow: hitting over $10 billion in sales, getting compared to Balzac and Dickens in Time magazine, being sued (unsuccessfully) for causing real-life violence. As I investigated in my book Jacked: The Outlaw Story of Grand Theft Auto, they also triggered what remains the most outrageous and cinematic scandal ever to hit the industry: the discovery of a hidden sex scene in 2005’s GTA San Andreas that would inspire attempts to legislate and ban the game. As Hilary Clinton said at the time, “The disturbing material in Grand Theft Auto and other games like it is stealing the innocence of our children and it’s making the difficult job of being a parent even harder.”

With the gamer generation now grown up and in power, the Reefer Madness panic around GTA has finally given way to something more important: this little game out of Scotland has become the most successful and influential entertainment product of all time. Today, Rockstar returns to Vice City with the launch of Grand Theft Auto VI. More than a decade in the making, with a budget Rockstar has never disclosed but analysts put between $1 billion and $2 billion, the game follows Jason Duval, an ex-soldier, and Lucia Caminos, who phones him from prison, as they’re pulled into a criminal conspiracy across a send-up of Miami. Its first trailer has been viewed more than 300 million times on YouTube, and footage premiering on Netflix nearly crashed the streamer’s servers. Take-Two Interactive, Rockstar’s parent company, expects record revenue of $8.1 billion this fiscal year.

For longtime fans, GTA 6 marks something more personal: the first game in the franchise since the original band behind it broke up. Following the departure of Donovan and other central figures, Dan Houser exited Rockstar in 2020, leaving his big brother Sam as the last founder still there. With Sam remaining notoriously quiet about the new game, I went digging through my old tapes and found my long-lost interview with gaming’s original punk rockers. During our two-hour talk, the young co-founders were brash, unrepentant, and championing a lifetime of obsessions, as Sam put it. “I wasn’t just obsessed about games,” he told me, “we were obsessed about everything—obsessive about music, obsessive about film, obsessive about fashion.”

GTA 6 shows that the obsession never left him. Here in this exclusive excerpt from my interview, the Rockstars share how it all started.

Sam Houser: We nearly had a fist fight last week. We have one argument a year.

Sam Houser: That was an old arcade game called Mr. Do! It used to be in like, a bodega, a corner store. You go in there and buy a cigarette for seven P. And you get a couple of games of Mr. Do!. But the games that really got me hooked was when I got into the Sinclair ZX Spectrum. It was very British: a weird little computer with rubber keys, and it was the games that came out then. I was obsessive about everything, really. We had every significant game on every game system going.

Dan Houser: They thought it was interesting. Sam was probably more into games throughout the time. I would come in and out from being interested in them, or being forced to play them. There was physical violence.

Sam Houser: I would beat him into playing.

Terry Donovan: I think for me, there was a period of arcade games: Defender, Track and Field, Tron, Pole Position. So that was that was like the golden age. You would you stand there and if a bloke lasted two hours on 10p on Defender, you thought he was it. If you grew up in the '70s, you probably wanted to be a guitarist. If you were in the '80s, you probably wanted to be a DJ. The things you grow up wanting now, you probably want to be a video game designer.

Sam Houser: Absolutely. That’s one of the essential things, for me, in games. Look at the games we do now. The game that really coined that in the early days, particularly for me, was this game Elite. You were a space smuggler, and it was just very simple graphics—black screen with just white lights on it. And you flew around space, interacting with other spaceships and other colonies and other planets and so on.

Dan Houser: It's like reading any kind of book when you're a kid or anything that makes you feel like you're not in the real world.

Sam Houser: What was interesting about GTA 1 was that graphically it was top down. It wasn't as sharp or appealing as things that were popular at the time, like Tomb Raider and all these other games that were coming out. You were put into the world, and you were given choices. It was a very sort of non-linear experience. Because it showed all these people out there that wanted this kind of experience that were prepared to forego the graphical gratification you got at the Tomb Raider—you know, that girl with the nice breasts. Suddenly you were playing in 2D and it was top down. It was utterly compelling.

I thought the subject matter was a really appealing hook. We felt that the sort of the fantasy sci-fi, fantasy, you know, Dungeons & Dragons, and here's my sword and there's that orc. We didn't feel that the mass market was that into that stuff. We wanted to make things that felt a little bit more relevant to people like ourselves. The reason it sold is it was incredibly fun to play. It was originally called Race n' Chase. Kind of catchy name. In the UK, the game was in the House of Parliament, and it got a massive share of media attention. It was on BBC News five times in one day. Like, oh my God!

Terry Donovan: It was one first piece of entertainment that was sort of unashamed antihero. There's no twist at the end where he turns out to be a good guy.

Dan Houser: Freedom was the thing that appealed. Because as free as games like Defender and Scramble were for us, 10 years on, they felt restrictive. I don't consider myself a master gamer. I play every game. I'm good at them. But I think that we try and make games that can be played by normal folk as well as hardcore too. And the problem with some of those other games is when you hit a point that's frustrating, you can't get past it. But in Grand Theft Auto, any of them, when you hit a point that's tough, you just go and do something else. And then while you're doing the other thing, you're thinking about this other thing that you've been stuck on, and then you come back to it and crack it. I think that's fucking great.

Sam Houser: I came out here and did a very energetic pitch where I must have sweated through three layers of clothing in my kind of insanely sweaty way, and everyone in the office was like, "Who the fuck is that guy?"

Sam Houser: They just didn't want it. Business is so like, “What's that person doing? We must copy that.” Everybody else was doing 3D platform games and 3D sports games, all these shooters.

Sam Houser: I first came here in my 18th birthday. It my 18th birthday present or whatever to just go and buy leather hip-hop puffers and Air Jordan Mark V's. From that moment I was chronically in love with the place. The idea that I would end up here making games where games is traditionally a West Coast thing, to me it's just like… in some ways I feel it was meant to be. We were kind of enlisted to sort of build that.

Sam Houser: The mission was basically to bring our attitude, to try and make games that felt more relevant to the audience that was playing.

Dan Houser: It’s more modern, more accessible, gameplay over graphics, but with a slightly anarchic, edgy fantasy—a fantasy you would have walking down the street rather than from reading Dungeons & Dragons. It’s a fantasy where you're a street racer rather than a wizard.

Terry Donovan: [to Sam] How much did you want a Def Jam baseball jacket?

Sam Houser: More than anything in the world. They had this attitude: “Here's where we from. Here's what we do. If you don't like us, fuck you. Everything we touch is somehow made more magical.” A guy like Rick Rubin is such a fucking hero, because you go from doing the hip-hop that he pioneered to doing Slayer, to doing The Cult.

Terry Donovan: It was a first-floor apartment on Water Street, in the South Street Seaport, and it had glass on the front. There was no fucking light in it.

Sam Houser: There was about 20 people. By the time I was in there, there was about seven people living in there. That whole teenager-in-the-bedroom-with-the-bottle-top-specs thing comes around the games industry like a fucking albatross. This wasn't the kind of lonely nine-year-old or 12-year-old kid, or the one who's going to end up exposing himself to schoolgirl doing this.

Terry Donovan: The lines are our decision of what's funny and what's tasteful. What isn't relevant to what a lot of what other people think. If you think it's funny, you put it in your machine, if you don't think it's funny, you don't. By the time you've reduced Grand Theft Auto on national television to a 30-second soundbite, it's a game with a hooker in it, and that's offensive to the very, very maximum. That's the thing—you realize that [gamers] aren't all 10 years old. There isn't some overriding social responsibility to have a redeeming social value. Our responsibility is to provide a toolkit with which young adults can entertain themselves.

Sam Houser: When you do something wrong in the game, the police come and get you. You don't just run around on a rampage and carry on. You commit crimes, the police are on you. You commit more, they're on you more. You commit more, the FBI will turn up, the SWAT will turn up, and then the Army turns up. When you play the missions, you're only you're only doing bad things to bad people. You're whacking other gangsters.

Sam Houser: When I play it for fun, I tend to just drive around and look at cool things and drive in as cinematic a way as possible— sort of like I’m a stifled film director. And I’m carrying out my own little stories in my own head, listening the music. I mean, gosh, I am 30 years old. It's sort of tragic, isn't it? I just sort of go off in my own world.

Sam Houser: Oh, well, Scarface, Miami Vice. At the end of GTA III, we took our whole team out to Miami. We're all out there. This is we live and breathe this place, learn this place. For research, we were staying in a very average hotel on Ocean Drive. You know, it was 30 Scottish guys walking around with cameras in the day. The second half of the week, everyone got insanely burned.

Dan Houser: Vice City is not quite Miami. It's Miami through the Grand Theft Auto lens. On the other hand, you don't want to offend the nerds because you've not put the right number of bridges in, or things are a little bit different. You know, we're so anal that we don't want to offend other anal people. For example, with the radio commercials, me and the other guy, one of the other guys who just then with me would sit for hours and listen to '80s radio commercials. We got we ring up advertising agencies and say, ‘send us all your best best '80s radio and TV commercials.’

Sam Houser: We're trying to do a satire about the 80s.

Sam Houser: We cast them ourselves. Contact their agents ourselves. Tommy Vercetti is played by Ray Liotta, interacting with Lance Vance, Philip Michael Thomas, [Ricardo] Tubbs from Miami Vice. The two of them together, they have these really entertaining moments.

Dan Houser: Her character is a porn star. She was really sweet, though. She was really lovely. She turned up with her husband and her mother and father, and I'm particularly very English in these situations, so I'm feeling slightly… I'm going, "Oh, hello, Jenna. Could you sound like you're excited?" And she's looking at me more and more obliquely and says "Well, how would it be if I sound like I was having sex?" And I'm like, “Well, that'd be actually perfect.”

Sam Houser: My favorite thing is probably the overall look. I think the thing that we set up so much stuff on was, get me neon. We've got to have neon!

Dan Houser: You can do everything you can do in a film and more. It's all the bits that go in between the equivalent plots of about five films. But it's a different experience compared to a film.

Sam Houser: Almost by definition, no. Apart from choose-your-own-adventure books. You feel like, “Hey, [I have] my little piece of digital real estate, and there's my car and everything. I own this little space.” It's just some numbers and ones and all this code. When the machine's on and it's going, it's there, it's real.

Terry Donovan: There's no point being edgy if your game's a shit.

Sam Houser: At the end of the day, there's enough people in the country that would like to see us thrown out and/or locked up for doing what we do. Look at the trouble we got in for the prostitute thing. You'd be amazed how conservative people are.

#6813. Prince Harry Says Moving to England Has Given Archie and Lili a Big Confidence Boost

The royal sat down for his first TV appearance since returning to England

Prince Archie and Princess Lilibet are all settled into their new life in England, it seems—but it wasn’t a simple transition. Today, Prince Harry sat down for a rare chat on U.K. daytime show This Morning—his first television appearance since returning to England—and opened up about the changes his kids have gone through since the move in late August.

Harry said of his children: “They love being back! I say back, they never really...”

“It’s a big culture shift ... The kids in school uniforms is a big change for them, but it’s certainly helped,” he added. “The confidence in both of them has gone through the roof since being at a U.K. school, which is really, really nice.”

Harry also touched on his kids’ accents, which seem to be leaning British these days. The prince joked that he is “squeezing the accents,” and Archie and Lili’s “friends at school are doing the same.” He noted that “Lili does a good impression of ‘Mummy.’”

Harry’s TV appearance came a couple days after he and wife Duchess Meghan were seen out with their kids for the first time since their move (though the couple has made some public outings without the children). The family went to Winter Wonderland, an annual six-week Christmas festival and fair in London’s Hyde Park.

Elsewhere during his TV chat, the Duke of Sussex discussed the upcoming Invictus Games, which are set to take place in July 2027 in Birmingham, England. Harry has been participating in related events leading up to the games, and over the weekend even made an appearance at the Invictus Games in Lviv, Ukraine.

#6811. Trump names Stephen Miller his chief of staff ahead of Dem takeover

Stephen Miller, White House deputy chief of staff, talks with reporters at the White House on April 28. (Tom Williams/CQ Roll Call)

President Donald Trump on Friday announced that Stephen Miller, the architect of the hard-line immigration and deportation program voters rejected this week, will become White House chief of staff.

Susie Wiles will depart that post and move to the Trump political organization, which appears to be in full operation, even after the president's last midterm election. Miller has been deputy chief of staff, overseeing the Trump administration's domestic policy.

Miller’s first day will be Monday, Trump said in a social media post.

Trump dubbed Miller “one of my most trusted advisors” and said Wiles, who also oversaw his 2024 presidential campaign, “has done an outstanding job.”

Wiles, who Trump recently announced had won a battle with breast cancer, said in a X post that “serving President Trump in the White House has been the honor of a lifetime,” adding she was “proud to have been a part of what we’ve accomplished together from securing our border and cutting taxes to strengthening our economy and putting America First.”

The chief-of-staff change came three days after Republicans were demolished in Trump’s final midterm election, with House Democrats on pace for the largest majority in over a decade and at least a 54-seat Senate majority come January.

Trump addressed the personnel move as he departed the White House Friday for an event and weekend in Florida — though he ignored a question about his recent declaration that Wiles would be sticking around.

“I wanted to make a change,” he said in a roughly three-minute exchange with reporters before he walked to Marine One. “Sometimes in life, you have to make moves.”

Trump in recent days had become open to working with Democrats after the new Congress is seated on Jan. 3, even after railing against what he called the party’s “bad” policy during an October-long campaign rally blitz.

“Look, I mean, I'll get along, probably, with the Democrats, unless they want to be destructive,” Trump told reporters Wednesday. “But I have the power of the veto. So if we don't like what they're doing… They've already called and they want to talk about things. And that's fine.”

Trump summoned Thursday's press pool into the Oval Office for an executive order signing. On his desk was a red baseball cap with "VETO" across the front in large white lettering.

Miller has worked on Capitol Hill as an aide to several GOP lawmakers, including former Sen. Jeff Sessions, R-Ala., who became attorney general in Trump's first administration.

But making the hard-line Miller his White House chief of staff even before congressional Democrats get organized likely will draw the ire of the incoming majority.

Virginia Rep. Donald S. Beyer Jr. was among the first Democratic lawmakers to slam Trump’s decision.

“Trump’s elevation of Stephen Miller doubles down on the politics of hatred, lawlessness, and failure that the American people soundly rejected three days ago,” Beyer said in a statement. "Miller embodies the worst elements of the Trump presidency. His promotion shows that Trump has learned nothing from his electoral debacle and has no intention of even trying to work constructively with the next Congress.”

Many congressional Democrats — leaders, returning incumbents and members-elect — want to investigate Trump, his inner circle and family members while also pursuing a legislative agenda aimed at paring the cost of living.

To that end, as chief of staff, Democrat-run committees calling Miller to testify is highly unlikely since he could continue to claim executive privilege. That allows senior White House aides to withhold many internal West Wing communications, documents, and presidential-related conversations and actions, from lawmakers.

In public comments and television interviews, Miller has accused Democrats of being aligned with figures and candidates who “hate” Americans, and once said the party is “unworthy of power.”

“So the Democrats have become a party of freaks, weirdos, losers and crazies,” Miller said during a July appearance on Fox News. “But you can’t laugh it off because it’s deadly serious. When you give these people power, everything you know and love in this world falls apart. You have chaos, corruption, decay, violence, poverty.”

#6809. Zohran Mamdani’s Mustache Is Singlehandedly Bringing Movember Back

When New York City mayor Zohran Mamdani passed out water bottles to marathoners on Nov. 1, few clocked the newly mustachioned man as the city’s head honcho. After all, he’s the first bearded NYC mayor in over a century after, and his constituents had grown familiar with his signature look. When images of his new facial hair circulated, people wondered what inspired the change. Had the mayor been influenced by his hip Brooklyn constituents enough to grow his own on-trend mustache? As we learned the day after the marathon in an online video, the mustache was in support of a somewhat-ancient tradition: Movember.

If it feels like no one’s talked about Movember for a while, that’s because the non-profit charity’s heyday online peaked in 2012. That year, according to the Australian-based foundation, over 1.1 million people participated in Movember—a campaign that encourages men to grow mustaches to raise awareness and funds for men’s mental health, prostate cancer, and testicular cancer—collecting more than $126 million in charity. But like planking, “Gangnam Style,” and flash mobs, Movember eventually faded from the cultural conversation. Google Trends recorded a 10% drop in searches for the cause the following year, and by 2026, search interest had fallen 90% from its 2012 peak. Now, after Mamdani, alongside Council Member Rita Joseph, announced a $4 million investment in a new prostate cancer center at Brooklyn’s Kings County Hospital in honor of the hairy month-long social movement, interest in Movember has since climbed to its highest level since 2019, according to Google Trends.

While the mayor’s stubble has come and gone throughout the month, the heavy mustache has remained constant.

The stubble around the mustache has come and gone during the month, but that thick mustache has remained a staple. He had it at a Veterans Day reception, during a plethora of press conferences, and when he announced the disbanding of the New York Police Department’s controversial Strategic Response Group.

Politicians have long been defined, at least in part, by their hair: Donald Trump’s comb-over, Bernie Sanders’ horseshoe-shaped hairline, Gavin Newsom’s slicked-back locks. But facial hair is increasingly becoming part of the political image, too, as more men embrace letting their beards grow. Pete Buttigieg, once clean-shaven, grew a beard after leaving his post as Transportation Secretary; JD Vance became the first bearded vice president in decades; and Mamdani ended more than a century’s worth of clean-shaven NYC mayors.

And hey, what better time to revive an early 2010s tradition than following the Democrats’ historic victories in the midterm elections. Millennial woke optimism is back, baby, and so is growing facial hair for a charitable cause. If the mayor’s facial hair can turn a bygone internet fad into renewed attention for men’s health, it may be his most consequential grooming decision yet.
