#6807. With Grand Theft Auto VI, Rockstar Bet $1.5B That Humans Could Make a Better Game Than AI

Somewhere in the far north of Leonida, Grand Theft Auto VI’s fictional take on Florida, a robbery is taking place. This much is unsurprising. Crime is baked into the title of the series, which began in 1997 and, despite its thick American accent, is still predominantly made in Scotland. But Jason and Lucia, the young, beautiful Bonnie and Clyde who together share the role of protagonists in GTA VI, are not the ones committing this crime. Rather, a grizzled biker hangs from the second-floor window of a locked bar, throwing valuables down to his accomplice on the street.

A thunderstorm has recently rolled through this area and the tarmac steams as sunlight knifes through the clouds. It’s Leonida-muggy. Patches of sweat swell beneath Jason’s arms as he takes in the scene. The bikers notice you watching and ask, politely, considering the circumstances, if you could fuck off. A choice presents itself: clamber back on your stolen motorbike and continue your road trip, or insert yourself into this micro-drama and take a principled stand on behalf of concerned citizens everywhere (then take the loot for yourself, of course).

Dozens of impromptu moments like this stud GTA6, a video game that makes you feel like one entity among many rather than the spoiled V.I.P. around whom everything has been artfully arranged. Most video games flatter the player by making them the centre of the virtual universe, the cog on which all the digital clockwork turns. What is extraordinary about Vice City and its surrounding area is how much of the world appears to trundle on without needing your presence or input.

These moments are made possible by dozens of interlocking systems built by dozens of discrete teams. An entire Los-Angeles-based team worked on the personalities and behaviors of the thousands of people who inhabit Vice City; the bar’s interior was meticulously set-dressed to suggest the lives and tastes of its proprietors and patrons, while the biker’s dialogue and reactions to your choices were written, cast and performed. The weather, too, is restless: cloud and rain move across the world, dynamically changing light and mood. This tableaux, which only a small percentage of players may ever encounter, has not been staged like a scene in a play but rather cultivated in what amounts to the world’s most expensive Petri dish.

Since 1984, when Elite first used procedural generation to conjure an entire galaxy on a computer screen, game designers have learned to produce universes from scant technological resources. But in the era of procedural generation, scale is almost routine; the harder, more costly work is making an expanse feel handmade and coherent. GTA VI manages both, with districts populated by distinct communities and social strata, each shaped by decades of imagined history. It reveals a kind of detail and thoughtfulness that A.I. has yet to reproduce convincingly—and perhaps never will.

The game’s lavishness is not only visible in the rain, the sweat and the individual strands of hair falling across Lucia’s shoulders, but also in the hundreds, perhaps thousands, of diversions away from the criminal grind. For example, Jason and Lucia can take time out from planning a heist to compete in illegal street races for prize money, enter dirt-bike scrambles or race stock cars in a stadium. You can become a hunter, choosing a rifle to suit your quarry so as not to ruin its pelt; start drone racing, streaking through abandoned buildings in an FPV headset; or dive for treasure among old shipwrecks, dodging both the circling sharks, and the authorities who want to check your license.

A compelling main story runs through the game, with missions that leave room for different approaches: you might play as a careful shooter, aiming to wound rather than kill, or as a less discerning one. The couple, so artfully written and performed, offer vivid points of entry into the world as you guide them from relative poverty towards financial security that, perhaps, might allow them to build a family life beyond crime. But much of the joy comes from straying from the main path.

To that end the game makes room for long, meandering drives across the country and peaceful early-morning flights over mountain ranges. You can spend a sleepy afternoon with a scenic view, peering through dollar-a-minute binoculars. Many of these diversions appeared in the studio’s earlier games, but here they feel newly vivid, rendered with extraordinary fidelity. The smaller encounters are just as memorable. Happen upon a possum at night and it might play dead on the tarmac. In a thrift store, you can while away assembling fits worthy of a Ralph Lauren vintage buyer; at a 24-hour nightclub, you can dance until sunrise, before emerging blinking into the dawn while wondering, as Kanye once put it, if they’ve got cabs still. (Of course they do: you can summon one in an instant from your virtual phone).

This is lavish game-making on an unprecedented scale. The irony, however, is that GTA VI arrives at a moment when other studios are bleeding staff (if they survive at all) and the cost of making games at anywhere close to this level continues to rise precipitously. In one industry survey published this month, a third of respondents said they had been laid off in the past two years; more than half of those laid off had yet to find new work—a stark picture of the ongoing insecurity across the industry. In the early 2010s, game budgets fell between $30 and $70 million. McKinsey puts the current cost of a blockbuster game production at $200 million to $500 million. At these prices, few can pay to play.

Even amid the union-busting allegations (which Rockstar denies; an employment tribunal is ongoing), the company has retained much of its long-serving talent between major releases. That continuity gives it a store of institutional knowledge that is hard to preserve when studios expand and contract around individual projects. Key members of GTA VI’s leadership have worked together for decades. The studio also has a clear commercial advantage over its closest creative peers: Naughty Dog, creator of the Uncharted and The Last of Us series, and CD Projekt Red, creator of The Witcher and Cyberpunk 2077. Their story-led games can dominate a moment, but their stories conclude when the credits roll.

After players finished Grand Theft Auto V’s story, by contrast, they could continue to inhabit its world through GTA Online, joining others in an ever-replenishing sandbox of play. A leak of Rockstar data suggested that GTA Online averaged about $9.6 million a week between September 2025 and April 2026—roughly $42 million a month. Rockstar is owned by Take-Two, and there is no evidence that GTA Online’s astonishing revenue was entirely funneled into GTA VI. But a game generating revenue year after year gives its parent a durable source of income, justifying artisanal-style work. These factors make Grand Theft Auto VI’s scale and fidelity exceptionally difficult for any rival to match.

That challenge is something even Rockstar’s former leaders have had to reckon with. Leslie Benzies—who for years was one of the three creative leaders on Rockstar projects alongside Sam and Dan Houser (of the trio, only Sam remains at the company)—founded Build a Rocket Boy, which raised more than $300 million before releasing MindsEye. The game’s troubled launch was followed by mass redundancies and, in September 2026, a slide into administration. The lesson seems clear. Money alone cannot buy the infrastructure, expertise and returning audience that have allowed Rockstar to build this world at extraordinary length.

In a game filled with enticing luxuries, from expensive shoes to glinting supercars, the visible human hand is, in this dawning age of AI abundance, the rarest luxury of all in Leonida. You can leave the bikers to their robbery: clouds drift on, the tarmac dries. The world keeps its own time. Making that unforced life cohere took years of work across many teams, and required a collective attention to every detail. With budgets rising and studios closing, perhaps no other company could afford such a wager. Leonida is a miracle of craft—and a monument to a form of game-making the wider industry can no longer sustain.

#6805. Macy Grimshaw’s Custom Pieces Have Already Charmed Harry Styles and Emma Corrin, Now She’s Won H&M’s Design Award

Macy Grimshaw was announced this year’s winner of H&M’s Design Award in London yesterday—the Swedish company’s prize for fashion graduates is back after a six-year hiatus. The Central Saint Martin’s MA student, who made her paper-and-leather graduation collection about watching her grandmother’s memory fade with dementia, has already blown up across Instagram since she graduated in February.

Looking a bit stunned as she received a giant bouquet and €150,000 from H&M’s Ann-Sophie Johansson, she blinked, “I need to switch on my phone and tell my parents!”

“The whole purpose of the award is to celebrate creativity and to encourage and support young designers,” said Johansson. The competition was organized through a call-out to 70 fashion schools around the globe. The finalists, whittled down to a shortlist of eight, who get €10,000 each, turned out to be an assembly of highly successful grads from Polimoda in Florence, the Bunka school in Tokyo, IFM in Paris, La Cambre in Brussels, the Antwerp Academy, and Central Saint Martins. Three have already landed jobs in Paris houses.

Grimshaw’s prize-winning work explores how her grandmother’s memory faded with dementia.

Grimshaw’s custom pieces for Harry Styles, Emma Corrin, Raye, Alex Consani (Grimshaw has had the stylist Harry Lambert as an enthusiastic supporter since she was a BA student) might have had something to do with swaying the jury in her favor. Lambert testifies to her prolific creativity. “On everything we’ve collaborated on, Macy’s always come up with a dozen fully-formed ideas. She’s incredibly hard-working and I’m thrilled for her.”

Grimshaw’s also one of the few amongst the cohort who’ve already struck out into business on their own. Next up, she’s hoping to stage a presentation in London Fashion Week in February. The cash prize will boost her chances of affording that, but (before or after her show ) she also has the second part of the H&M Prize to work on: a separate contract for a collaboration collection. “I’m just excited for what it means to have help to produce my collection,” she said. “But also, I think it’s going to be a fantastic opportunity for myself to learn how that’s done on a larger scale. I’ve done it myself once on a very small scale, so I think it would be really interesting to learn from people how to do it. And what this means for my brand, I don’t really know. But I know I need to hire a financial advisor!”

Johansson illuminated: the winner goes to Stockholm to meet the collection team, product developers, and pattern makers. “Then we go on a production trip, most probably somewhere like Shanghai, to see the products actually taking shape.”

Zooming out, the comeback of the H&M graduate prize fits the bigger picture of the fierce competition to upgrade fashion credibility that’s gripping the high street. With Zara collaborating with John Galliano, Steven Meisel, and a stable of high-flown creative talent, and the likes of Gap hosting Victoria Beckham, H&M is presumably looking to defend its home turf. Some of its past designer collaborations have been legend, particularly the ones with Martin Margiela and Versace. Lately, they’ve tapped Elie Saab and Stella McCartney, who was one of the Swedish retailers earliest collaborators.

Talking to the gathering of the finalists also gave a good overview of the aspirations and ideals of the class of 2026. Though they’re navigating a very difficult time for young designers, it’s their fearlessness and pragmatism, as well as their highly specific dedication to making both materials and meaning, that unites them. Ennis Finnerty Mackay (also from CSM MA) combined his outstanding tailoring skills and the poignant backstory about the generational impact of alcoholism in his collection. It won him an internship with Daniel Roseberry at Schiaparelli, and now a full-time role at Balenciaga couture, where he’ll be working with Martine Rose. “I love what I’m doing”, he said. “I want to learn as much as I can now. Only then, maybe a long time in the future, would I do my own thing.”

Jessica Lin, a BA graduate of IFM also made a collection with a strong personal message. “A tribute to my mother and also a reflection of migrant women in Hong Kong back in the days. She’s had several migration experiences, to the Philippines and coming to Hong Kong and then back to China.” Lin’s clothes incorporate boiler suits, textiles evoking tiles, plastic slip-on shoes, and frontal harnesses suggesting baby-carriers. “I was inspired by the migrant woman who has dual identities as a mother and also at the same time as a worker.” Her ambition, eventually, is to start a lifestyle brand, between China and Europe.

The finesse and sexuality of Evelina Kryvopust’s collection was exceptional in close-up. “I think I’m the only Ukrainian that’s ever been in the H&M finals,” she smiled. The first step, literally, for this Polimoda graduate is into footwear. She’s producing her lingerie lace kitten heels in Italy. “I found a great factory in Naples.” Now she’s living in Milan, hoping to set up a studio with her designer boyfriend. One glance through her Instagram @evikrv proves this is someone who lives her brand, and who we’re likely to see a lot more about.

Custom orders are generally a way of life and a financial support-system for this generation. That’s how Macy Grimshaw’s done it so far. For many it makes complete sense, and is far more profitable than the risks involved in self-funding production for wholesale upfront, and then waiting months to get paid, if at all. On the other hand, this gathering of talent did go to prove that interesting, skilled, and intelligent young designers still are getting hired in top places. Fantin Delattre Pirenne, from La Cambre, is designing denim at Saint Laurent, while the exquisitely light textiles hand-made by the Antwerp Academy’s Yvonne Schichtel have landed her an internship at Dior haute couture. It may be a precarious time, but there was a lot of hope in that room.

#6803. 15 Best Beard Oils for Taming Your Stubborn Facial Hair

So, you've realized. Growing a beard was only the beginning: Now you’ve gotta give it some love. Thankfully, the best beard oil will keep that sculpted scruff feeling great! And not just for you, either, because the right beard oil leaves your skin glowing—and stops your facial hair from annoying any partner who gets close to it. Great beard oils also keep coarse hair from getting brittle and wiry, and soften your beard so you’re not scratching your neck or dealing with beard dandruff (yes, it exists). All that from just a couple drops of wonder oil.

I’d go so far as to rank beard oil as the second most important beard care product, just after beard trimmers. And it’s a close second. Because whether you keep your facial hair at a precise 24-hour stubble, go full hermit-mode, or stay somewhere in between, a beard oil does irreplaceable work. (The kind you can’t replace with any old moisturizer or conditioner.) So, if you’re tired of flyaways, coarse beard hair, dandruff, or itchiness, these GQ-tested and approved beard oils will turn your scruff into fluff. Promise.

For the longest time, I thought of Honest Amish as “that Amazon beard oil,” given its enormous popularity on the e-tailer. (At the time of writing, we’re talking nearly 30,000 reviews and a 4.5-star rating.) And often, I'll intentionally seek out an alternative to these Amazon-beloved grooming products—if only because it feels like the equivalent of recommending Applebee's to an out-of-towner. But this time, well: Welcome to Applebee's.

Frankly, I couldn't find a better beard oil. Not only is Honest Amish cost-effective (at the second-best price-per-ounce on my list), but the brand nailed the blend. It's a pure oil recipe (no non-oil ingredients), with seven carrier oils and seven essential oils that give it an earthy scent and help tone the skin beneath your bushel. It absorbs fast, works great to smooth down flyaways, relax stiff bristles, and battle both dry skin and flake-prone beards. And did I mention the value? Six bucks per ounce is a steal.

To be clear, I only use Honest Amish on my beard, largely because I'm not a fan of using any essential oils on bare skin—I particularly find that pumpkin seed oil (a key ingredient here) can be slightly pore-clogging on acne-prone skin. That said: I count myself as acne-prone and haven't had issues with Honest Amish, so I'm probably being over-cautious. It's really just that good.

Despite the name Beardbrand, this company's product line covers the whole body, with a focus on multi-tasking grooming products like dual washes, all-purpose balms, and this utilitarian oil. You'll catch me using it on my hands, elbows, and hair—whether for a dash of shine (to offset a matte hair product) or a protective coat against frizz and humidity. But Beardbrand's Utility Oil is on this list because it works beautifully in the beard.

Sure, it's a touch heavier than some other oils—but that also makes it a terrific pre-shave lubricant. It's important to note that no matter which “flavor” you choose, this oil has a strong dash of fragrance. They're all good, but I'm partial to the brand's new Bold Fortune scent, which oozes a sexy combo of leather, oud, and tobacco. (And pro-tip: The brand's utility wash has the same come-hither scent, if you want to double down.)

Horace's beard oil will make a lot of you happy, though for different reasons. For those overwhelmed by scents—particularly artificial ones—you'll appreciate that its got the subtlest of aromas. I'm partial to the Lemon & Mint one, which reads as “fresh" and never overwhelms. For those looking to stay away from chemicals, Horace's formula is 99% natural—and the 1% is a synthetic grapeseed oil, not something with 12 syllables, which I'm fine with.

The brand gets bonus marks for its easily-understood ingredients labels, which break down these details and err on the side of much-appreciated transparency. Finally, if you're on the fence about beard oils, Horace will make you a believer. It absorbs fast, gives a pinch of shine, and makes just enough difference in terms of taming, moisturizing, and softening that you'll see why a beard oil makes sense.

In the grooming world, Jack Black (the brand) has earned its own fanbase, thanks in large part to one product—the signature Jack Black beard oil. Apply just a few drops and it immediately goes to work softening coarse and brittle facial hair, and without an overpowering fragrance that will distract you all day long. It's one of the most popular beard oils on Amazon, but don't hold that against it. While it softens and adds shine, this oil also delivers a dose of antioxidants and vitamins from natural ingredients such as brown algae and rosemary.

It also uses a unique blend of oils with the subtlest whiff of melon fragrance. Instead of the usual suspects (jojoba, avocado, sunflower seed), this product uses Kalahari melon oil, marula oil, and plum oil, which moisturize your facial hair and skin on contact. Because these oils are all fast-absorbing (my beard hair soaks it up like a sponge), they make this product much less greasy than other beard oils, giving it a more premium feel.

I test literally hundreds of grooming products a year, and I'm so intimately acquainted with brands new and old that I often think about them based on their best product. For Pura d'Or, it's this organic beard oil. It's the best value on this list, at $3 per fluid ounce. Given how quickly you can go through beard oil—just think, if you're applying up to twice a day, that dollar-per-ounce math becomes important. So, in addition to being my budget pick, this also gets my recommendation as the best beard oil for big beards.

Of course, it's only important if the formula measures up—and here, it does. There are two carrier oils, argan and jojoba, along with a bit of grapefruit peel and some sandalwood essential oil for both a natural scent and a touch of antimicrobial powers. It's a great beard oil for anyone, no matter how big your bank account, but if you're bearding on a budget, you'll appreciate how much goodness Pura d'Or packs into a few bucks.

Blind Barber's tonka-tinged oil is wonderful for the guy who wants to moisturize skin and whiskers with the same product. It wears extremely light—so much so that my oily skin barely registers its presence—and I find its carrier-oil-centric and shea-butter-forward formula especially comforting on the skin. It should be noted that this formula includes artificial perfume, so fragrance-averse individuals should steer clear. But for those of us who want a special scent, a flake-free beard, and soothed skin, Blind Barber's beard oil does it all.

While I'd use most of the above options as both a hair oil and a beard oil, Aesop's is one where I think of it as a hair oil first and foremost. It brings control without making things look greasy, while trapping moisture and stopping frizz. I'll use it in short hair to tame down the sides, in medium lengths (mixed with a cream or clay) for added shine, or in long hair to prevent split ends. Then, once my hair's settled up, I'll run my hands through my beard afterward. So, if you want a great hair oil with beard benefits, Aesop's is the winner.

Since the beginning of the men's grooming boom, I've been waiting for the big designer fashion houses to start catering to guys with facial hair. For now, Tom Ford's beard oil stands alone. So, how does the designer oil stack up against our everyday favorites? After trying it out, I can tell you it’s better as a fragrance than a beard oil, but the scent alone earns it a spot in this guide.

The tobacco vanilla fragrance is much more sophisticated than what we’re used to seeing, or smelling, in this space. The scent puts an emphasis on vanilla, and you just need one or two pumps for full coverage. Considering the price and the name on the bottle, we were shocked to find this oil comes in a plastic pump bottle, but that fragrance. If you want to buy beard oil as a gift, this premium conditioning oil is a step up over your average Amazon brand, even if it doesn't soften coarse hair as well as some of our other options.

If you have sensitive skin—or if you're afraid of your beard oil clashing with your daily cologne—then Marlowe makes one of the only fragrance-free beard oils. Most beard oil formulas contain both essential oils and some type of perfume oil (or, parfum, for my fellow ingredients detectives), which can be risky for your skin, follicles, and pores, so it’s nice to have an option for acne-prone and sensitive skin types.

This beard oil couldn’t be simpler—and I mean that in a good way. Marlowe No. 143 has an all-oil formula and just four ingredients: safflower seed oil, sunflower seed oil, corn oil, and soybean oil. That’s it. Considering the average beauty product contains up to a dozen ingredients we can’t even pronounce, it’s a refreshing change. Although, I have to say, if you want the softest possible beard, Honest Amish also makes a fragrance-free oil.

Most guys use beard oil to tame flyaways. But what if you want the thickest, fullest beard possible? In that case, I’d recommend reaching for Bevel’s oil. This brand makes skin and hair products for Black men, but anyone with coarse or curly hair can benefit from their products. GQ contributor Timothy Beck Werth reports that this oil gave him a thicker-looking beard without excess frizz, which isn't easy to do. (BTW, that’s as much as you can expect from any beard oil, so please don’t fall for the “beard growth serums” you’ll find all over the internet.)

For parched facial hair, the grapeseed, macadamia, jojoba, and argan oil will condition your beard and add a healthy shine. For beneath the scruff, the avocado and primrose oil with soothe your skin without clogging up your pores and follicles. Werth says this beard oil instantly softened his facial hair, and he also liked the sweeter smell of the macadamia-based formula. Most beard oils opt for woodsy fragrances, and the more subtle scent profile here is a nice surprise, he explains.

Anthony’s conditioning beard oil is a 2-in-1 product—a pre-shave oil and regular beard oil. That approach can be risky, since a lot of multipurpose products try to be everything and succeed at nothing, but luckily that’s not the case here. If you regularly cycle between clean-shaven, stubble, and full-on lighthouse keeper, then you can still use this product daily. As a pre-shave oil, it moisturizes the skin and softens facial hair for a smoother shave with fewer morning-after red bumps. As a beard oil, it hydrates, then locks in that moisture so your beard stays soft and shiny all day. I found the consistency to be somewhere in between a leave-in conditioner gel and a traditional oil, making it thicker and less messy to apply.

The oil also has an herbaceous, minty fragrance that’s so refreshing it feels like aromatherapy every time you run it through your hair. That’s because the formula is full of ingredients that smell good, feel good, and actually help your skin and hair. It uses carrier oils like soybean and castor to deliver skin-pleasing and antimicrobial botanicals such as rosemary, basil, tea tree oil, eucalyptus, and spearmint. And on the hydration front, calendula and olive oil nourish your whiskers into their softest, fluffiest selves. Because of the more complex fragrance and ingredients profile, this wouldn’t be my pick for guys with sensitive skin. Still, it’s a multi-functional serum you’ll look forward to using every day.

Lots of beard oils promise to get flyaways under control, yet those rogue hairs keep popping up. So, what do you do? Instead of trying yet another oil, try reaching for a product that’s more of a leave-in conditioner for your beard. We’ve previously praised Zeus’s beard brushes, but the California-based brand also makes a beard serum that deserves a spot in your bathroom cabinet. I love that this beard oil has the simplest possible ingredients list: sweet almond oil, soybean oil, argan seed oil, trihydroxystearin, avocado oil, and fragrance.

The only multi-syllable ingredient there is a simple thickening agent, which gives this refined beard oil a gel-like consistency. That thicker formula will also put flyaways in their place. The very subtle fragrance is more Mediterranean than winter pine forest (we tested the Verbena Lime version), but you can also opt for a fragrance-free formula if you have sensitive skin or allergies. The plastic bottle uses an air pump, letting you get every last drop of oil. For clean freaks who hate the messiness and spillage that always comes with beard oil application, this is another bonus.

Tired of being left out of couple’s skincare night just because those stupid sheet masks won’t hold onto your glorious beard? Cardon’s got you, boo. Actually, this bead oil hack is perfect for guys who only sparingly use beard oil on an as-needed basis.

The bamboo and charcoal sheet mask is specially shaped for guys with beards, and each mask comes with a dose of beard oil made with argan and jojoba oil. In the Venn diagram of Korean skincare and beard care products, this sheet mask-beard oil duo is the tiny, microscopic bit of overlap in the middle.

The best best oil should support your skin and your beard, and Baxter of California's sweet-smelling oil doesn't sacrifice one for the other. In his guide to the best beard care products, GQ's Timothy Beck Werth said that “In our testing, no beard oil absorbs as quickly or spreads as evenly as this one.” Squalane helps nourish the most overlooked skin on your face, while avocado oil, vitamin E, and botanical extracts smooth out your beard.

As with anything you put on your skin, the ingredients are what matter most. And with beard oils, there are two categories of ingredients: carrier oils and essential oils. No two ingredients will perform exactly the same. Some will help with very specific situations or conditions; some may actively aggravate your skin. Essential oils in particular can aggravate skin if used by themselves. So, consider the following carrier oils and essential oils, favored both by myself and Alyssa Bachowski, a senior barber at Fellow Barber’s Williamsburg, NYC barbershop.

Carrier oils—often vegetable and see oils—make up the bulk of these beard oil formulas, bringing beard nourishment and skin hydration. The most popular carrier oils are argan oil and jojoba oil (both of which Bachowski recommends), though there are dozens of varieties, from castor to marula to grape seed oils. They're usually unscented, though some—like coconut oil—may carry an aroma. Because carrier oils make up the “base” of the oil, they should be among the first to appear in a product's formula or list of ingredients.

“Jojoba oil is non-comedogenic, which means it will not clog your pores. It is also a lighter oil that is absorbent, so it won't just sit on top of the hair and give you a greasy appearance,” Bachowski notes. Along with argan oil, you’re going to see a lot of jojoba oil in most popular beard oil blends.

“Argan oil is filled with a ton of vitamins and minerals, as well as linoleic acid, sterolins, and squalene. All of these aid in protecting and keeping your skin and beard healthy,” Bachowski says.

“Castor oil is known for promoting beard hair growth,” says Bachowski. It’s hypothetically the best beard-growth oil of the crew, because it acts like a vitamin for your strands—though there's more on that below in the FAQs. But don't expect it to grow more hairs, or thicker hairs. Castor oil does have a smoke-and-mirrors benefit, at least. “It is a heavier oil and dark in color, so it can give the appearance of a denser beard on men with dark facial hair,” Bachowski says.

Grape seed oil can help tone down your skin's oil production, and is extremely moisturizing for scruff and skin alike.

Rich in fatty acids, this is a frontrunner for “most nourishing oil” for your skin and whiskers.

Yes, shea butter is in fact a carrier oil, and it has supremely moisturizing properties that make it great for detangling scraggly beards and softening stiff hairs.

Essential oils are distillations from more potent sources, and they tend to be additive—and active—ingredients in a beard oil. These oils are typically extracted through steaming methods, and their range of benefits can include antimicrobial properties, oil-toning abilities, and soothing superpowers. Typically, any natural fragrance in a beard oil will come from essential oils. But pure essential oils can be extremely damaging to the skin.

“You should not apply pure essential oil to a beard—it will do the opposite of what you are trying to achieve,” Bachowski says. “These [pure oils] are not good for your beard nor your skin. as they can be irritating and harmful. Essential oils can cause dryness, itchiness, and redness—and some people may have allergic reactions to them.” This is exactly why you want beard oils from trusted, tested brand.

Bachowski likes cedarwood oil for its naturally astringent properties. “It helps cleanse the pores and prevent ingrown hairs,” she adds. It also adds a natural fragrance.

“Sandalwood has anti-inflammatory properties that help reduce any skin irritation,” Bachowski says of this deliciously scented oil.

Lavender helps calm inflammation and redness, says Bachowski. Plus it brings lavender's soothing scent.

This antimicrobial, anti-inflammatory, oil-toning ingredient is terrific for acne-prone faces and oily skin types.

Along with its refreshing scent, grapefruit peel oil helps nourish and detangle.

As GQ's lead grooming product reviewer, I've tried just about every beard oil in the biz. The ones chosen for this roundup excel at protecting and moisturizing both skin and hair, with minimal risk for things like inverse reactions and blemishes. They absorb cleanly, unless they expressly choose not to (like those which expressly use silicone), and they smell terrific (with both natural and artificially scented options). I've tested beard oils for a decade, on a beard that's been long, short, and everything in between. I've also scoured online reviews to confirm that these products are backed by throngs of loyal fans, whose collective opinion echoes my own experiences.

What is the best beard oil according to GQ?

With more than a decade of testing beard oils under his belt, GQ’s expert tester chose Honest Amish as the best beard oil. And 29,000 Amazon shoppers have rated this same product 4.5 out of 5 stars.

What is the difference between beard balm vs. oil?

Both beard balm and beard oil are both terrific for facial hair—but they don't do the same job. Beard balm is optimal for taming and styling facial hair, especially at medium and longer lengths. Beard oil provides less control over the shape and style, but is excellent at softening and conditioning the hairs and the skin underneath (preventing beard itch, beard dandruff, and split ends).

After using a beard wash, most beard oils are applied the same way: “Start off with 2-3 drops in the palm of your hand,” says Bachowski. “Rub it into your hands so that it is evenly distributed from fingertips to the heel of your hand. Start by rubbing the oil with your fingertips onto the skin beneath the beard hair, then eventually working the oil from root to tip of the actual beard. Make sure to also apply oil to your neck and cheeks where you may have shaved or trimmed as well. If you feel like the 2-3 drops wasn't enough based on the length or density of your beard, apply another 2-3 drops.”

Preventing beard dandruff starts with changing your skincare regimen. Namely, introducing exfoliation and moisturizing products that can get past a thick nest of facial hair. For exfoliation, look for physical scrubs (even a beard brush will work). For the best moisturizing effects, be sure to apply beard oil both morning and night. The beard oil will also soften and condition the facial hair to prevent itchiness.

No, beard oil is not meant to help beard hair growth, whether that’s to make facial hair thicker or grow faster. To be honest, we don’t know of much that can really change your scruff's density besides a beard transplant and maybe minoxidil. Beard oil is here to make your existing beard hair softer and more cooperative, while also nourishing the skin beneath the beard—skin that’s often prone to dehydration and dandruff. It’s already doing a lot.

Absolutely. Beard oil is a great investment for anyone with facial hair, since it can help soften and tame your scruff while simultaneously moisturizing the skin underneath. This means no more itching, no flyaways, and no beard dandruff.

Alyssa Bachowski is a senior barber at Fellow Barber’s Williamsburg NYC barbershop location.

#6800. Hunter Schafer Revisits Her Life in Looks, From ‘Euphoria’ to the Red Carpet

When it comes to red carpet dressing, few have as much fun—and take as many risks—as Hunter Schafer. The actor, who has been promoting her new series Blade Runner 2099, brings a sense of drama and experimentation to the Hollywood scene—often thanks to her right-hand stylist, Dara. So what better time than now to have 27-year-old Schafer revisit her already bountiful life in looks?

In her new Vogue video, Schafer takes a look back at some of her most memorable red carpet looks—and there were plenty to choose from. But even before she was shutting down the step and repeat, she was busy serving on the catwalks as a model. The video begins by revisiting some of her earliest shows, like walking for R13 and Marc Jacobs back in 2018. “I remember being very excited to do runway, because I’d grown up watching fashion shows and being obsessed with the models and their street style,” says Schafer, who donned an avant-garde, wide-brimmed hat on the Jacobs runway.

Since then, Schafer has brought the same fierce attitude to her red carpet approaches. In 2019, when she rose to fame thanks to her role as Jules in Euphoria, Schafer wore a gray Rodarte lace and floral-appliquéd dress that instantly cemented her as a style star to watch. “It felt so appropriate for my first carpet,” says Schafer. “It felt youthful.” Schafer has only continued to up the ante—and now totally commits to treating dress up as an artform. Who could forget, for instance, her itty-bitty feathered Ann Demeulemeester bra top and ball skirt she wore to the 2024 Vanity Fair Oscar’s Party? “This was a new era for me,” reflects Schafer. “I remember seeing this look come down the runway. I thought it was so fierce. I still think it’s insane that I wore this to an Oscars after-party.”

More recently, some of Schafer’s most noteworthy style moments have included her gauzy pink 2024 Golden Globes dress by Prada. “The organza was super long—it was essentially a train—and it so happened to be a very windy day,” recalls Schafer. “The wind picked up all the organza, and floated around me. I was really feeling my oats. The photos were awesome!” She also reminisced on her Met Gala after-party look from this year’s festivities, by Steve O Smith. “We had a fun, long night,” says Schafer of the wearing the look. “I met Connor Storrie that night and we danced together to Donna Summer’s ‘I Feel Love.’” Consider us jealous.

#6799. How To Roast Butternut Squash

Roasting is my favorite way to prepare butternut squash, hands down. The high, fast heat of the oven brings out the squash’s natural sweetness and eliminates excess moisture, concentrating all that flavor into a smaller package. Once roasted, the squash is ready to be pureed into soups, tossed into grain bowls—or simply eaten by the forkful. While many roasted butternut squash recipes restrict seasonings to just salt and pepper, I’m going a little extra here. Yes, there is a basic recipe for simply seasoned squash if you want to puree it into soup or use it as the base for a creamy orzo skillet, but I’ve also given you a few other options to try if you want to give the squash more personality. The garlic-herb variation is just begging to be tossed into a salad as “squash croutons,” and the sweet ’n’ spicy is so good I almost ate it all, straight off the baking sheet.

For the basic roasted butternut squash recipe, preheat the oven to 425° with the rack in the middle position. Line a large baking sheet with foil to make for easier cleanup.

Spread the cubed squash out onto the foil-lined sheet, drizzle with olive oil, and sprinkle with salt, then use a flexible rubber spatula to toss the squash until everything is evenly incorporated. Spread the squash back out into an even layer, making sure the pieces are not crowding each other. (If they’re too close together, they’ll steam and you’ll lose some of that great caramelization.) Season the squash with freshly ground black pepper.

Roast the squash, undisturbed, until tender and lightly caramelized on the bottom, 25 to 30 minutes. Remove the baking sheet from the oven and let the squash cool slightly before removing.

For the sweet ’n’ spicy variation, preheat the oven to 425°, with the rack in the middle position. Line a large baking sheet with foil.

In a large bowl, toss together the squash and olive oil. (I know it might seem weird to use a bowl here when we’re just tossing on the baking sheet for the basic version, but this is because the spices tend to stick to the baking sheet and burn while the squash is roasting.) Sprinkle the garam masala, salt, sweet paprika, cumin, and cayenne over the squash, and toss everything until evenly coated. Finally, drizzle the spiced squash with the honey and toss to combine.

Spread the seasoned squash out on the foil-lined baking sheet, making sure the pieces don’t crowd each other too much. Roast, undisturbed, until tender and caramelized on the bottom, 25 to 30 minutes. (I recommend checking at the 20-minute mark to make sure the pieces of squash on the outside of the baking sheet are not caramelizing too fast on the bottom. If they are, just flip the pieces that are getting dark on the bottom.) Remove the baking sheet from the oven and let the squash cool slightly before removing.

For the garlic-herb variation, preheat the oven to 425°, with the rack in the middle position. Line a large baking sheet with foil.

Spread the cubed squash out onto the foil-lined sheet, drizzle with olive oil, and sprinkle with salt, then use a flexible rubber spatula to toss the squash until everything is evenly incorporated. Spread the squash back out into an even layer, making sure the pieces are not crowding each other.

Roast the squash, undisturbed, until it’s just beginning to be tender (about 20 minutes), then remove the baking sheet from the oven and sprinkle with the garlic, thyme, and sage, then return the baking sheet to the oven and roast until the garlic is golden and the squash is tender and lightly caramelized on the bottom, about 10 minutes more. Remove the baking sheet from the oven and let the squash cool slightly before removing.

The full list of ingredients and instructions can be found in the recipe below.

Roasted butternut squash is wonderful fresh, but it also makes good meal prep! If you’d like to make it ahead of time, store in an airtight container in the refrigerator for up to 3 days.

Nutrition (per serving): 135 calories, 2 g protein, 19 g carbohydrates, 6 g fiber, 4 g sugar, 7 g fat, 1 g saturated fat, 487 mg sodium

Bakeware and cookware courtesy of Crate & Barrel.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we back.

#6798. How Will Robert Pattinson’s James Bond Dress?

When Terence Young, the director of Dr. No, needed a suit for Sean Connery to wear for his debut as James Bond, he took him to see his personal Savile Row tailor, Anthony Sinclair.

“Connery’s suits were considered relatively contemporary, because Anthony always had a very soft shoulder on his suits,” explains Elliot Mason, the director of business at Anthony Sinclair, which is no longer located on Savile Row, but still makes classic British suiting. “Anthony liked to nip in the waist, so it gave that real hourglass silhouette to the suit, which really is quite timeless. And as was the trend at the time, the Connery suits all had a relatively high rise, double-pleated with English forward pleats, and most of his trousers had Daks button-tab adjusters on the side.”

Opting to dress James Bond in a Savile Row suit might seem like a no-brainer in hindsight, but the decision—and the cut of the suits themselves—still resonates more than 60 years later. Sinclair would make all of Connery’s suits during his nine-year run as James Bond, and his blend of classic British tailoring and of-the-moment influences would create a template for every Bond to come. With Robert Pattinson—a Dior ambassador and a guy with a reputation for adventurous style—chosen to take up the mantle in Bond 26, the question now becomes: what will he wear?

Tailor Anthony Sinclair fitting Sean Connery for one of the suits he wore in 1963’s From Russia With Love.

Ian Fleming, the author who created James Bond, spent a lot of time talking about what Agent 007 drank (Wolfschmidt Vodka) what he drove (Bentley convertibles, for the most part) and how he made his coffee (he was a dedicated pour-over guy), but, surprisingly, he never specified where the spy bought his clothes. As a result, for the last six decades, it’s been up to the directors and costume designers of the 25 previous films to create a new look for every successive Bond. In the Connery and Moore eras it was Savile Row suits. Timothy Dalton’s Bond wore Paul Smith. Brosnan was a bespoke Brioni guy, as was Daniel Craig, who also brought Tom Ford into the mix. At 6’1” to Craig’s 5’10”, with a leaner build, and a long track record of fashion-forward dressing, Pattinson has everything needed to reinvent Bond once again.

“I’m pretty sure we’re going to see something quite different from Pattinson's Bond,” says Matt Spaiser, the tailoring obsessive behind BondSuits.com and the author of From Tailors with Love: An Evolution of Menswear Through the Bond Films. “Pattinson is a different kind of actor, and he’s going to wear something that moves differently. Plus, he’s 18 years younger than Daniel Craig.”

The actor playing Bond has a major influence on how he dresses—Daniel Craig, an adventurous dresser himself, was very hands-on with the wardrobe in his films—but Pattinson’s ongoing contract with Dior could also factor in. “Some of his Dior looks look somewhat like James Bond—he wears a great tuxedo—but I don’t think it’s going to tell us what he’s going to wear as James Bond,” Spaiser says. “He’s worn suits from other brands in other roles—like in Tenet, he wasn’t wearing a Dior suit—so I don’t think he's necessarily obligated to be wearing Dior as James Bond.”

Daniel Craig, seen here in 2019 with then-Prince Charles on the set of No Time to Die, mainly sported sharp Tom Ford suiting in his later Bond films.

Another important factor that contributes to a new Bond’s look is what’s happening in fashion whenever the film eventually gets made. “One thing that we've seen throughout most of the Bond eras is that he dresses in suits that are fairly traditional, but there's always some nod to the era that they’re in,” says Spaiser. “Sometimes they lean a little more into fashion, like when Roger Moore was wearing flares. That was something that I think they couldn't avoid without making Bond look too old-fashioned at the time.”

Tone also plays an important role, and historically has ping-ponged between serious (Connery, Dalton, Craig) and more campy (Moore, Brosnan), but with Denis Villeneuve, the auteur behind Sicario, Blade Runner: 2049, and Dune in the director’s chair, the smart money isn’t on hovercraft gondolas and punny Bond girl names. A darker, more atmospheric milieu could make for an interesting complement to Pattinson’s taste for high-fashion.

If Mason were dressing Pattinson for the role, he says, he’d take a lead from his clientele, many of whom, like Bond, are well-heeled Londoners with a taste for expensive sports cars and well mixed cocktails. “The majority of our customers are reasonably conservative, and when it comes to fit, they’re sensitive to current trends,” Mason notes. As such, he’d opt for longer jackets, both single- and double-breasted, that are fuller through the body, and straight-leg pleated trousers. “It’s not that very, very slim fit that we'd seen for so many years, but it still needs to be refined,” he says. “I don’t think it would be appropriate to put him in wide-leg or baggy trousers. I just don't think it’s right for Bond.”

Robert Pattinson, the latest 007, has been the face of Dior’s menswear for over a decade.

Given the intense scrutiny on every relaunch of the Bond franchise, and the tendency of fans to get worked up online over perceived mishandling of a beloved character, Pattinson, Villeneuve, and the film’s yet-to-be-announced costume designers have little room for error. Go too conservative and they’ll be accused of playing it safe; make Bond into a fashion guy, and they’ll incur the wrath of the traditionalists.

“He needs to look current, but also timeless,” Spaiser says. “It’s a hard balance to find.”

#6797. The Best Bomber Jackets for Women—And How to Wear Them in 2026

All products featured on Vogue are independently selected by our editors. However, we may earn affiliate revenue on this article and commission when you buy something.

When it comes to layering for the cold-weather season, bomber jackets are always a go-to: The classic silhouette is as versatile as it is stylish, a forever favorite that withstands the trend cycle. From leather to suede, techy nylon to faux-fur trimmings, the bomber jacket exudes an understated cool factor.

As seen in the latest street style looks during Fashion Week in Paris, Milan, and New York, it’s clear that the jacket has staying power. It makes complete sense: They’re an easy staple for dressing up or down. To style, pair them with jeans for a casual boyish look, or throw them over a dainty dress for an unexpected mix of hard and soft textures and layers.

Below, an edit of our favorite bomber jackets—with ideas on how to wear each.

You can’t go wrong with a classic leather bomber. City-slickers will love Róhe’s oxblood version, paired with Emily Dawn Long’s shawl-scarf and corduroys from The Row for a cozy yet stylish seasonal look.

Arma’s suede bomber covers all the bases: It’s versatile for day-to-night wear and the rib-trim adds the right amount of structure to this sweater-and-skirt look. The finishing touch? Paris Texas’s patent knee-highs, which share a name with the desert-set Wim Wenders film).

The latest jacket trend has taken a stand, literally. The stand-up collar sends a strong statement with Balenciaga’s Thriller-inspired look. Pair with white barrel-leg pants and fanciful accessories like Savette’s bejeweled pouch and Khaite’s fringe loafer for a touch of whimsy.

Flights of fancy may take hold with Nour Hammour’s aviator-style, shearling collar bomber. Pair it with Maria McManus’s opera scarf and Rùadh’s oversized turtleneck for a winter look that doesn’t sacrifice style for warmth.

Channel Jean Luc-Godard’s film, Masculin Féminin, with this look that blends masculine toughness in Staud’s distressed leather bomber with the delicate femininity of If Only If’s dainty nightgown. Pair with Vibi Venezia’s girlish flats and Malene Birger’s lipstick necklace to complete the look.

The canvas and wool bomber from LA-based brand Cobra S.C. is an easy throw-on style with an oversized fit and impeccable tailoring. Pair with a printed waffle shirt from Emily Dawn Long, perfect straight-leg Mother jeans and sporty-chic flats for an everyday, errand-ready look.

This draped, batwing style from Louis Vuitton serves as testament to the recent nylon bomber trend—a more fluid approach to the ’80s look that feels fresh for the season. Pair with barrel-leg military-style pants from Deiji Studios and deer-print ankle booties from Loulou de Saison for an outfit that plays with volumes and textures.

#6796. Where Is Caroline Ellison, Portrayed by Julia Garner in The Altruists, Now?

The cryptocurrency executive spent 11 months in prison.

This story contains spoilers for The Altruists.

Netflix’s The Altruists tells the true story of FTX, the celebrated cryptocurrency exchange, and the trading firm Alameda Research, which both collapsed amid accusations of wrongdoing by executives. Anthony Boyle and Julia Garner play Sam Bankman-Fried, who founded the company, and Caroline Ellison, the co-CEO of Alameda, respectively. The two, who met while working for the New York City firm Jane Street, are said to have been a couple at one point. Both were sent to prison on charges of fraud, with Ellison having been released this year. Here’s what you need to know about where she is now.

A native of Massachusetts and a Stanford grad, Ellison joined Alameda as a trader in 2018. She was promoted to co-CEO in 2021.

Ellison was sentenced to 24 months in prison in 2024 after pleading guilty to seven charges, including wire fraud and money laundering. Prosecutors praised Ellison for her cooperation, which included testifying against Bankman-Fried. “In her many meetings with the government, Ellison approached her cooperation with remarkable candor, remorse and seriousness…and she persevered despite harsh media and public scrutiny and Bankman-Fried’s efforts to publicly weaponize her personal writings to discredit and intimidate her,” they wrote.

Ellison served about 11 months in a Connecticut prison before being moved to what was referred to as community confinement, which typically means being under house arrest or living in a halfway house. She was released in January 2026.

While in college, Ellison became interested in effective altruism. The movement, which is popular within tech circles, urges people to use evidence to make decisions that will yield the most good for society. That can mean choosing to take a high-paying salary rather than volunteering, believing that the money earned will provide more benefit.

The nonprofit Manifund, a grant-funding organization that was inspired by the effective altruism movement and had deep ties to FTX, hired Ellison. The nonprofit’s CEO, Austin Chen, announced in September that she had begun working over the summer. “I believe in redemption. Caroline has admitted her faults, worked to make creditors whole, and served her time in prison. I’d like to give her the opportunity to contribute back to the world; I hope that the world will concur,” he wrote.

Chen quoted Ellison in his announcement. “I’m deeply sorry for what I did, and I completely understand why people may not want to work with me. But I’m very grateful to Austin for giving me a second chance, and judging me on my current work performance rather than my past. I feel very lucky not only to have a job, but to be working somewhere that I think is doing great work and has a mission I believe in,” she wrote.

#6794. 15 Best Zip-Up Hoodies for Men 2026, According to GQ Editors

Surveying the best zip-up hoodies for men takes me back to my younger days as a hopelessly romantic high school kid. My formative years were marred by angst, soundtracked by pop punk, emo, and indie rock. The piece of clothing that got me through it all? A zip-up hoodie. It was core to the indie aesthetic, with every forlorn frontman sporting one over a thrashed band tee, skinny jeans, and shredded Chucks. But nostalgia never seems to stay in the past, especially when fashion is involved.

Gen-Z has revived every minute piece of 2000s pop culture, which means zip-up hoodies are back and have all but usurped the pullover hoodie as the sweat du jour. The zip-up is inherently versatile in both practical wear and sheer styling dexterity. With the flick of the zip, you can put your beloved Death Cab for Cutie tee on full display while letting your pits breathe, or keep it zipped up when it's just a bit too nippy out. To find the best zip-up hoodies on the market, I dusted off Transatlanticism and went on a deep dive. The good news is that the old classics are still good. The better news is that there's a lot of great competition to choose from.

Uniqlo’s no-nonsense clean aesthetic and low price point has made it a go-to for any menswear enjoyer looking for good style on a budget. While the brand excels at cashmere sweaters, T-shirts, and a slew of homerun collabs, the Japanese label’s sweats are criminally underrated. We’d go so far as to say that they should be talked in the same breath as brands like Champion and Sunspel. This zip up hoodie took us by surprise thanks to its comfy midweight, all-cotton fabric and solid construction. But the main selling point is the impeccable fit.

So many other brands cut their hoodies too long in the body and leave you looking like Jack Skellington’s pubescent brother. Uniqlo managed to cut its French terry hoodie to sit right at the hips which makes for a more classic and natural silhouette that’s neither lanky nor sloppy. Because of the fabric, the feel, and the overall fit, this is my favorite zip-up hoodie by a sizable margin. It’s my most-worn hoodie and has yet to show signs of any fraying.

Hoodies were originally meant for sportswear, as warm up gear before a grueling game of football. These days, they’re more often worn for lounging on the couch to watch the kickoff. And if you want to kick it in comfort while saving your pennies for booze and amuse bouche for the big game with the boys, the best budget zip up hoodie to buy is Hanes’ EcoSmart zip hoodie. The fit is spot on—relaxed, but not sloppy, comfy in the body and the sleeves without swallowing you like a sand pit.

The fit is impressive for the price, and the overall quality means you’re getting a little more than what you pay for. It’s not a hoodie that’ll last you for too long, but it is a good quality piece for just 15 bucks. And when other hoodies offer lesser quality and worse-fitting options for about the same price? The answer is clear.

It’s hard not to sing the praises of Reigning Champ, a brand that built its fleece empire on sweatpants and hoodies. The Canadian label’s flagship midweight terry fabric has drawn the attention of streetwear royalty and menswear buffs alike and its dedication to quality has elevated the humble hoodie to oxygen-deprived heights. We named Reigning Champ’s crewneck sweatshirt and pullover hoodie as among our favorites in their respective categories, and the same goes for its zip-up hooded sweatshirt. It’s made of the same 100% cotton French terry fabric as its superlative-earning brethren and is cut with high-quality flatlock stitching for a smooth, un-bulky feel.

I had high expectations for Reigning Champ, given my previous experience, and I’m not surprised at all with how nice this zip-up is. And while I'm partial to the standard fit, it’s also available in a slim fit version, too.

Hoodies live in a universe well beyond the sportswear world from which they originated. And while we still enjoy the OGs and the classics that made the hoodie a bona fide menswear must-have, we also love seeing all the ways the style has been pushed, tweaked, and freaked. Our Legacy's Level Hood has been a successful avenue for the Stockholm-based brand to explore the limits of the hoodie and this particular version strikes a delicate balance between the freaky and the fashion-curious. It's made of an English wool knit into an all-over ribbed texture for a throwback '90s feel. The cropped, boxy silhouette and elongated sleeves harken back to the grunge-y era, while the sultry dual-tone nightshade colorway throws you off in the best way possible.

Menswear influencers will be happy to regale you with their TedTalk on the golden ratio of clothing proportions. Essentially, that means your outfit should be divided into three equal sections where the top takes up one third of the outfit. Basically, your tops should be cropped and sit closer to your natural waist. Follow it up with some high-waisted pants with a wide silhouette and you’ve instantly garnered 300K views on TikTok (RIP). With so many indie brands and influencers scrambling to make their own versions of “the perfect cropped hoodie” you’re probably tired of wading through all the choices. Do yourself a favor and go to Abercrombie.

The Moose’s version is backed by actual trend forecasters, actual designers, and actual fit models. What does that mean, exactly? It means that its cropped zip hoodie fits really well and uses surprisingly beefy fabric. It was a revelation for me, too—Abercrombie wasn’t a name I associated with hoodies, but after I saw this in person, I had to readjust my menswear word cloud.

Some of the best zip-up hoodies for men are made by a brand you never see in brick and mortar stores. Camber has been crafting the most durable sweats for decades, right here in America and have been the shining example of the difference between good quality and great quality. Sure, you have to order through an old-school web 1.0 website and wait for several weeks to actually get your hoodie. But trust us when we say that the relatively minor inconvenience is completely worth it.

The heavyweight fleece is almost as thick as a leather jacket and can stand in as outerwear if you wanted it to. While other hoodies are washed to hell in giant industrial washing machines to reach a super soft texture by the time it gets to you, Camber’s actually require you to do the work yourself. It sort of feels like you’re being swaddled by an anaconda, which means if you’re a proponent of hard clothes like raw denim, you’ll like Camber’s hoodie a lot.

Cashmere zip hoodies, once the exclusive province of Silicon Valley unicorn founders and PJ-flying nepo babies, have been democratized, none better than Naadam's take. You know the brand for bringing thoughtful, sustainable cashmere pieces at “really, only that much?” prices. Its zip hoodie is as soft as it gets, with extra credit points awarded for coming in a classic fit (too many cashmere hoodies err on the side of slim) and six colors, including this decidedly handsome olive. Wear it on weekends, work-from-home Fridays, and long flights—even if it's just Economy+.

Shoutout to brother in arms Louis Cheslaw, GQ Recs contributor, whose writing for the newsletter Magasin put us on to young brand Cease. From seed to sewing, it's beefy 20-ounce cotton is fully American-made. Assembling the fabric vertically means the hoodie will shorten up to a slightly cropped, oversized fit. Act fast if you want some of the less-boring colors—they move fast.

We can’t deny Russell Athletic’s contributions to the hoodie space. And while its formula has changed over the years, the new hoodies are still pretty good for the price. The fabric is soft and lightweight, and it’s available in a rainbow of colorways. Plus, if you’re looking for a hoodie with a little more length, this is a great option.

Bold graphics aren’t usually deemed classics, but Gap’s retro-leaning collegiate-style block logo has staying power almost as much as the Levi’s red tab. Add to that a wonderfully fluffy midweight fleece material and a tailored straight fit and you’ve got something you’ll see on moodboards for generations to come.

Champion’s Reverse Weave fabric is iconic for its nearly shrink-proof quality and almost unsettlingly rough exterior. Flip it to the inside and you’ve got a luxuriously brushed interior that’ll have you feeling like you’re melting into a cloud. Heads-up that pricing varies kinda wildly by size on Amazon.

The secondhand market is chock full of navy-blue hoodies that have been sun-faded and beaten down with time. They'll cost you just as much as this Mfpen version, but will also fall apart faster than you can zip 'em up.

Les Tien's dedication to all things sweats results in a catalog of luxurious fleece and terry cloth with a modern approach. Featuring high-end Cobrax hardware, RiRi zippers, raw-edge hems and sleeves, and a heavyweight brushed fleece, no detail is phoned in.

The Real McCoy's is considered by many to be Americana repro wizards. This revival of an early 20th-Century hoodie is just one small piece of the brand's preservation prowess. It's made using beefy sinker-knit fleece, old-school Talon-style zippers, and vertical patch pockets.

A.Presse's chokehold on the menswear illuminati refuses to let up and it's pieces like this vintage-inspired hoodie that keep the pressure on. Based on very rare “after-hood parkas", this made-in-Japan hoodie features an idiosyncratic attached hood, super dense and lofty fleece, with artfully distressed details throughout.

Think of the zip-up as the pullover hoodie's elder brother, but with way more layering potential. In its most natural state, it can cuddle up to a matching set of sweatpants and a baggy tee with flair. (Top it off with a swanky necklace, crisp sneakers, and some stellar shades and you’re left with a fit on par with Shai Gilgeous-Alexander at his tunnel-trotting best.)

Unzipped and left open, it can also take on the role of a picture frame ready to display your sickest graphic tee—much like the cardigan, another treasure of 2000s fashion. Or make like a mid-Aughts garage band ironically donning corporate attire and wear your zip-up over a dress shirt and baggy tailored trousers, a high-minded fashion move with a jolt of kooky Scandinavian cool.

Style is subjective, we know—that’s the fun of it. But we’re serious about helping our audience get dressed. Whether it’s the best white sneakers, the flyest affordable suits, or the need-to-know menswear drops of the week, GQ Recommends’ perspective is built on years of hands-on experience, an insider awareness of what’s in and what’s next, and a mission to find the best version of everything out there, at every price point.

Our staffers aren’t able to try on every single piece of clothing you read about on GQ.com (fashion moves fast these days), but we have an intimate knowledge of each brand’s strengths and know the hallmarks of quality clothing—from materials and sourcing, to craftsmanship, to sustainability efforts that aren’t just greenwashing. GQ Recommends heavily emphasizes our own editorial experience with those brands, how they make their clothes, and how those clothes have been reviewed by customers. Bottom line: GQ wouldn’t tell you to wear it if we wouldn’t.

We make every effort to cast as wide of a net as possible, with an eye on identifying the best options across three key categories: quality, fit, and price.

To kick off the process, we enlist the GQ Recommends braintrust to vote on our contenders. Some of the folks involved have worked in retail, slinging clothes to the masses; others have toiled for small-batch menswear labels; all spend way too much time thinking about what hangs in their closets.

We lean on that collective experience to guide our search, culling a mix of household names, indie favorites, and the artisanal imprints on the bleeding-edge of the genre. Then we narrow down the assortment to the picks that scored the highest across quality, fit, and price.

Across the majority of our buying guides, our team boasts firsthand experience with the bulk of our selects, but a handful are totally new to us. So after several months of intense debate, we tally the votes, collate the anecdotal evidence, and emerge with a list of what we believe to be the absolute best of the category right now, from the tried-and-true stalwarts to the modern disruptors, the affordable beaters to the wildly expensive (but wildly worth-it) designer riffs.

Whatever your preferences, whatever your style, there's bound to be a superlative version on this list for you. (Read more about GQ's testing process here.)

#6793. The Best Shearling Shoes for Cold-Weather Style and Comfort

Cozy, polished pairs to wear all winter long.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

There’s a reason shearling keeps coming back every cold season. It’s the rare material that manages to be cozy and stylish at once, with a plush, cloud-like lining that wraps your feet in warmth and a rich texture that instantly makes an outfit feel more intentional. What began as practical gear for sheepherders and pilots has become a fixture of modern closets, showing up on everything from chic boots to sleek slippers, ballet flats, and fashion-forward sneakers.

Part of its staying power is function. Shearling is naturally insulating, breathable, and moisture-wicking, so your feet stay warm without overheating. Versatility is another reason why shearling has become so popular. The same material that elevates a pair of jeans and a sweater on a weekend errand run can make lounging at home feel like a small luxury.

Whether you’re hunting for the perfect slip-on to kick off at the door, a boot to carry you through winter, or a statement piece that turns heads, below are the must-have shearling shoes that earn a spot in your rotation this season.

Worn with tailored trousers, a slouchy knit, or bare ankles and a long wool coat, the shearling ballet flat moves effortlessly between the polished and the pared-back. The easiest way to make winter feel a little more cozy and luxurious.

Le Monde Beryl Apres Ski shearling-lined suede ballet flats

The loafer, softened for winter. A shearling lining lends quiet warmth to a classic silhouette, proving that heritage tailoring and true comfort can share the same shoe.

#6792. Taylor! Lainey! Ella! Kacey! Megan! Backstage at the CMA Awards

“Little Lainey would be losin’ her mind if she knew she was about to be hosting the 60th Annual CMA Awards,” Lainey Wilson said ahead of country music’s biggest night at Nashville’s Bridgestone Arena. Indeed, when Vogue caught up with the chart-topper in a quiet moment leading up to the show, she was incredulous about landing the gig.

“I’m still that little girl from a town of 200 people in Louisiana who just grew up dreamin’ about being a small part of this world of country music,” Wilson said in her signature twang. The feeling of going from the middle of nowhere to the center of the ceremony, and in such a milestone year, is nothing short of surreal, she added. Not only was it the show’s six-decade anniversary and a mega year for the genre, but the night was also set to include a tribute to the late Dolly Parton—and the first CMA performance in 13 years from a certain recently wed crossover superstar.

“I’m just excited to celebrate the ones before, the ones now, and the ones comin’,” Wilson said. “I think the biggest thing for me is being present and being right where my feet are. It’s tryin’ to find that balance.”

On the day of the show, Downtown Nashville was a flurry of activity, fans, hot chicken, and road closures as the capital of country music prepared to step into the national spotlight. The recent FAA ground stop gave some out-of-towners agita, but the delay clouds thankfully parted in time. Music was ringing out from every bar and restaurant on Broadway, most of the time in the form of Langley’s ubiquitous “Choosin’ Texas.” The country queen of 2026 was also nominated for almost every award imaginable. It’s to the point where if Langley miraculously cloned herself, they’d be nominated for Best Duo, too.

“This isn’t a fad: everyone you know can sing along to every word of ‘Choosin’ Texas,’” Brittany Schaffer, the incoming Country Music Association CEO, told Vogue. Just hours earlier, Schaffer bolted out of her hotel at 6 a.m. to help give out the first award of the day live on Good Morning America. (Naturally, “Choosin’ Texas” won for Best Music Video.) Schaffer was also busy contending with the Taylor of it all, as her CMA comeback was a closely held secret.

“A small group of us knew for quite a bit of time in advance of the announcement,” Schaffer revealed with a wink. “Obviously, with this being an anniversary, she is such an important part of country music history, so we certainly started that conversation with her early on. We are just thrilled that she was so excited to join.”

While most major red carpets are hectic affairs, the vibe at Music City Center was decidedly chiller—even when an Oscar de la Renta-clad Swift arrived. A hush fell over the space as phones lifted to capture the moment, even that of the lighting technician. Elsewhere, VIPs tucked into fried green tomato sandwiches and sipped whiskey, seemingly unfazed.

“I had to bring my sister, Leslie,” actor Glen Powell, who’s set to play a country music star in next year’s The Comeback King, told Vogue. “ To share this together…I mean, we’ve been country fans our entire lives. We did these long road trips to the ranch, listening to country music the whole way.” A “starstruck” Leslie chimed in: “Two of our childhood idols, Faith Hill and Tim McGraw, are sitting next to us.”

As the Tennessee sun began to set, a series of Sprinter vans whisked people from the carpet to the arena for a 7 p.m. start time. Backstage, a trophy engraver was waiting to etch the names of future winners into history. Inside the arena, the crowd milled about as a booming voice on the loudspeaker urged, “In respect to Dolly, please take your seats.” It proved to be a subtle teaser, as a tribute featuring Wilson and Vince Gill singing “I Will Always Love You” was about to open the show. Later, Kacey Musgraves and Miranda Lambert lightened the mood with their honky-tonk ode “Horses and Divorces,” from Musgraves’s recent album Middle of Nowhere.

From her dressing room, Musgraves, resplendent in a custom suit by the Los Angeles-based western-wear designer RoseCut, took a breather on the couch. “I think people would be shocked to know how boring my pre-show ritual actually is,” she told Vogue. “It’s pretty chill. I don’t shut myself in a hotel room all day. I try to move and connect with the heartbeat of wherever I’m at.” Alas, a vocal doctor did warn once that she should have “no chocolate, no caffeine, or no booze before a performance. “I’m like, ‘Have you ever been on stage without booze,’” she recalled with a laugh. “So pre-show, the band and I usually do a tequila shot.”

According to Musgraves, a night like tonight is all about reconnecting. “So many of us here spend so much of our time out on the road. It’s really a reunion of sorts, getting to see a lot of people and friends that you haven’t seen in so long.”

One of those friends is Megan Moroney, whom Musgraves was happily sharing a dressing room with. Alongside excitement over her two nominations at the CMAs, Moroney is buzzing about her two recently announced Grammy nods. “It’s been such a crazy year,” Moroney told Vogue as the ceremony blared on a TV in the background. “On the morning of the nominations, my manager came into my bedroom at like 6:30 a.m. and said, ‘We need to turn on the Grammy announcement because I just have a good feeling.’ And I was like, ‘Are you insane?!’”

The show itself played out as expected. Ella Langley cleaned up, making history by winning everything from Entertainer of the Year to Female Vocalist of the Year and Album of the Year, breaking a CMA record for most wins. Even the country stalwarts were impressed. “A highlight for me was hugging Miranda Lambert,” Lady A’s Hillary Scott told Vogue of Langley’s collaborator. “What’s funny is we toured with her and Kenny Chesney twenty years ago, early on in our careers.” Her bandmate, Dave Haywood, added: “Longevity was the goal when we started. So to be here after all of these years is a dream realized for us.”

As the show wrapped up and fans departed the arena, stars grabbed snacks to munch on as they got ready to head back out on the road—including a lollipop-sucking Michael Bublé. “What I love about country music is this,” he mused to Vogue, “when you take away all of the production, all of the lights, and all of the magic tricks, you are left with good souls.”

#6790. This week: Lame duck kicks off with Hegseth impeachment effort

Departing GOP Rep. Thomas Massie of Kentucky told CQ Roll Call in late October that the Hegseth impeachment resolution would come up in the first week of the post-election lame duck session. (Bill Clark/CQ Roll Call file photo) (Bill Clark CQ Roll Call)

The lame-duck session of Congress kicks off this week as the House is expected to consider a resolution to impeach Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth while the Senate seeks to advance a bipartisan deal on permitting reform.

Last week’s midterm elections saw Democrats claim sizable majorities in both the House and Senate in the new Congress, and among the immediate questions is whether the demoralized GOP will face attendance woes.

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., the expected next speaker of the House, said Sunday that he would support the Hegseth impeachment.

“I'm a hard 'yes' on the Hegseth resolution,” Jeffries said on ABC’s “This Week.” “The so-called secretary of Defense has abused his power, undermined our national security and engineered this reckless and costly war of choice in Iran, which has skyrocketed prices on the American people and made the country less safe. Pete Hegseth has got to go.”

Departing Rep. Thomas Massie, R-Ky., told CQ Roll Call in late October that the Hegseth impeachment resolution would come up in the first week of the post-election lame duck session.

It’s also orientation week on Capitol Hill for new members of Congress. House orientation got underway Sunday.

The Senate, meanwhile, looks to take up a bipartisan permitting bill that has the backing of President Donald Trump.

“This Landmark Permitting Reform Legislation will codify our America First Energy Agenda, and ensure that we can build Pipelines, Mines, and Power Plants faster than ever before, lowering Energy Costs for American Families, and creating High Paying American Jobs,” Trump posted on Truth Social.

Senate negotiators over the weekend released text of an amendment seeking to address remaining concerns.

“I’m grateful for the input throughout this process from Senate and House colleagues, environmental and labor organizations, and Tribal leaders. Our manager’s amendment improves [the bill] and addresses the thoughtful feedback we have received,” Senate Environment and Public Works ranking Democrat Sheldon Whitehouse of Rhode Island said in a statement. “Now it’s time to pass this bill so we can lower costs, juice the clean energy economy, build big things again in America, reduce pollution, and create tons of new well-paying union jobs.”

Beyond the permitting bill, the Senate Republican majority may seek to use its remaining time trying to confirm more of Trump’s nominations before the control of the floor agenda passes to the Democrats.

"We've got the farm bill, the NDAA, and we have that third reconciliation bill that we need to get across the finish line, and there are so many things that we need to get done and buttoned up," including nominations, departing Sen. Marsha Blackburn, R-Tenn., said in a weekend Fox News interview. Blackburn, one of the Republicans who had success at the ballot box, is now governor-elect of Tennessee. But it's an open question how long lawmakers will want to stick around.

There’s also the matter of an imminent fight over funding the government and laying the groundwork for the new Congress. Republican leadership elections are scheduled for Tuesday, while Democratic leadership elections are expected in December. Democratic senators-elect are due on Capitol Hill Monday and Tuesday, according to the office of Senate Minority Leader Charles E. Schumer, D-N.Y. Schumer is widely expected to be the majority leader in 2027.

There have been some calls for Senate Republicans to delay their elections, though current Majority Leader John Thune of South Dakota has been widely expected to remain as the Republican leader.

“One of the things I learned in business and the Army is that after a failure, you have to start with honesty, transparency, and accountability,” Sen. Dave McCormick, R-Pa., said in a Sunday night statement. “As I’ve shared with Leader Thune, I don’t believe we should move to leadership elections until we have digested and debated the lessons of the midterms and how to move forward.”

Sen. Ron Johnson, R-Wis., has also been circulating a letter calling for a delay, and he has argued the election should not be held, in part, because the makeup of the Senate Republican Conference is not yet final. Senate races are still not called in Alaska and Mississippi.

Absent this week will be Sen. Christopher S. Murphy, D-Conn., who recently had a stroke.

“The doctors are very encouraged by the progress I’ve made so far. In order to take my recovery seriously and get back to work on the quickest timeline possible, I will be spending several weeks at a rehabilitation center after being discharged from the hospital today,” Murphy said in a Friday statement.
