#6788. 10 Best Eye Cream for Dark Circles of 2026, According to Experts

While some people appreciate the natural look of a shadowy under-eye, many of us hope to find a solution—which is where the best eye cream for dark circles comes in. “Not all eye creams are created equal, because not all eye problems are equal,” says board-certified dermatologist Dr. Shereene Idriss. “When a person is looking to treat dark circles, the question is: Why do you have dark circles?”

The answer is different for everyone. For some people, it’s pigment, which can be genetic or the result of tiredness. For others, the skin under the eyes is so thin that the blood vessels underneath show through as a bluish or purple cast. If you’re experiencing puffiness, that can cast its own shadow, especially in the morning. Finally, as we get older, a little volume loss where the lower lid meets the cheek can make the whole area look darker than it really is.

That’s why when it comes to your eye cream, the ingredient list matters. Vitamin C, niacinamide and licorice root are good picks for pigment. Formulas that include retinol and peptides help thin skin look firmer and less see-through over time, and caffeine is every skin expert’s go-to for puffiness. Hollowing is the one thing a cream can’t fix, but the right formula can still make the skin around it look smoother and brighter.

Below, we rounded up the best eye creams that target dark circles—according to dermatologists, estheticians, skin experts, and Vogue editors.

British Vogue’s Jessica Diner uses Medik8’s Crystal Retinal Ceramide Eye Cream.

Vogue’s Kiana Murden uses Chanel’s N° 1 De Revitalizing Eye Serum.

Vogue’s Kiana Murden uses RoC Retinol Correxion Eye Cream.

“Yes, good eye creams formulated with clinically-proven ingredients do work and can provide visible benefits to the skin,” Murphy-Rose admits. “Dark circles caused by vascular congestion can be targeted with caffeine, vitamin K, and arnica. Dark circles caused by true hyperpigmentation can be treated with retinol and vitamin C-containing eye creams that help to brighten and fade discoloration.” And those with more prominent pigmentation around the eye should look to in-office treatments alongside topical solutions. “When dark circles are caused by a loss of volume or other anatomical cause, it can be difficult to achieve a significant improvement with topical therapy alone. Sometimes in office treatments like injectable fillers will bring about the most significant improvement to help this shadowed area reflect more light.”

Do eye creams actually get rid of dark circles?

Though eye creams can soften the appearance of dark circles, they’re (alas) not a one-swipe magic wand. “Eye creams can improve the quality of the skin around your eyes, but they can’t fix every undereye concern,” says Brauer. “Eye creams mainly help to moisturize and protect the skin around your eyes—one of the most delicate areas of skin on your face. The right eye cream can help to minimize dark circles, but they won’t get rid of them permanently.” So, what to do when those dark circles crop up? Read on.

What do dermatologists recommend for dark circles?

Whether dark circles are spurred by a sleepless night, a red-eye flight, or a host of other factors, there are fixes. But your particular path to bright-eyed pertness will be personal. “A lot of what is recommended is dependent upon the underlying cause or causes of the darkness,” Brauer says. “Aging—including thinning skin, bone resorption, and fat-pad changes—sun damage, and allergies are some of the main causes of what one sees in the mirror.” To find the right eye cream for you, Brauer recommends talking it over with a pro. “It’s important to find an eye cream that works for your specific skin type and dermatologic concerns to get the best results,” he says. “The best approach is to speak with a dermatologist to determine what would work best for you.”

In addition to particular eye creams, Brauer has set his patients up with everything from lasers and injectable fillers and prescription medications (such as topical steroids) to brighten the appearance of under-eye circles. He specifies that prescriptions are generally his recommendation for those whose dark under-eye circles are a secondary cause of allergies, while under-eye circles resulting from volume loss are generally best treated with a multifaceted approach that includes injectable fillers and energy-based treatments that incorporate lasers or radiofrequency.

How often should I apply eye cream for dark circles?

To reduce existing dark circles and prevent more gaze-darkening baggage from popping up in the future, Brauer recommends applying eye cream once to twice daily, morning and night. “You can apply it as the first step in your skin-care routine,” he says—issuing a friendly reminder that no morning regime is complete without sunscreen for face. When it comes to applying the eye cream, “Be sure to use your ring finger,” he says, to lightly tap it in. “You want to be gentle when applying cream to this delicate area of skin.”

She explains that the pesky dark circles could be the result of pigmentation or underlying redness and vasculature. “If the dark circles are due to pigmentation, then you want to look for ingredients with brightening effects. Products containing vitamin C, hydroquinone, and kojic acid work best. Also, look for products with retinol in them because retinol can help a person even out pigmentation over time. However, if the dark circles are due to underlying redness or puffiness, then you want to look for eye creams with caffeine or vitamin K, which is an ‘underdog’ and not used as frequently as I wish it were.”

Like many other skin conditions, dark circles can be reduced with lifestyle changes as well. According to board-certified dermatologist Dr. Morgan Rabach, “good sleep, hydration, low salt, and sunscreen under the eye” can make a positive difference because “factors are genetic and environmental.” (On the makeup front, consider layering your tinted moisturizer with SPF beneath your concealer of choice to temporarily veil dark under-eye circles.) For more intensive treatment, Rabach often uses “fillers made from hyaluronic acid to mask the bags and also sometimes undergo under-eye surgery (called a lower blepharoplasty) to remove the eye bag.”

Ingredients to Look for in Dark Circle Treatments

While eye creams tend to make grand promises—advertising their ability to firm, lift, and brighten—sussing out certain ingredients is the surest way to determine a cream’s eye-brightening efficacy. “There are so many factors that contribute to dark circles, including thin, translucent skin from collagen loss, hyperpigmentation, blood vessels and venous pooling, eye bags and puffiness, and lifestyle factors such as stress, lack of sleep, and dehydration,” says Dr. Adazeh Shirazi, a board-certified dermatologist who’s based in California. “Different ingredients help address different causes to reduce the appearance of dark circles.” Herewith, find a cheat sheet of the most powerful ingredients for combatting under-eye circles.

’Tis the season to embrace all things cozy, and that includes gingerbread. The scent isn’t just wafting from our ovens and favorite coffee shops anymore. It’s showing up in our everyday lives, too, as gingerbread fragrances grow more popular.

In perfume, gingerbread is a gourmand accord: warm ginger, molasses, and cinnamon, usually rounded out with vanilla or woods. Ginger is a clever ingredient. Perfumers often use it as a top note, where it adds sparkle and energy, and it pairs beautifully with pepper, citrus, rich woods, and amber. That’s why a good gingerbread perfume can feel cozy without turning into a candle.

#6786. Best Corn Casserole

In my opinion, corn casserole is the most underrated Thanksgiving side. Don’t get me wrong, I love classic stuffing and mashed potatoes, but for me, this is the dish that the holiday isn’t complete without. This tried-and-true recipe is my go-to; it's packed with sweet corn, balanced by a pinch of paprika and herby chives, and has an insanely tender and custardy texture that will have you on your third helping before you even know what hit you. Let this classic side be the star of your Thanksgiving dinner if you’re ever in the mood for endless praise and compliments.

What People Are Saying:“Very delicious, made it exactly like written, came out perfectly.” - mgnpanossia4246

After greasing your baking dish (very well) with softened butter and preheating the oven, break out that box of Jiffy mix. Whisk the mix with granulated sugar in a bowl until very few lumps remain.

Then mix—in a medium bowl or large measuring cup—your eggs, milk, cream, and melted butter until combined. Add that bowl into your Jiffy mix bowl, and stir until just combined. You don’t have to overmix here. Stir in your (thawed!) corn, creamed corn, salt, paprika, and pepper.

Pour your mixture into your prepared baking dish, using a spatula to smooth out the top. Bake until the casserole has fully set and the top is golden brown. This should take anywhere from 40 to 50 minutes.

Once done, let it cool slightly before serving, and top with fresh chives.

The full ingredients and instructions can be found in the recipe below.

Since only 10 minutes of prep go into this corn casserole, you’ll have plenty of time to pull together the rest of dinner as it bakes in the oven. Try this with simple air-fryer chicken thighs or a hearty harvest chicken skillet for a comforting weeknight dinner, or add it to your holiday sides of mashed potato casserole, sweet potato casserole, and butternut squash mac & cheese alongside your roast turkey.

If you want to make your holiday easier, then this corn casserole is a perfect make-ahead Thanksgiving recipe. Simply prepare the casserole, then cover it tightly and store it in the fridge for up to 48 hours. Remove and bake once you're ready to serve, keeping in mind that it may take slightly longer to bake coming straight from the fridge.

If you somehow manage to have any leftovers, tightly cover your casserole dish or store it in an airtight container in the fridge for 4-5 days.

#6784. Andrew Scott on Elsinore, the Met Gala, and Why “Shakespeare Is Really for Everybody”

To be or not to be? Andrew Scott knows a thing or two about that question. On Wednesday evening, the Irish actor brought a touch of Shakespearean flair—and plenty of leading-man charisma—to the Museum of Modern Art for the New York premiere of his latest film, Elsinore.

Dressed in a pinstripe suit by Vivienne Westwood, Scott made quite the entrance. Joining him were director Simon Stone, co-star Joe Locke, and members of the film’s creative team, all exchanging warm embraces, big smiles, and the kind of affectionate greetings that made the evening feel less like a formal premiere and more like a particularly well-dressed reunion.

There was plenty to celebrate. Based on a remarkable true story, Elsinore features Scott as Ian Charleson, the Scottish actor best known for his role in Chariots of Fire, during the final months of his life in 1989. As he prepares to take on the titular role of Hamlet at London’s National Theatre, Charleson is privately battling AIDS, though determined to return to the stage despite his deteriorating health. Named after the Danish castle that provides the setting for Shakespeare’s tragedy, the film follows Charleson’s journey toward one final, extraordinary performance, exploring the complicated relationship between art and mortality, and the courage it takes to keep pursuing one’s passion in the face of devastating circumstances.

Also starring Olivia Colman and Luke Thompson, Elsinore finds unexpected lightness in a story shadowed by tragedy. At its heart is a celebration of the transformative power of performance—and of a man whose final act would become one of the most meaningful of his life.

Scott is no stranger to Shakespeare’s most famous prince. His own acclaimed turn as Hamlet in Robert Icke’s 2017 Almeida Theatre production earned him an Olivier Award nomination. His experience with the Bard may make Shakespeare’s lines look effortless, but finding his way into the language is another matter. So what’s his secret? “I always think of it like rap music. If I don’t understand every word when someone is rapping, there’s the energy behind it and the musicality or the velocity of it—and it’s still electrifying,” he told Vogue. “It’s the same with Shakespeare, but I hate the idea that it’s kind of kidnapped by academics. Shakespeare is really for everybody.”

And when it comes to commanding an audience, Scott is something of an expert, though even he admits that some occasions are more nerve-racking than others. Asked whether portraying Hamlet or getting dressed for the Met Gala presents the bigger challenge, he didn’t hesitate. “Playing Hamlet is more intimidating—but only just,” he said with a laugh. “I think with the Met Gala, you always think it’s going to be incredibly intimidating. The stairs are a bit scary, but it’s only clothes! So you’ve just got to have a bit of fun.”

Scott’s greatest challenge in Elsinore was getting beneath the mythology of Charleson and discovering the man himself. What struck him most was the actor’s sense of humor—a quality he was determined to preserve on screen. Despite its emotional weight, Scott insists, this is not a film without comic relief. “It’s a very uplifting and a very funny film,” he said, calling Stephen Beresford’s screenplay “as hysterically funny as it is heartbreaking.”

That humor was also something producer Andrea Occhipinti was keen to protect. His connection to Charleson, however, went far beyond the screen. The two first met in 1984 while working on the television miniseries The Sun Also Rises. As Occhipinti recalled, they became close friends, bonding over the fact that they were the only two gay men in the group. Charleson never disclosed that he was HIV-positive. It was only after his death, when their mutual friend Ethan Silverman shared the story of his final six months, that Occhipinti began to imagine the possibility of a film.

Years later, he found his screenwriter in Beresford and his leading man in Scott. The casting, it seems, was almost written in the stars. Asked what Charleson might have thought of the film and the evening’s celebrations, Occhipinti said he was certain his friend would approve. “Ian would be pleased because Andrew Scott is amazing. He really looks like Ian and he has incredible talent. I think he would have smiled about all of this tonight. He very much was a loving and warm person.”

That celebratory spirit carried into the evening, which brought out a suitably starry crowd. Among those on hand to mark the occasion were Sigourney Weaver, Ben Platt, Dylan Mulvaney, Patricia Clarkson, Katie Couric, François Arnaud of Heated Rivalry, And Just Like That’s Sarita Choudhury, Kate Walsh, and Christopher Meloni.

And no Shakespearean celebration would be complete without a little drama after the curtain fell. The festivities continued at Le Rock, where guests gathered for the after-party, eager to congratulate Scott, exchange a few words, and sing his praises. Over Champagne and conversation, partygoers tucked into sliders and fries, while an impressive seafood display piled high with fresh lobster, oysters, and shrimp provided a lavish centerpiece.

Naturally, Shakespeare had the last word on the evening’s décor, too. Copies of Hamlet were scattered throughout the restaurant, alongside a rather cheeky nod to one of the play’s most memorable scenes. A skull emblazoned with the words “This is what you’ll look like by the end of it”—a joke that also features in the film—added a deliciously irreverent touch. It was a fitting detail for a celebration of a story that finds plenty of laughter amid the tragedy. The party carried on well past midnight, with no one seemingly in a hurry to call it a night.

#6781. Gordon Ramsay’s Routine for a Six-Pack at 60 Includes Daily Crunches and Red-Light Therapy

Gordon Ramsay has earned 17 Michelin stars throughout his career and still oversees nearly 100 restaurants. He also fronts prime-time TV franchises on both sides of the Atlantic and holds a couple of casual Guinness World Records (call him if you need to fillet a 10-pound fish in 1 minute five seconds).

This is a man who’s never met a goalpost he didn’t want to surpass. So it wasn’t entirely surprising when he posted a bare-chested shot of his six-pack to the grid earlier this month. What generated headlines was the occasion: his 60th birthday. “Gordon Ramsay thirst trap was not on my bingo card,” reads one comment. “My wife is on this app sir,” reads another.

At home in London, he shows me the Insights section on his Instagram account, which reveals the photo has gotten over 62 million impressions. “It’s very strange,” he says. “I’ve had a lot of ribbing from my eldest daughter, Megan. She’s a police officer. She was like, ‘Dad, how can you do that to me?’ All her senior sergeants and officers were laughing and telling her to tell me they’re going for a run. They’re in their early 40s. And then my Royal Marines Commando son just sent me a message with: ‘WTF dad, what are you doing?’”

Ramsay has been a pretty active guy for a while now, despite working in an industry typically associated with chaotic diets and zero time for exercise. He was a youth soccer player back in the day, before becoming a chef and then, in his 40s, going from weighing 252 pounds to competing in marathons and Ironmans.

All very impressive, but it’s even rarer to be this chiseled in your 60s (maintaining a low body fat percentage becomes harder as your testosterone and growth hormone production slows down). Read on to learn more about his preferred forms of cardio, his favorite pre-workout treat, and why he's established a daily cryotherapy routine. “I call it my freezer,” he says.

“15 minutes a day. It starts off with a quick two-minute stretch and 100 sit-ups every morning. Precore lifestyle is my ab cruncher thing—these things are fucking amazing, by the way. If I miss a day, then I have to punish it and do double the next day.”

“It’s not physically hard with big weights. These are 25 pounds. I’ll do a strength class once a week, 45 minutes. That’s it. Occasional push-ups and squats. No personal trainer.”

“What’s helped tremendously, with no impact on any of the joints, is a lot of swimming. So I have an injury in my patella, my right knee, currently. So I just increased the swimming because that’s the only sport that can use every muscle in your body with zero impact. But cardio is off the charts.”

“I have a Zwift bike, so I jump on that for 45 minutes. There’s no impact on that. I’ll do that Monday, Wednesday, Friday and gear up for 90 minutes on a Sunday. If I wasn’t injured, I’d be doing three 5K runs again, Monday, Wednesday, Friday, 20-minute runs. I’ve got my 5K back down to 21 minutes. The dream is under 20. I was running at 19 and a half back in July. A 5K run is easy to do in the morning before work, it’s done within half an hour. Once I do that, I can gear up for either a two-hour bike on a Sunday or a 90-minute run. I alternate. So, one Sunday’s run, one Sunday’s bike.”

“10 years ago, I ruptured my Achilles. I invested in an AlterG, a beautiful running machine that takes your weight, so you get all the cardio you want and less impact on the joint.”

“From the chef’s perspective, I call it my freezer. It’s the cryochamber. I do two sets of three minutes every morning. I try to go seven days a week in that thing, straight after [working out]. That is incredible. That’ll get up to like minus 160. For six minutes.”

“I’ve invested in a tiny little red-light therapy sauna outside in the garden, and it seats two of you, and so [I’ll do] half an hour of that, every other day. This doesn’t need to be done every day. Three times a week’s fine. So when I’m not running, the other day I’ll sit in that red-light therapy and spend 20 minutes, 30 minutes in there, which is brilliant.”

“The older you get, the more you have to stretch. I use a lot of bands. And so after sitting and stretching, I had those incredible bands where you lift your feet up and pull and stretch. 5 minutes is always a nice stretch before you [work out] and then 10 minutes stretch after that. I’ve never been to a yoga class in my life.”

“I’m big on oatmeal for breakfast, Monday to Friday. I indulge Saturday, Sunday. I will do [oatmeal with] blueberry compote, caramelized banana for my potassium, and then fresh blueberries on top with some—it sounds weird, but it’s delicious—hot honey.”

“I have my friends at Precision. I’ll do one of their gels before I start that 5K, a protein gel, 30 grams, and then that sees me through the whole 45-minute routine. Coffee is just a full-fat, flat white, extra hot. Extra hot means I can sip it slowly. I cut out a lot of dairy, a lot of butter, a lot of cream. And so that treat in the morning with a flat white is that sort of, ‘I’ve earned this shit.’”

“I don’t overstress on that because I stay fit. I monitor what I eat, but if anything, I’ll do smaller portions now. I’ll involve a lot of spinach, involve a lot of salmon, chicken, especially thighs. We’re just smart with less cream, less butter intake. And then when we travel, we just keep it light.”

“I have a red wine with dinner, Californian, something light, something delicious. In 20, 30 years running restaurants, I’ve never gone around the room holding a glass of wine, holding a glass of champagne, sitting down, drinking with customers. Again, discipline.”

“Just a couple of spoons of creatine, five mornings a week. A little scoop in my water.”

“One thing I’ve learned in my 50s and my 60s—everyone said the older you get, the less you sleep. I think it’s the other way around. I like to be in bed by 11, and I’m up at 6. My Oura ring for me has become my best mate, because it just gives me the proper day-by-day indication and then keeps you on track for sleep.”

“Sign up for something. Have a target because that gets you out of bed, with a bigger, incentivized plan. I need that target twice a year, every six months. And I’ve done that since my 40th birthday. Whether it’s half an Ironman, whether it’s a marathon, half a marathon, I’ve always got something to focus on.”

“No, no, no, no, no, no. Definitely not. No. I mean, no. My meditation is Kitchen fucking Nightmares. Dealing with some of those contributors on there and having to sit with them and talk with them. But no, not very good at sitting still.”

#6780. ‘Political malpractice’: Calls grow to delay Senate GOP leadership elections

Sen. Ron Johnson, R-Wis., walks through the Ohio Clock Corridor this summer. Now he’s pushing to delay leadership elections for his conference, urging a discussion of last week’s larger than expected losses. (Bill Clark/CQ Roll Call)

After last week’s midterm elections saw them lose at least seven Senate seats and the majority, some Republicans are pushing for a reckoning, saying the conference should stop and think about its future.

“After the stunning defeat [we] just can’t continue on business as usual. That kind of defeat demands change,” Sen. Ron Johnson, R-Wis., said on Monday evening.

Asked if he himself would want to run for Senate minority leader, the Wisconsin Republican responded with a question of his own: “That’s part of our problem. Who’d want the job? Would you want it?”

Though no hopeful had emerged as of Monday afternoon — at least publicly — to challenge current Majority Leader John Thune, R-S.D., for the top GOP post, the frustration was palpable as Republican senators returned to D.C. and grappled with the election results. Calls grew throughout the day to delay the conference’s leadership elections planned for Tuesday morning.

“Well, I mean, I’m open to hearing what the arguments are for that,” Thune told reporters Monday when asked about the possibility of postponing the leadership votes. “I don’t know if that solves anything, but like I said, it’s going to be a decision that’s ultimately made by Republican senators. We’ll hear them out.”

Johnson told reporters on Monday that if the election is held, he would “leave the room” and walk out. Either way, he seemed certain to at least get a “fulsome” discussion of the topic, as he requested in a letter he sent to Republican Conference Chair Tom Cotton, R-Ark. Four other Republican senators signed on to the letter, hitting the threshold to call a conference meeting under internal GOP rules.

Also signed by Sens. Mike Lee of Utah, Ted Budd of North Carolina, Rick Scott of Florida, and John Kennedy of Louisiana, the letter obtained by CQ Roll Call cites last week’s “stunning defeat” and reads in part: “Holding leadership elections without engaging in a robust discussion and evaluation of how those running for leadership intend to lead would be political malpractice.”

Kennedy said Monday evening that he was asking to postpone the leadership elections because “we need time to talk to each other.”

“We’re like a shop owner who’s losing his customers. It’s not the customer’s job to fix that. It’s the shop owner’s job, and I’m not looking for blame-shifting. I’m looking for self-examination,” he said, adding that he wouldn’t consider running for minority leader himself.

In the letter, the group asked that at the time the leadership elections were set to begin, 9 a.m. on Tuesday, they instead “have a fulsome conference discussion and delay holding leadership elections until we all have a chance to hear from leadership candidates as to what type of collaborative conference governing model we should adopt and what our strategy will be to counter the emboldened Democrats.”

Thune echoed that wording Monday as he spoke to reporters, saying, “I do think that we need to have a very fulsome, robust conversation about where we go from here, and then we’ll see what the consensus of the conference is.”

But Sen. Shelley Moore Capito, R-W.Va., who serves as chair of the Senate Republican Policy Committee, said she has seen such requests launched in the past and didn’t think it would do much to affect the ultimate outcome. “I think Sen. Thune will be reelected,” she said.

And several Senate GOP aides said that they suspected a “robust” conversation wouldn’t do much to delay the plans and that leadership elections were still expected to take place Tuesday. They noted similar efforts from some of those same senators in previous years to sow discord in the ranks.

That includes Scott, who unsuccessfully challenged Thune in 2024 and then-GOP leader Mitch McConnell, R-Ky., in 2022. But Scott told Fox News over the weekend, “I don’t want to run again. I’ve run twice.”

Republicans “failed. We had a horrible night,” Scott said. “Instead of having an [leadership] election, the first thing you do is you say, ‘OK, what are we going to change? We have to change something.’”

Lee floated the idea of waiting until after the uncalled Senate races in Mississippi and Alaska are officially resolved.

An online pressure campaign was building in some of the same MAGA corners of the internet that have pushed to pass a sweeping voter ID bill, known as the SAVE America Act, that has stalled in the Senate. On Monday morning, influencer Scott Presler in an X post urged constituents to call their senators while praising the lawmakers who have already pushed for the leadership elections to be postponed. “Where is everyone else?” he wrote.

Sens. Jim Banks of Indiana and Dave McCormick of Pennsylvania also called for a delay, though they did not appear on Johnson’s letter.

“A week ago, we had the worst election results in decades,” McCormick said in a post on X, calling Tuesday “too soon” to vote for leaders. “I know it’s been practice to go straight to leadership elections in recent years, but we should not rush it.”

Banks similarly said Tuesday was “too soon,” posting on social media, “Our conference needs to sit down and have a frank conversation about where we fell short, what we stand for, and what we’re going to do for the working families who sent us here.”

One senator speaking out in Thune’s defense was Sen. John Cornyn, R-Texas, who competed with Thune for the top spot in 2024 but on Monday said the leader isn’t to blame for the midterm results.

“I think the president needs to look in the mirror and look at the flawed candidates that he endorsed, and the kind of environment that we’re in,” said Cornyn, who is still smarting from losing his primary to a challenger backed by President Donald Trump.

Cornyn’s loss means he has no say in the coming conference leadership elections. But he said, “I think Thune’s a good man, and did as well as he could against a president and an environment that was pretty hostile.”

#6779. The New Nudie Suit Is Everywhere—Here’s How To Get The Look Without Going To Auction

On the red carpet at Saturday’s Rock & Roll Hall of Fame induction ceremony in LA, amidst a sea of black leather, silver chains, and sunglasses (and Billy Idol in all of the above, plus a velvet shirt, all custom Ann Demeulemeester), one look stood out above and beyond the rest: The producer and director Polly Parsons was wearing a modern-day interpretation of a Nudie suit—an homage to the rhinestone-heavy, rococo-Western work of the late Ukrainian-born Nuta Kotlyarenko, better known as Nudie Cohn.

Parsons, of course, isn’t alone in paying tribute to Nudie’s legendary work: In recent years, everyone from Harry Styles and Lil Nas X to Taylor Swift and Jenny Lewis has stepped out in his flamboyant pieces, which can fetch thousands (or, in the case of his iconic pieces, tens of thousands) of dollars at auction.

Polly Parsons (left) with her son Lee on the red carpet at the Rock n’ Roll Hall of Fame induction ceremony in Los Angeles.

The suit Parsons was wearing on the red carpet, though—a tribute to an iconic Nudie suit worn by her late father, Gram Parsons (inducted to the R&R HOF class of 2026 as an Early Influence), on the cover of the Flying Burrito Brothers’ 1969 album Gilded Palace of Sin—was made by Judith Pierce of LA’s RoseCut Clothing. For the past decade or so, Pierce has been specializing in the chain-stitch embroidery used by Nudie to create custom work for the likes of Phoebe Bridgers (and the other members of boygenius), Beyoncé, Orville Peck, Margot Price, and Post Malone, among others.

“It’s kind of crazy, because the reason that I first became obsessed with Nudie suits years ago is because of Gram's Nudie Suit—the Flying Burrito Brothers suit on the cover of the record,” Pierce says. “And of course it made me want a chain stitch machine years ago, but they were really, really hard to find, and so I started doing appliqué, which is a different type of needle work, and I remade the Gram Parsons suit for myself.”

While Nudie, of course, wasn’t the only rodeo-rhinestone tailor doing this kind of early cowboycore work, “he took it to the next level” in both flamboyance and iconography, Pierce says. “Nudie brought in what I guess you’d call not-traditional imagery—I mean, I don't think anyone else was embroidering naked ladies on suits, or pills, or poppies, or weed leaves”—all of which feature prominently on the suit of Gram Parsons. (Parsons wasn’t just ahead of his time in fusing country music with rock and other genres—he became a charismatic counterculture icon, with his suit and seemingly his entire lifestyle attracting the ire of conservative country fans.)

Orville Peck in Pierce’s chain-stitch-embroidered suit

A full suit can take Pierce somewhere in the neighborhood of 80 hours to make, given the labor-intensive nature of chain-stitch embroidery. For starters, the machine at the heart of the operation is hand-operated. “You guide the way the needle moves with your hand on a handle under the machine, which you turn in circles,” she says. “It's kind of like writing cursive—and if you don't finish off the embroidery when you're done with it, you can just pull out the entire thing, because it's all connected with a series of loops. Basically, you create a texture—it looks completely different than a machine embroidery, and it takes a really long time. But it's fun.”

Pierce, of course, isn’t the only person out there doing chain-stitch embroidery—the Austin, Texas-based collective Fort Lonesome makes custom suits (for Diplo and many others) and off-the-rack embroidered jeans, patches, and prints. Jerry Lee Atwood, who made the Nudie-esque suit that Lil Nas X wore in the “Old Town Road” video, works out of Indianapolis. And Nudie’s former apprentice, Manuel Cuevas, does custom work out of his Nashville studio.

For Polly Parsons, there was never a question about what she’d wear to the Rock n’ Roll Hall of Fame induction honoring her father. “He and I had a really beautiful connection, and I knew that I wanted to pay tribute to him,” she said on the red carpet, “but I wanted to have it made in a different color: His suit is so iconic, as was his music—he blazed trails between rock n’ roll and country that had never really been touched on before. People were not dressing in a rock n’ roll style in country music at that time, and Gram boldly went into that moment with his iconography stating who he was, and I think that’s what fashion is all about. He did his own thing.”

#6778. The Key To The Perfect Pie Crust Is In These 5 Simple Rules

Admit it, a pie is only as good as its crust. No matter how good the filling is, a flaky, buttery crust is what separates the good from the great. But how to achieve the pie crust of your (and your guests’) dreams? The master of all things baking, Senior Food Editor Makinze Gore, is here to explain her five golden rules of pie crust—just in time for the holidays.

A buttery, flaky pie crust is the stuff of dreams; to achieve this, Gore says it’s essential to use cold butter. The same goes for many flaky pastries and baked goods, as the steam that cold butter releases as it melts creates those layers inside the crust. If you use butter that isn’t cold, it will melt too quickly—it can even melt just during the process of rolling the dough out if it's warm enough, which won’t produce any steam. Warm butter will leave you with a dense, mealy crust.

The size of your butter chunks will affect the end product. Gore says larger and smaller chunks can both work, but larger chunks leave a flakier crust, while smaller chunks let the crust hold more intricate designs. Typically, you want your chunks to be at least pea-sized to form the steam pockets required to create flaky layers.

Sensing a theme here? Gore says it’s vitally important to chill your dough at several points throughout the process. This is because you need to keep the butter cold, as it will warm up as you work with it. Gore recommends chilling your crust after you first make it, after rolling it out, before crimping it, and once more before baking it. This will help keep the butter cold and prevent the crust from shrinking as it bakes.

One great trick to ensure an even bake is to use a metal pie tin. Metal conducts heat better than glass, so it bakes the crust more evenly, though glass does have some benefits. Baking pie crust in a glass pie dish lets you see the bottom of the crust and know exactly when it’s perfectly cooked.

Another tip Gore says will help bake your crust more evenly is to set a baking sheet in the oven as it preheats, then to place the pie tin on top of the hot baking sheet as it cooks. This extra heat helps the crust cook slightly faster, giving it more time to set before the liquid filling seeps in.

#6777. A Brief Guide to Grand Theft Auto VI’s Nike Sneaker Dupes

GTA 6 is finally out, which means we’ve got some in-game sneaker shopping to do. We’d already spotted Air Force 1 dupes in the trailers, but now that we can finally play the long-awaited masterpiece itself, we’re taking a closer look at what else is on the shelves. There are tons of different sneaker stores sprinkled throughout the game, and Eris—Rockstar’s fictional answer to Nike—has given us a lot to work through.

Even the name is a joke. Nike takes its name from the Greek goddess of victory, while Eris is the goddess of strife and discord—an appropriate choice for kicks you might buy with money from an armed robbery. The Swoosh has been replaced with a pointier, upside-down logo, and plenty of the sneakers baring the mark are instantly recognizable.

The Eris Rise Low is the obvious starting point. It’s the game’s AF1 equivalent, complete with the familiar paneled upper, perforated toebox, and a big, beefy sole underfoot. The Eris Strife 360, meanwhile, looks straight out of the Air Max 97 catalog. There’s the rippling upper, rounded toe, and long cushioning window underneath. Designed by Christian Tresser and released in 1997, the IRL sneaker’s silhouette took inspiration from water ripples, while the OG “Silver Bullet” finish drew on mountain bike components. Eris keeps enough of that shape for you to be able to spot it from across the shop.

Some versions get even more specific. There’s a white-and-orange Strife 360, for example, with oversized branding, contrasting lace loops, and printed side text that recalls Off-White’s own Air Max 97s. It isn’t a straight one-to-one recreation, but the reference feels pretty clear. An apparent take on the Off-White Air Force 1 Mid also appeared in the teasers. Someone at Rockstar is clearly a fan of Virgil Abloh’s work.

Then there’s the Eris Strife 90, with pairs that borrow heavily from the Air Max 90 “Infrared.” The bright red accents and sharp, aggressive lines are all there, with one colorway even adding black scribbles across the overlays.

Arguably the most iconic signature shoe of all time, the Air Jordan 1, gets a look in the game as well. The Eris Rook 1 High comes in leather and suede variations, with a tall ankle collar and laces running almost all the way up. There’s a black-and-white pair that swaps Jordan branding for an Eris basketball graphic on the tongue. You’ve probably got something quite similar in your closet at home.

The Eris Cross Trainers are, well, harder to pin down. Available in several colorways, they have a mesh base and leather overlays. One white-and-gray version gets bright green hits that nod to Nike’s signature “Volt” hue. The simpler construction puts it closer to an older Nike runner, somewhere around MD Runner 2 territory, than a direct Zoom Vomero 5 or V5 Rnr copy. And for those times that tying laces feels like too much work, there are the very appropriately named Eris Basic Slides. The broad branded strap and flat footbed are very Nike Benassi.

There are different versions to consider, too. “Origins” go all-white, while “Elite” options are matte and all-black. So if you‘ve found a silhouette you like, you can still spend another 10 minutes deciding which version works with your trousers. A problem we were hoping to leave outside the game.

It gives you an idea of just how much there is in GTA 6. The older games let you change your clothes, but here you’re choosing between different colorways, materials, and versions of individual shoes. And Eris is only one part of it: There are clothes and accessories that look like takes on Stüssy, Loewe, Chrome Hearts, and Louis Vuitton, too. You could spend a fair bit of time getting dressed before you get anything else done.

We’ve waited this long to play the game. A few more minutes in the sneaker shop before you really get going on missions won’t hurt.

#6776. Lisa Rinna On Broadway Superstitions, Acting With Husband Harry Hamlin, and Dressing Like the Grinch

Some couples wouldn’t dare mix love and work, but Lisa Rinna and Harry Hamlin beg to differ. “I love working with Harry because he’s professional, hardworking, and he knows his lines,” Rinna tells Vogue. “But the best part of working with him is that I get to sleep with him, and it doesn’t cause a scandal!”

Last night, Rinna and Hamlin celebrated the opening night of It’s A Wonderful Life: A Live Radio Play on Broadway (she’s Violet; he’s Mr. Potter), joining Broadway legends Andrew Rannells, Megan Hilty, and Tituss Burgess on the call sheet. “Our family watches It’s A Wonderful Life every year on Christmas Eve. We love the movie so much, so when we got the call that Broadway legend Jerry Zaks was directing, it was a no-brainer,” Rinna says. “The first thing I asked Jerry when he cast us was: Who thought to put Broadway icons in a cast with Lisa Rinna and Harry Hamlin? He didn’t really have an answer, but he likes us all together.”

While she delights in sharing the stage with her husband, Rinna also enjoys the solitude of her backstage getting-ready routine. “I am someone who loves to have music playing, and I am a bit superstitious, so I have specific songs that I have to listen to before each show,” she says. On the It’s A Wonderful Life playlist? “While I am getting my wig put on, I listen to the La Bola Negra soundtrack. Then we go to Whitney Houston’s Greatest Hits—we listen to ‘Heartbreak Hotel’ and ‘It’s Not Right, But It’s Okay.’ Finally, right before the five-minute call, I listen to ‘Party’ by Troye Sivan.”

Rinna wanted to convey the holiday spirit through her premiere night look. “I had a vision of myself wearing a Willy Chavarria men’s suit,” she says. “[My stylist, Daniela Romero] found this one for me, and I’m absolutely in love with the color. She took my vision and added a nod to the holidays. I am a cunty Grinch, how perfect!” For the after-party, Rinna opted for a look from her longtime friend Sergio Hudson. “I wanted to wear American designers for opening night,” she says. “I am excited to have a piece of [Sergio] with me tonight.”

Here, Lisa Rinna brings Vogue along for the opening night of It’s A Wonderful Life: A Live Radio Play on Broadway.

Getting ready for the carpet with my incredible hair and makeup team—Jae Cardenas and Katie Nova.

Harry and I really have been loving this chapter together in New York…dare I say it suits us?!

The icon Andrew Rannalls—our most perfect George Bailey!

Still pinching myself, truly. This entire experience has been such a dream, I’m so grateful.

#6775. As an Awkward Teen, Grand Theft Auto Was My Escape. Now It Feels Like Real Life.

This morning, I woke up at 7:00 AM to play Grand Theft Auto VI just hours after its release. It’s the first new installment of the franchise since 2013; the one whose first trailer was released three years ago; the one that probably cost over a billion dollars to produce; the one set in a satirical, fictionalized south Florida; the one described in this very magazine as “the peak of entertainment.” During my first four hours with the game, I made a few hundred dollars doing odd jobs, performed several repairs on my swamp-front fixer-upper, knocked out a few sets of kettlebell swings on the deck, did favors for two friendly neighbors, and ran a mile to retrieve a freshly repainted car I left behind at a fishing spot just outside of town. I’m writing in vague terms not only to avoid spoiling the game’s plot but because, so far, it hasn’t been all that exciting. The absurd and hyperrealistic mundanity of its gameplay is, simply, astonishing, and more than I ever could have wanted from a franchise I’ve been playing for nearly a quarter of a century.

Grand Theft Auto, first released in 1997, and GTA 2, which followed two years later, were top-down driving games whose mechanics and visuals never intrigued me as a child. I wasn’t even aware of their existence until learning of the franchise’s third entry, and the first in 3D. I discovered GTA in a hotel room in Wichita Falls, TX, a small city just a stone’s throw from Oklahoma, because a classmate named Zach (or Zack) risked damaging his PS2 by toting it along on a school trip. We were there for the annual Texas Thespian Festival, an opportunity for dramatically inclined teenagers to perform plays and musicals in front of strange kids from strange schools and compete for prizes that, like most awards, quickly lost meaning after they were bestowed. After attending the required main stage productions, many of which were watered down to their school-friendly “Junior” adaptations—Into the Woods without the second act deaths and darkness, Guys & Dolls with considerably less gambling—we returned to the nearby hotel where students slept three to a room and were told to behave. For several nights we took turns playing this impossibly violent and vulgar game, five or six of us huddled around the television parked on a dresser no one used. We stole cars, killed non-player characters with assault rifles, and even completed an occasional story mission, most of which required, well, stealing cars and killing non-player characters with assault rifles. We were actors, and wasn’t gaming just another form of performance?

I don’t remember much about that particular ThesFest, but I do remember being grateful for Zack or Zach’s generosity. Before that moment, he was simply an intimidatingly charming and friendly older boy I knew best for playing Romeo in a production that our school’s theater director chose to set during the Vietnam War for reasons he was unable to articulate. (In that, my first stage role, I was cast as Juliet’s unnamed little brother, a role that doesn’t exist in the original text.) Of course I’d played video games before that weekend—Myst, Rollercoaster Tycoon and a Titanic-themed mystery were my favorites at that point—but never before had I experienced something so immersive or graphically impressive. It was loud and exciting, a simulation of real life at its most extreme and chaotic. When I got home, I knew the console–and GTA 3–had to be mine. My life, laser-focused on math homework and our theater director’s post-apocalyptic interpretation of Antigone, could have really used a thrill.

I traded in my PlayStation, a Christmas gift from years earlier on which I played mostly rented or borrowed adventure games, and used money I’d saved from mowing lawns (my family’s own, and those of a few elderly neighbors), to pay the balance. The only game I wanted was GTA III, and it’s the only one I played for months. The novelty was the thing. There was a story, of course: some nameless protagonist with a chiseled, low-polygon face committing crimes and various acts of violence to achieve a goal of… staying alive? Perhaps that’s the goal of most games, maybe even the goal of life itself. I skipped cutscenes in favor of driving from one glowing cylindrical marker to the next, shooting police officers in the head and picking up sex workers who did unseen, noisy things to my character that made the stolen cars bounce up and down like a lowrider with hydraulics. I’d largely missed the groundbreaking graphics of the N64 era—having never played Zelda or Mario Kart or GoldenEye outside the bedrooms of childhood friends with bigger houses and never-ending supplies of soda in the fridge—which meant my first truly passionate dive into console gaming was filled with manic, gratuitous violence. The original PlayStation was for catching troublemaking monkeys in nets and flying around gentle landscapes as a purple dragon. The PS2, though, was for imagining a life of crime. That my parents allowed me to play these games at all still surprises me, but I suppose to them anything was better than me getting drunk at parties I knew were happening but was never invited to.

By the time Grand Theft Auto: Vice City was released the following year, I had a part time job at a local AMC, so I bought the game with money earned selling popcorn and cleaning that same popcorn off the floor. Playing it was a reward for completing an essay on Ernest Hemingway or telling multiple moviegoers that we couldn’t give refunds simply because they “didn’t like” Men In Black 2. The game’s soundtrack introduced me to songs that still activate memories sitting cross-legged on the carpet of my bedroom, turning the volume down as low as I could to prevent my father from stepping inside and saying, gravely, “Am I going to have to turn that off?” To this day, I can’t hear Laura Branagin’s “Self Control” without conjuring Tommy Vercetti, the protagonist of Grand Theft Auto: Vice City, driving a motorcycle down a sparsely populated road flanked by neon-lit palm trees. For months, I listened to more in-game radio than the local Top 40 station, unaware at the time that its curated period playlists would shape my musical appetite for the rest of my life.

The third installment of that PS2 trilogy, Grand Theft Auto: San Andreas, came out two years later, when I was a freshman in college. I was 18, adjusting to life in a new city, and trying desperately to make friends. The game was less a reward for hard work than a balm when I didn’t feel like socializing, a distraction from my countless first semester anxieties. By the time I completed its main story, life away from home had become normal. I had new friends, new obligations, new problems. I used my PS2 as a DVD player almost exclusively, watching several movies a week, all borrowed from Netflix (during its original red-envelope form) and the school library. By the time GTA 4 was released, I’d lost interest in gaming altogether. We were in a recession, and I was hellbent on keeping my very first real job. In fact, I didn’t play another Grand Theft Auto game until 2013, on a PS3 Slim (the console’s discounted, late-generation entry), weeks after my now-husband and I moved into our first apartment together. In the time since my last GTA experience, I’d come out of the closet, moved across the country, started a new career, adopted my first cat, and made an entirely new group of close friends. A franchise that was once a distraction from teenage malaise was now pure, unadulterated fun. Gone were the days of playing it at a low volume–I turned it all the way up, and didn’t skip cut scenes. I was there for the story as well as the spectacle.

13 years later, the longest ever interval between new installments, my relationship with gaming has evolved alongside the games themselves. It’s not enough to be distracted; being distracted is now our default state. Disturbances arise, whether seeking them or not: a text message in the middle of a terrible Peacock show; a news alert regarding a global calamity about which you can do nothing but panic; the jittering beeps of FaceTime call while reading a wonderful novel, or that mindless, habitual scroll through a feed that shows videos meant to be seen but not remembered. Lately I’ve turned to gaming for total immersion, for sensations of genuine awe that might keep me from succumbing to the dopamine rush of my phone. I spent much of the summer replaying Kingdom Come Deliverance and its sequel, a pair of life simulators set in medieval Europe. In those games, every action has a consequence, and every success is hard-fought and requires patience.

After several hours in its impossibly dense world, GTA 6 seems to be encouraging a similar sense of duty. My real life is fine, often even good. It’s the kind of life I thought impossible when the franchise first entered my life nearly a quarter century ago. I’m not bowled over by the game because it’s better or even more fun than the life I live outside Vice City—there are gun-toting mass murderers in both, after all—but because it’s totally different. Even when you’re comfortable in your own life, isn’t it extraordinary to enter another? The games have always been about adults, and for adults. It says so right on the box. Since 2002, their goals have fundamentally remained the same: get a job, make money, stay alive. As a teenager, its simulated depravity was funny and exciting, with graphics and gameplay so vivid I could feel like a kid again. But now, at 40? With 20-plus years of GTA under my belt and a life 16-year-old me would find more unbelievable than the functionalities of the PS5 Pro? No game has ever made me feel older. What a privilege that is.

#6774. Maple Bourbon Chicken With Brussels Sprouts

If you, like most people, hear the term “bourbon chicken” and have no idea what it is, let me call it by its more common name: toothpick chicken. Yes, the suspiciously delicious chicken shelled out at mall food courts on toothpicks is most likely bourbon chicken. Popular at American Chinese or Cajun-style Chinese restaurants, the sticky-sweet chicken is the utter definition of delicious, luring weary shoppers into food courts to find refuge from the bubblegum pink sweatpants and ripped denim. This version takes cues from the classic but swaps in some autumnal ingredients to make it fit for the season. Charred brussels sprouts leaves and pecans add bulk, while maple syrup and apple cider vinegar give it that delightful tart-sweet flavor we know and love. Serve it over rice or noodles or all by its lonesome for a hearty dinner that will take you back to the '90s.

More chicken dinner winners here: Sheet-Pan Hot Honey Chicken and Peaches; Basil Chicken Stir-Fry; and Ginger-Garlic Chicken and Vegetable Stir-Fry.

Whisk one 1½" piece ginger, peeled, finely grated, 3 garlic cloves, finely chopped, ⅓ cup bourbon, ⅓ cup pure maple syrup, ¼ cup apple cider vinegar, ¼ tsp. crushed red pepper flakes, 1 Tbsp. cornstarch, ¼ cup low-sodium soy sauce, and ½ cup water in a small bowl to combine. Set sauce aside.

Toss 1½ lb. skinless, boneless chicken breasts, halved crosswise, thinly sliced against the grain, remaining 1 Tbsp. cornstarch, and remaining 1 Tbsp. low-sodium soy sauce in a medium bowl until chicken is coated; set aside.

Heat 2 Tbsp. vegetable oil in a large skillet over medium-high. Add 1 lb. brussels sprouts, halved through stems, leaves separated, and cook, stirring very occasionally, until just tender and blackened in spots, about 5 minutes. Transfer to another medium bowl.

Add more oil if pan looks dry and, working in batches if needed, arrange reserved chicken in a single layer and cook, turning occasionally, until golden brown and cooked through, about 5 minutes. Transfer to bowl with brussels sprouts.

Add reserved sauce to same pan and bring to a simmer. Cook, stirring constantly, until thickened, about 1 minute. Remove pan from heat and return brussels sprouts and chicken to pan; top with 1 cup salted roasted pecans, coarsely chopped. Toss to coat in sauce. Serve chicken and brussels sprouts with steamed white rice if desired.

#6772. This Piping Hot Zenith Collaboration Explains the Future of the Watch Market

This is an edition of the newsletter Box + Papers, Cam Wolf’s weekly deep dive into the world of watches. Sign up here.

In early July, I met with Zenith’s chief product officer Romain Marietta over Zoom to get a sneak peek at a watch that wouldn’t come out until November. The latest Zenith G.F.J., made in partnership with the Los Angeles-based independent watchmaker J.N. Shapiro, is a gorgeous timepiece that combines the former’s mechanical know-how with Shapiro’s meticulously handmade dials. Over four months before the watch was even unveiled to the public, Marietta shared that 20 of the 25 pieces produced had already been sold. Of all the releases from the major Swiss brands in 2026, none better represent the future of luxury watchmaking than this high-end, fussily made, independent-adjacent, sold-out-before-you-ever-heard-about-it G.F.J.

Zenith recently announced that it’s laying off roughly half of its current workforce as it attempts to shift its position in the market. “All of us in our segment, which is described as mid-range, are going through a very difficult period,” Zenith CEO Benoit De Clerck told staff as he explained the restructuring, according to Le Temps. The brand has been shifting upmarket since last year, when it released a rainbow gem-set version of its flagship sport watch. The G.F.J. is the cornerstone of that new business. As the economy tilts even further in favor of the uber-rich, it’s also altering where the bulk of the profits come from for watchmakers. “Wealthy people are still wealthy, and even more wealthy,” Marietta said. “It’s easier [now] to sell high-end watches than watches for 3,000 to 5,000 Swiss francs.”

That’s true. Lorel Monroy, a sales associate at LA’s Feldmar Watch Company, tells me there’s “definitely been an uptick” in interest around Zenith this year. Not for the brand’s flagship Chronomaster, which remains firmly in the starter luxury watch zone at $12,300, but for the far more expensive G.F.J., which starts at $49,900. “The luxury direction is the game changer,” Monroy said. Feldmar only received three G.F.J.s to allocate in 2026 and they were all quickly snapped up. There’s now a waitlist building at the retailer for the upscale model.

When it comes to the G.F.J., Zenith has acted like MacGyver, finding endless uses for its smash-hit model. First released in the spring of 2025, the watch received two very pretty, well-received sequels earlier this year. In March, I got to test out the new onyx-dial version for just a few hours before I had to send it along to Hollywood stylist Fabio Immediato, who promptly strapped it on Simu Liu’s wrist for the Oscars.

Over the summer, Zenith also launched this double-signed program, which allows the brand to work with independent makers like J.N. Shapiro. Before this latest release, the Swiss brand linked up with Japan’s beloved Naoya Hida.

That watch was a fascinating representation of the project—the spotlessly clean dial was straight out of the latter’s workshop. Zenith, a historic brand backed by LVMH, ceded control entirely to the small Japanese indie. It was a massive success. To allocate the pieces, Marietta said they narrowed the list to 10 clients, mainly from the Naoya Hida side, then sent an email that included the price tag of 60,000 Swiss francs but zero images of the watch. “They said yes right away, trusting us with the design,” Marietta said. “And that’s a great testimony of how the trust can be at that level when you buy a piece for [$75,000] without even seeing it, not even a picture.”

In the topsy-turvy world of watches in 2026, it’s much sexier to be an independent brand than one in the stable of the world’s largest luxury conglomerate. Marietta calls the G.F.J. “an independent brand within our global lineup,” pointing to the high-level finishing and historic movement inside the piece—the type of nerdy details that appeal to the most seasoned collectors driving the industry. “The G.F.J. collection itself is bringing us new clients,” Marietta said. “It's attracting clients who did not really have Zenith on their radar. These guys were more A. Lange & Söhne clients or with independents.”

Zenith created irresistible bait for this new stripe of collectors. The new J.N. Shapiro-designed G.F.J. is absolutely gorgeous. The partners leverage the LA independent’s dedication to creating its dials by hand through a technique known as engine turning. I got to visit Shapiro’s workshop a few years ago and was charmed by the contrast of the brand’s cavernous industrial warehouse and the row of almost medieval-looking engine-turning machines lined up at the front of the room.

The dials of the new collaborative watches were painstakingly made on those very machines—on which a single mistake means tossing the whole thing in the bin—and then shipped off to Switzerland, where Zenith watchmakers installed them into cases. There are three different patterns on the dial, including a basketweave at the center and Shapiro’s signature invention, the Infinity Weave, inside the subdial. Much like on the Naoya Hida version, the dial is all Shapiro. Marietta notes, however, that it already resembles the brick pattern seen on the original G.F.J., which is based on the exterior of Zenith’s manufacture in Le Locle.

Zenith plans to release two collaborative versions of the G.F.J. annually moving forward. According to Marietta, a couple of years down the road, the brand may allow their indie partners to go beyond the dial and play with the case and movement as well.

With the G.F.J., Zenith has created something that is tailor-made for today’s collectors. Seven-figure pieces are currently booming at auction, and it would take Moses himself to part the money flooding into the market right now. These deep-pocketed collectors are most interested in watches from independent makers: Auction houses have sold more million-dollar F.P. Journe watches this year than they have from either Rolex or Patek Philippe. As collectors search for the next hot watchmaker, they’re finding exactly what they’re looking for in a 161-year-old Swiss luxury brand.

#6771. Silver Eye Makeup: “Stardust” Makeup Is Here to Bring Whimsy to Your Holiday Glam

No matter your feelings on how festive is too festive during the holiday season (for the record, we’re pro decorating your home like a European Christmas market from November to February), there’s one universal rule when it comes to beauty looks during this time that we can all agree on: The holidays were meant for glitter. And this year, we’re leaning into a more whimsical take on looking all shiny and bright. Say hello to “stardust” makeup.

This is more than just putting on a glitter eyeshadow or some tinsel in your hair. “Stardust” evokes a warm, nostalgic feeling and sprinkles some magic along the way. “Adding a pop of shimmer is such an act of self-celebration, and it feels really good,” says Donni Davy, celebrity makeup artist and founder of Half Magic. “It’s like decorating your face the same way you buy something beautiful for your home. It’s a feel-good thing that also sparks conversation and joy in other people and inspires other people to do the same.”

“[It]’s something magical that gives us the same feeling we had as kids opening presents,” adds celebrity makeup artist Alexandra French. “I’m always letting my inner child play when creating these looks. I’ll never not be whimsical."

The most obvious way to add some sparkle is with the eyes. But for that “stardust” effect, there’s a delicate art to it. “Stardust eyes are all about creating that dreamy, ethereal twinkling effect,” says Davy. “It's not about packing on a ton of glitter; it’s more about these delicate flashes of sparkle that catch in the light in a really magical way.”

The key to the “stardust” effect is in the application. Compared to traditional glitter or gems, Davy says that stardust eyes are more subtle. “It’s almost like a dusting that catches the light in a totally unexpected and very flattering way.”

For Davy, she loves starting with a soft wash of color (pale pink, lavender, or champagne are her personal picks) on the lids, like Half Magic’s Duo-Chrome Eyeshadow in Blushing Lizard. You can also try Saie’s viral Supersuede Baked Eyeshadow in Bellini or K-beauty’s Hince Chill Wave for the same shimmer effect. Apply shadow with your fingers, she instructs, focusing on the center of the lid and then blending out. “You want the sparkle to look almost random, rather than perfectly placed,” she says. “If you want a bit of a grungier look, you can use a brush to apply some underneath your eye as well.”

For a more ethereal look, build up the shine. She recommends adding some color to the inner and outer waterline corners for added dimension and again all over your eyelids. “You’re going to feel like yourself, but just sparkly,” Davy says.

But for those who want to go full glam, pack it all on. Think Alix Earle’s Halloween costume as a “gynoid.” Inspired by Japanese illustrator and artist Hajime Sorayama’s “sexy robot” illustrations, French leaned all the way in with silver.

“I always said for silver eye makeup to look truly metallic you need to layer different levels of textures and finish it off with a high-shine sparkle,” French says. “I wanted the eyes to feel like this mask that sparkles and looks alive the way his paintings feel so alive. I wanted people to stop in their tracks when they saw it from across the room.”

Here, she layered the Urban Decay Chrome Melt Eye Shadow in Temper Tantrum on top of the MAC Dazzleshadow Liquid Eyeshadow for that bold metallic eye. Then she topped it off with Question Beauty Silver Mirror Glitter (her pro tip: Use eyelash glue for application with this) to elevate the metallic look and get something with a more reflective sheen.

But no matter how intense you go with your eye look, both Davy and French say you can never go wrong with sparkle. “It’s a simple way to add a little something to your everyday makeup,” says French.

Why stop at just the eyes? This holiday season, experts say to sprinkle some “stardust” all over. For the body, Davy likes shimmer on the collarbones, shoulders, and décolletage. “Especially if you’re wearing something that shows a little skin,” she says. Try Half Magic’s Glitterpuck, which she says is a water-based formula made for zero glitter fallout, or we love the Marc Jacobs Money Shot Highlighter and Eva NYC’s Lightweight Silver Hair & Body Glitter Shimmer Spray for an iridescent shine. French recommends using the One/Size On ’Til Dawn Mattifying Waterproof Setting Spray mixed with glitter. She also recommends using the Fenty Beauty Diamond Bomb All-Over Diamond Veil for some body sparkle and more. “It’s the best,” she says. “I use it in the hair as well.”

And everyone’s nails have been getting the stardust treatment as early as October, with Taylor Swift wearing a faint, gold “fairy dust”shimmer on her nails at the 2026 Academy Museum Gala. If shadow or body glitter doesn’t speak to you, stardust nails might be the perfect fit.

“I love depth and dimension, and that’s exactly what this is,” says celebrity nail artist Tom Bachik. “The way this differs from just applying a cat eye or a chrome powder is by actually adding them all. It’s all about layering to get that Stardust effect. You want to have a nice shimmer and iridescence, and chunky glitter, and even a few rhinestones thrown in for good measure.”

Inspired by Christmas, Bachik wanted to play off gold with a pink iridescent shimmer for Selena Gomez’s latest mani. It’s a subtle pop of color that gives that golden sparkle some added depth. He used the Apreś Nails Maisie Neutral Coffin Gelx Tips before painting on BS-101 and Cosmic Chrome in Etherea from his own collection. Then he used a 3-D gel for that raised gold detail for a nice added touch, and to capture all that holiday spirit. “People love a little sparkle and this is the time of year that actually gives you permission to wear it,” he says.

So go get your whimsy on this holiday season. It’ll make this time just a little bit more special.

#6770. New Congress will still be filled with losers

The Capitol Dome is illuminated before dawn on Oct. 13. (Bill Clark/CQ Roll Call) (Bill Clark CQ Roll Call)

OPINION — Once again, the next Congress is going to be filled with losers.

This is not a personal judgment about their character or personality, but a simple recognition that many members have lost races before getting elected. And the incoming class is no different.

I’ve written many versions of this column for more than a decade, and yet the lesson always seems to get lost, no pun intended. Parties routinely attack candidates who enter a race with losses on their record. And, of course, it happened this cycle as well.

“We beat him once. We’ll beat him again,” National Republican Senatorial Committee Chairman Tim Scott declared when Democratic former Sen. Sherrod Brown announced his challenge to appointed GOP Sen. Jon Husted in the summer of 2025.

“Ohioans gladly sent him packing last year, and they’ll not hesitate to toss him aside again,” said a Republican strategist.

Brown lost reelection to Republican Bernie Moreno in 2024 but came back to defeat Husted for the state’s other Senate seat. And Brown is far from the only candidate to avenge a loss with an electoral victory last week.

The fact of the matter is that losing a race doesn’t have to be the end of a political career. Losses can lay the foundation for future success by building name ID or a fundraising network. Or sometimes it takes a change in the political environment or change in circumstances, such as running for an open seat instead of challenging an incumbent.

In addition to Brown, there are more than a dozen losers coming into the next Congress:

In some instances, though, the losses are piling up.

Republican Rep. John James of Michigan now has three statewide losses on his record. He lost Senate contests in 2018 (taking 46 percent of the vote) and 2020 (48 percent) and came up short in his bid for governor this year (44 percent).

Republican former Sen. John E. Sununu of New Hampshire, who lost reelection in 2008 to Democrat Jeanne Shaheen, fell in his comeback attempt for the open seat last week.

But they could take heart from another repeat loser. Charlie Crist, a former Florida governor and congressman, has had a handful of high-profile losses over the years, including for Senate and two gubernatorial comeback bids. He was just elected mayor of St. Petersburg.

So even though I know it’s a futile request, let’s have a bipartisan agreement not to attack candidates for past losses, because losing doesn’t mean they can’t later pull off a win.

#6769. Steak Bites With Spinach Couscous

A traditional steak dinner can feel like too much for a weeknight: too expensive, too likely to trip the smoke alarm, too fussy to cook to a perfect medium-rare. But cut a steak into bite-size pieces and suddenly it transforms into a quick-cooking protein that’s no more demanding than chicken breast or tofu. Rub the steak with the spices and make the quick no-recipe yogurt sauce a few hours ahead if it’s helpful with time management. To account for the extra liquid thrown off by the spinach as it cooks, the water-to-couscous ratio in this recipe differs from that often found on the back of the bag (or box) so measure accordingly.

More quick dinners this way: Spicy Chicken Lettuce Wraps; Gochujang Chicken Stir-Fry; and Easy Fish Tacos With Spicy Sauce.

Place 1–1¼ lb. boneless New York strip steak (1–1½" thick), cut into 1" pieces, 1 Tbsp. dried oregano, 2 tsp. onion powder, 2 tsp. sugar, and 2 tsp. Diamond Crystal or 1¼ tsp. Morton kosher salt in a medium bowl; season generously with freshly ground pepper and toss to coat. Let sit at least 10 minutes or cover and chill up to 8 hours (no need to bring to room temperature before cooking if chilling).

Heat 2 Tbsp. extra-virgin olive oil in a large high-sided skillet or medium Dutch oven with a tight-fitting lid over medium. Add 1 bunch scallions, thinly sliced, ½ cup finely chopped dill, 3 garlic cloves, finely chopped, and a large pinch of kosher salt; cook, stirring occasionally, until scallions are softened, about 5 minutes. Add 10 oz. baby spinach in 2–3 handfuls, stirring and letting wilt slightly after each addition before adding more, 5–7 minutes total. Pour in 1 cup water and bring to a boil; season with more salt if needed. Add 1 cup Moroccan couscous and stir to break up any lumps. Remove from heat; quickly cover with lid and let sit 10 minutes.

Meanwhile, heat 1 Tbsp. extra-virgin olive oil in a large skillet, preferably cast iron, over high. Arrange steak in a single layer; cook, undisturbed, until deeply browned underneath, 6–8 minutes. Reduce heat to medium; continue to cook, stirring often, until meat is just cooked through, about 1 minute more. Remove from heat. Add remaining 3 garlic cloves, finely chopped, and a large pinch of kosher salt; toss to combine. (Residual heat from pan will cook out any raw garlic flavor.)

Uncover couscous and fluff with a fork to separate grains. Spoon into a large shallow bowl or onto a platter. Top with steak. Cut 1 lemon into quarters and squeeze juice from 1 quarter over steak. Scatter thinly sliced cucumber and some dill sprigs on top; drizzle with oil.

Combine 1 cup plain whole-milk yogurt and a generous pinch of kosher salt in a small bowl; squeeze juice from 2 lemon quarters into yogurt. Stir to combine. If sauce is thick, stir in water, a spoonful at a time, to loosen.

Serve steak and couscous with yogurt sauce and remaining lemon quarter and more lemon wedges for squeezing over.

→ Baca artikel asli

#6768. Cranberry Paloma

A paloma is a classic summer cocktail in my rotation, but I still want an excuse to enjoy it throughout the colder-weather months. Enter: the cranberry paloma. Just like a classic paloma, this version is made with a base of tequila and grapefruit. But it gets a boost of sweetness and tartness with the addition of cranberry juice cocktail. This sweetened cranberry-based beverage replaces the need for sugary sodas or simple syrup. Bonus: It adds a gorgeous red hue to your glass, perfect for the holiday season.

Reposado tequila:The type of tequila you use is ultimately up to you, but I recommend either reposado or blanco tequila. Reposado tequila is aged for up to a year, typically in oak barrels, and takes on a warm sweetness with notes of vanilla and caramel. It’s a great choice for cocktails (especially when grapefruit is involved) because the smoothness balances out the tart and bright citrus.

Blanco tequila:Blanco tequila, on the other hand, is for people who want to double down on the brightness. It’s barely aged, if aged at all, so it retains the sharp, vegetal flavor of the agave plant. This recipe works well with either option, so it all comes down to personal preference and what you already have on your bar cart.
